“Statistics and Data Foundations for AI provides a rigorous yet accessible introduction to the
statistical theory and data analysis essential for understanding and using modern AI. The book is
grounded in real-world examples from authoritative sources such as NASA and NOAA, and thereby
connects core statistical concepts directly to contemporary AI applications. Its distinctive emphasis
on data quality, ethics, bias, and the limitation of AI equips readers to engage critically and
responsibly with AI systems. Suitable for both STEM and non-STEM audiences, the text supports
hands-on learning through tools such as R, Excel, and Google Sheets, without assuming prior
programming experience.”
Sivaramakrishnan “Bala” Balachandar, Chairman and William F. Powers Professor of Mechanical
and Aerospace Engineering at the University of Florida
“This slender volume creatively fills the gap between Data Management, Statistics, and Artificial
Intelligence, proving once again that less can be more. It embraces what many educators avoid: The
use of Large Language Models (here: Google Gemini) as partners in education. Demonstrations and
exercises are built on real, large datasets, not on the toy datasets from yesteryear. While being
scientifically rigorous, it builds on the reader's intuition, to the point of making “Data Intuition” a
primary topic of discussion.”
James Geller, Professor and Chair, Department of Data Science, Ying Wu College of Computing,
New Jersey Institute of Technology (NJIT)
“This engaging textbook, richly laden with real world examples and case studies, gives an intuitive
yet thorough view of probability and statistics, and of how we can use them to understand the
stochasticity in our world, the uncertainty in our knowledge, and the workings of artificial
intelligence. This is a timely book that deserves a very wide audience.”
Sanjoy Dasgupta, Professor, Jacobs School of Engineering, University of California San Diego
“This amazing book is written both as a textbook for data science and AI learners and educators, as
well as a reference for more experienced users. Using a pedagogical approach targeting adult
learners, the authors have explained the various aspects of AI, statistical foundations of AI and
LLMs, and provided examples, practice exercises, and links to additional learning from current
sources. The book provides thoughtful questions for discussion, illustrates the strengths and
weaknesses of AI, and engages the reader with descriptions and links to applications from a wide
variety of fields. It is well-researched and written in accessible yet scholarly language. As a long-time
teacher of statistics and now engaged in teaching with AI, I found the book extremely valuable. I
would highly recommend this book for college instructors, individuals who are self-teaching AI use,
and researchers seeking to extend their knowledge of AI applications in their work. Congratulations
to Dasu and Murthy on this excellent book!”
Rene Parmar, Dean, School of Education, SUNY New Paltz
“At a time when artificial intelligence is often perceived as a “black box,” Statistics and Data
Foundations for AI authored by Tamraparni Dasu and Geetha Murthy offers the essential map and
compass needed to explore its inner mechanics. By positioning data as the fuel that drives every AI
model—from training and learning to inference—the authors establish a robust foundation in data
collection, storage, and management, addressing core concepts that are frequently underrepresented
in traditional curricula.
What distinguishes this text is its forward-thinking pedagogy. The Data Debate features encourage
students to engage deeply and thoughtfully with complex statistical concepts through structured,
nonjudgmental discourse. Complementing these are the GeminiBox sections, which exemplify active
learning in action—guiding students to craft precise AI prompts and, importantly, to evaluate model
outputs critically for accuracy and potential “hallucinations.” Together, these elements cultivate both
technical proficiency and data literacy essential for working with modern AI tools.
The book succeeds in bridging theory with real-world relevance. From analyzing multivariate factors
influencing hurricane trajectories to addressing ethical considerations and sampling bias, the material
remains grounded in authentic data and contemporary challenges. The integration of rich visuals, real
datasets, and well-designed summative projects ensures that students not only understand core
principles but can apply them to meaningful, data-driven inquiries.
Finally, the inclusion of a 125-year historical perspective on the co-evolution of AI and hardware is a
masterstroke. It provides invaluable context, reminding readers that today's AI revolution is the
product of a century of innovation in computation and data science. Statistics and Data Foundations
for AI is an indispensable resource for educators, students, and practitioners seeking to understand
not just where AI stands today, but the data and statistical foundations that will shape its future.”
Kiron Sharma, Professor of Computer Science, Interim Chair, Dept. Computer Science &
Mathematics, Olsen College of Engineering and Science, Farleigh Dickinson University
Statistics and Data Foundations for AI is an interdisciplinary approach to statistical concepts and
data foundations of AI with real-world illustrative examples from authoritative sources such as
NASA, NOAA and the United States Census Bureau. Co-authored by a data science research expert
and an experienced educator, the book serves as a prequel to an AI and machine learning course.
Given the interdependence of data and AI, understanding data and using it responsibly to create and
interact with AI tools requires a high level of statistical skill and data intuition. The book includes
topics such as data management, exploratory data analysis, sampling, probability theory, hypothesis
testing, multivariate analysis, data quality, ethics, data privacy, and responsible use of AI. Every key
statistical concept is presented in the context of how it is used by AI applications in areas such as
sports, fashion, climate science, environmental science, health, medicine, and space exploration. The
book makes AI relatable to everyday life so that it is no longer an abstraction. Instructor resources,
supplementary materials, further reading, and debate topics enable advanced study and deeper
thinking.
Statistics and Data Foundations for AI is intended for undergraduate and graduate students, and
practitioners interested in learning statistical foundations in relation to data and AI with application to
real-world problems. The content is accessible to learners from a wide variety of backgrounds
(STEM and non-STEM) without sacrificing rigor.

Preface
Statistics and Data Foundations for AI grew from the discussions that the
authors, long-time friends with shared interests, both educators and
scientists, had about the level of preparation of college students for an
education in Artificial Intelligence (AI) which will soon be a key
requirement for the workforce. AI is increasingly integrated into
productivity tools at the workplace. In daily life, GPS navigation, self-
driving cars and AI chatbots that can plan itineraries and provide counseling
are already a reality. Understanding the underpinnings of AI is therefore
essential to use it effectively to its full potential, and to handle its peril and
its promise.
For many, AI is an abstraction. However, a majority of the key concepts
of AI have their foundations in classical statistics including probability
theory and statistical inference. AI applications are trained on massive
amounts of data, and in turn generate data in the form of text, images,
analyses and predictive models. Therefore, a deep understanding and
facility with data is important as well.
The main purpose of the book is to act as a bridge between classical
statistics, data science and emerging AI technologies. Every key concept is
explained in the context of AI applications and real-world data examples
from authoritative sources such as NASA (National Aeronautics and Space
Administration) and NOAA (National Oceanic and Atmospheric
Administration). In addition, given the primacy of data for AI, the authors
take the unique approach of devoting an entire chapter to the technical and
quantitative aspects of data quality. While the pursuit of AI has become a
modern-day gold rush, it is also important for students to be aware of the
ethical concerns, biases and potential catastrophic errors that accompany
such a rapid development.
To make AI an immersive experience, the book features the use of AI as a
learning tool in the form of prompts to AI-agents (Google's Gemini, and
OpenAI's ChatGPT in one instance) to delve deeper into any topic for
further study and research. The authors draw on real-world AI applications
from many disciplines including sports, fashion, and the arts and make the
content accessible and relatable to students from different academic
backgrounds. The open-ended nature of many discussions and exercises
allows the book to be taught and read at multiple levels of depth and rigor.
In addition, the book provides a strong grounding for advanced courses in
AI and machine learning.
The book is suitable for undergraduate and graduate students, and
practitioners from both STEM and non-STEM backgrounds. It is
comprehensive and rigorous with a strong focus on foundational concepts.
With the emergence of AI, basic data literacy is no longer sufficient. The
authors believe that in order to thrive in an AI-powered world, it is essential
to develop critical 21st Century skills. This book provides those skills.
Tamraparni Dasu & Geetha Murthy

associated with datasets, some with the output of GeminiBox prompts, and
some point to resources such as articles for further study and research. We
have provided a QR code to facilitate access to the URL list to users of the
print version of the textbook. The URL list is organized in order of
appearance in the textbook, annotated with the chapter and section
numbers. The URLs are denoted by a blue (cid:0) symbol.

Authors
Dr. Tamraparni Dasu (Ph.D. Mathematical Statistics, University of
Rochester, 1991) is a research scientist and Data Science expert specializing
in computational statistics, machine learning and data quality. She retired as
Lead Inventive Scientist after 31 years at AT & T Bell Laboratories and now
teaches Data Mining, Machine Learning and AI as an adjunct professor at
Fairleigh Dickinson University, New Jersey. Dr. Dasu has published
extensively in top-tier journals and research conferences such as SIGMOD,
KDD and VLDB and authored the field's first technical book on data
quality, Exploratory Data Mining and Data Cleaning, John Wiley (2003),
with Dr. Theodore Johnson. As an educator, Dr. Dasu is committed to
mentoring the next generation of quantitative thinkers, computer scientists
and data scientists.
Dr. Geetha Murthy (Ed.D. Instructional Leadership, St. John's University,
Queens, New York, 2015) is a highly experienced educator and
administrator whose research focused on describing and dismantling self-
limiting beliefs in students that inhibit them from pursuing STEM
education/careers. Most recently, Dr. Murthy served as the K-12 director of
mathematics for Herricks School District in Long Island, NY, a high-
performing public school district. Under her leadership, the district made
significant progress in increasing student achievement through initiatives
that focused on systemic changes to create and sustain greater equity, access
and success for all students. Dr. Murthy is passionate about promoting 21st
Century Skills in teaching and learning.
The authors believe that statistics and data foundations are essential in an
increasingly AI-native world and should be accessible to students and
practitioners from varied backgrounds. The authors have a unique
combination of complementary skills, a deep understanding of the core set
of fundamental concepts and practice of statistics and data science, and
extensive experience and insights into foundational education, that makes
this a canonical book for the study of statistics and data in relation to AI.
OceanofPDF.com
1
Statistics, Data and AI
AI has become a ubiquitous part of every aspect of our lives. We use AI
when we dictate text messages to our smartphone, when we ask our
navigation system for directions, and when we use AI tools like Gemini,
ChatGPT or Co-pilot to plan travel, write summaries and draft emails.
We rely increasingly on AI assistants such as chatbots to find
information, to reach customer service, to get counseling and even
health advice.
Data is used to train, manage, monitor and deploy AI, and AI in turn
generates data in the form of content as well as logs. Learning how to
manage and understand data, to use it responsibly, to develop an
intuition for its usability and limitations, are essential for living in an
AI world. These data skills together with key statistical concepts
constitute the first step to learning AI. The objective of this book is to
deconstruct complex, foundational concepts of statistics and data
without sacrificing rigor, and to place them in the context of real-world
AI applications in order to make them widely accessible.
FIGURE 1.1: Left: Australian marine scientists use drones to collect data
and monitor coral reefs. (Image: Australian Institute of Marine Science.)
Right: Sports analytics use AI and real-time data collected through sensors
to scout, coach, match up and improve the performance of athletes. (Image:
Freepik.)
1.1 Why Statistics and Data Foundations?
Many aspects of our life depend on AI, and going forward AI will be an
inextricable part of our daily life, like cars or electricity. In addition to our
personal use, industries and institutions too rely on AI. Social media
companies use AI to tailor recommendations about movies, food, clothes
and restaurants based on our browsing history. AI is used by scientists to
launch rockets, design self-driving cars, discover new drugs, diagnose
tumors and to monitor hurricanes. Sports, fashion, marketing and
agriculture industries use AI-powered tools extensively to manage their
businesses, inventory, and supply chains. Even museums and libraries use
AI to organize and preserve artworks, artifacts and books.
AI is powered by data. Self-driving cars are trained on thousands of
images of other vehicles, pedestrians, traffic rules, speed limits and weather
conditions. Sports clubs use data extensively to research strategies, evaluate
athletes and engage viewers, e.g., through sports betting. AI applications
use image recognition to transcribe and understand documents from
hundreds of years ago. Such documents are hard to decipher because of
deterioration in quality, obscure text and notation, and damage to
documents, as explained in the article Using AI to decipher historical
manuscripts, in Section 1.6.
AI itself generates data. Generative AI can write novels, make videos and
paint pictures. It can have philosophical conversations, write code and
answer any question posed to it. In addition, every action performed by AI
leaves a digital trace in the form of system logs that can be used to analyze,
audit and improve AI.
The need for strong statistical foundations and data intuition in order to
understand emerging AI technologies has been emphasized by experts and
leading organizations, e.g., the American Statistical Association (ASA),
referenced in Section 1.6. This book aims to fulfill that need. Figure 1.2
summarizes the main goals of the book and special features that enable
achieving that objective.
Bridge between Statistics and AI applications: The book connects
classical statistical concepts to AI by demonstrating through real-world
examples how such concepts are used by AI applications. By drawing
from a wide spectrum of applications in sports, fashion, climate science,
environmental science, health, medicine, and space exploration, to name
a few, AI is made relatable to everyday life and is no longer an
abstraction. The examples used in this book cite authoritative sources
such as (National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NASA),
National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA), Centers for
Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), Audubon Society, and the Pew
Research Center. The content is accessible to learners from a wide
variety of backgrounds (STEM and non-STEM) without sacrificing
rigor.
Precursor to AI and Machine Learning courses: The book provides a
comprehensive discussion of fundamental concepts of statistics and data
science such as data management, exploratory data analysis, sampling,
probability theory, hypothesis testing, and multivariate analysis. The
book can be used at different levels, including as a rigorous statistical
course that serves as a prequel to AI and machine learning courses.
Foundation for strong data intuition with emphasis on data quality:
According to AI experts, data is the new frontier of AI. Algorithms and
computing infrastructure that support AI, both hardware and software,
have advanced at a rapid rate, but the data on which AI is trained has
lagged behind. A strong focus on the quality of data, as well as an
intuition for data in the context of a specific problem, is critical to using
AI effectively. In this book, we devote an entire chapter to data quality
while emphasizing its importance throughout the text.
Guide to ethical and responsible use of data and AI: AI has been known
to provide incorrect or imaginary responses called hallucinations, as well
as perpetuate and amplify errors and biases embedded in its training
data. AI has sped up the creation and transmission of misinformation,
fake videos and polarizing content with serious real-life consequences.
This book emphasizes the need for careful evaluation of AI output and
content, and screening it for intentional and unintentional bias through
AI audits. Preserving the privacy of individuals by securing their data is
another important aspect of the ethical and responsible use of data and
AI.
FIGURE 1.2: The purpose of the book Statistics and Data Foundations for
AI, and special features that enable fulfilling the goals. ⏎
1.2 Special Features of the Book
We have organized the content of the book in such a manner as to reiterate
concepts already learned, and to connect them to real-world applications,
particularly related to AI. The book features special pedagogical elements
that enable delving deeper into the material in an interactive way, to build
upon and extend understanding of concepts, and to demonstrate how they
are connected to material discussed in earlier chapters. These features are:
1. Data Debate: Statistical and data concepts often require deeper thinking
to fully grasp their meanings and applications. Data debates are an
opportunity for students to actively engage in classroom discussion and
share their thinking and potentially conflicting points of view in a non-
judgmental environment, facilitated by the instructor. There is no one
correct answer or solution. Data Debate 1.3.1 is an example.
2. GeminiBox: The motto “Learning by Doing” inspired our GeminiBox
which teaches how to write prompts for AI tools. Gemini is an AI
assistant developed by Google. A GeminiBox demonstrates how to
gather information related to specific questions by using an appropriate
prompt. We present a prompt as well as a link to its output, typically to
foster curiosity and extend a concept beyond what is taught and
discussed in the classroom. Using the prompts in the GeminiBoxes and
examining the output is mandatory.
GeminiBoxes are an opportunity to teach students to think analytically,
to question and validate AI and its output, e.g., to identify
hallucinations, AI's tendency to fabricate factually incorrect answers.
For example, in order to learn more about data literacy and historical
manuscripts, the following GeminiBox can be used. The link points to
the output of the prompt. Note that the same prompt might return
different outputs each time. Students must review the output for
accuracy. We encourage students to use the GeminiBox prompts as they
progress through the book in order to get the most up-to-date output
because the information available on the internet is constantly refreshed.
GeminiBox 1.2.1: Data literacy and deciphering archives
“How is data literacy used in deciphering historic documents?” (cid:0)
3. ReferenceBox: ReferenceBoxes contains quick-reference material such
as terminology, definitions, and scientific and statistical vocabulary that
is essential to master the concepts in a chapter. Students should become
familiar with, and use, these terms as they answer questions or
participate in discussions in class. ReferenceBoxes are positioned close
to related content, and they make it easy to refer to important technical
terms.
4. Read & Reflect: AI is a fast-moving field, and there is a vast amount of
information and knowledge available to educate the public. It is difficult
to navigate this ocean of material of varying quality and provenance,
written at different levels of technical detail, and intended for a wide
range of readers from a lay audience to experts. We have vetted and
curated high quality articles such as research papers and videos related
to concepts presented in each chapter, and we listed them in a special
Read & Reflect section at the end of each chapter. Instructors may use
these for assignments.
5. Data sets: To reiterate the “Learning by Doing” theme, we use real-
world data in most examples and exercises, curated from authoritative
sources such as NOAA and from publicly available data resources such
as Kaggle. Using real-life data serves two purposes: first, to demonstrate
how data and statistical principles and concepts are related to concrete
real-life situations; and second, to practice such concepts and skills on
real data, to explore its features and flaws, to choose appropriate
analytics, and to interpret the results.
6. Exercises: At the end of each chapter, we provide a set of exercises
created to deepen conceptual understanding and practice data and
statistical analysis skills. The focus is to interpret results and gain
insights on how fundamental statistical concepts are applied in different
fields such as science, business, medicine, and education. Exercises are
also an opportunity to learn data skills, and to interpret and
communicate results in the context of a problem discussed in the
exercise.
7. Summative projects: Projects are summative and bring together
numerous concepts discussed in the chapter, and frequently concepts
from prior chapters. Projects have multiple parts, and students are
required to present a comprehensive response to each part. By working
through the summative projects, students will gain further understanding
of how different aspects of data and statistical analysis fit together, and
build data intuition. Summative projects may be used for student
evaluation by the instructor.
1.3 Statistics, Data and AI
AI originated many decades ago, as far as back in 1950. However, the
availability of vast amounts of data and the explosion in computing power
led to the widespread development and deployment of AI that we see now.
However, the foundational concepts of AI are drawn from classical
statistics, which came to be known as Data Science in recent years when
combined with basic data processing and programming skills. A simple
introduction to basic statistical concepts, especially for non-STEM majors,
can be found in Statistics, by Freedman, Pisani and Purves [17].
Figure 1.3 shows the connection between classical statistics, data and AI.
AI builds on fundamental concepts from statistics and uses data for training,
while statistical models gather data from AI through feedback loops to
improve performance and advance foundational concepts. Since examples
are the best way to establish this connection, we start the book with an
introduction to real-world examples of topics discussed in each chapter and
illustrate their connection to AI. We will revisit this figure in Chapter 9 and
discuss how the statistical concepts discussed in the book are used in AI.
Extended Description
FIGURE 1.3: Statistics, data and AI are interconnected. AI builds on
fundamental concepts from statistics and uses data for training, while
statistical models gather data in the form of feedback loops from AI to
improve performance and advance foundational concepts. ⏎
The following sub-sections present a brief overview of the topics in each
chapter of the book along with an illustrative example.
1.3.1 About Data, Chapter 2: Monitoring weather data with
AI
Data is the fuel AI runs on: it is trained on data, learns from data, and
generates data. Data collection, storage, retrieval, and management are
fundamental steps in data science and AI, as shown in Figure 1.3. AI is used
extensively to collect and manage data for other AI applications to use, such
as continuous data collection through sensors on spacecraft. Chapter 2
discusses different types of data, their storage and retrieval, and how data is
used by AI.
As an example, NOAA deploys satellites as a part of the Joint Polar
Satellite System (JPSS), Figure 1.4, left, to orbit the earth multiple times a
day collecting data for AI applications that monitor weather and climate.
The project is described in Fact Sheet about the Joint Polar Satellite System
to Monitor Weather and Climate in the Section 1.6. Such satellites, in
addition to collecting external data, also monitor their own movements,
internal environment, and functionality of key systems through telemetry
data from various sensors and performance metrics. Data collection and
management on such a large scale requires AI-powered tools.
FIGURE 1.4: Left: NOAA's satellite system, JPSS, circles the earth several
times a day collecting data. AI applications analyze and manage the data, as
well as use it for predictive purposes such as hurricane warnings. Right: AI
enables real-time exploratory data analysis (EDA) of vast amount of sports
data changing the way sports are played and athletes are measured, as well
as driving massive user engagement in the form of sports betting and
fantasy sports. ⏎
1.3.2 Exploratory Data Analysis, Chapter 3: How soccer clubs
use AI
Exploring data helps in understanding the objects about which the data are
collected, as well as the strengths and weaknesses of the data, e.g., satellite
trajectories. Is the data relevant? Is it clean? Is it complete? Is it timely?
How should it be prepared so that AI applications can ingest it and process
it seamlessly? Such a fundamental analysis of data outside the context of a
particular task such as prediction, is called exploratory data analysis
(EDA).
AI has powered the use of real-time EDA in many fields, from marketing
to consumer recommendations. Fantasy sports and sports betting, have
become juggernauts of user engagement through real-time EDA of sports
data, even as games are in progress. EDA is also used to gain a deep
understanding of the sport, Figure 1.4, right, analyzing opponent strategies,
player capabilities, coaching tactics, and user engagement. A Forbes article,
Unleashing The Power Of AI To Create A New Era In Soccer Excellence,
listed in Section 1.6 offers further reading on this fascinating topic.
Data Debate 1.3.1 ⏎ How would you use EDA to help your college
basketball team?
1.3.3 Sampling, Chapter 4: AI speeds up financial trading
Sampling is the process of using a subset to make inferences about a larger
population, e.g., learning about migration patterns of whales by tagging or
observing a select group of them. Chapter 4 discusses different types of
sampling and the scenarios in which they are used. Sampling is used
extensively to test soil, air and water for contaminants, and to study the
spread of disease.
Sampling plays a significant role in AI. For example, samples of human
speech are used to train and generate synthetic voices for AI chatbots. In
general, AI algorithms are more efficient when trained on properly chosen
data samples. It is often not possible to use all the available data while
making real-time decisions, such as predicting the behavior of complex
financial instruments Figure 1.5, left, or guiding a self-driving car. The
massive amounts of data available in these situations might take too long to
process and analyze, resulting in latency in reaction time which could lead
to huge financial losses, or worse, damage to lives and property.
FIGURE 1.5: Left: Sampling is used by AI tools to make real-time
decisions in the finance industry (Source: Freepik). Right: Sports betting
uses AI to compute hundreds of thousands of real-time probabilities. Photo
credit: Baishampayan Ghose. ⏎
1.3.4 Probability, Chapter 5: AI and sports-betting
Probability theory is used to evaluate the chances of a particular event
happening. A coin toss is a simple example, where each of the two
outcomes, heads or tails, has an equal chance of occurring. Events can also
be highly complex, like the probability of a particular film breaking box
office records. Numerous factors, such as the cast of the movie, the director,
cinematography, word-of-mouth, marketing and ad campaigns, and possibly
even weather conditions, figure in the probability that a movie will be a
blockbuster.
Probability theory is critical to AI. For example, AI algorithms use
probabilistic reasoning to guide movements of robots and autonomous
vehicles, e.g., by detecting moving objects and their most probable
trajectories in order to avoid collisions. AI is also used in sports betting,
Figure 1.5, right, to provide continuously updated odds for different types
of events: number of runs scored by a baseball team, the order of finish at
the Kentucky Derby, and the likelihood of Michigan Wolverines winning
the National Championship in college football.
1.3.5 Hypothesis testing, Chapter 6: How AI tests user
preferences
Suppose we want to find the answer to a question, e.g., “does a drug help
relieve the symptoms of a disease”, in an objective, data-driven manner
rather than based on opinions or convenience. Hypothesis testing provides a
rigorous statistical framework for using data from an appropriately chosen
sample of individuals to make inferences about the drug's effects on the
population in general.
AI has accelerated the process of hypothesis testing. It can generate
hundreds of hypotheses, collect enormous amounts of data, and through
repetition, output reliable results. Social media companies do this
continuously to test user engagement and interest. Should the “like” icon be
dark blue or light blue? Should it be a thumbs-up icon or a heart? They use
hypothesis testing to make constant changes to their websites and the
products and services they recommend, based on your preferences and
those of others like you.
Data Debate 1.3.2 Give an example of a hypothesis about campus security
that you would like to test.
FIGURE 1.6: Left: A scientist in the process of testing a sample in a lab.
(Source: U.S. Navy. Naval Medical Research Center.) Right: Satellites use
AI based on geo-spatial multivariate data to detect and contain wildfires.
Photo credit: Noah Berger.
1.3.6 Variable relationships, Chapter 7: AI detects wildfires
early
Most real-world phenomena are complex, with multiple factors interacting
with each other to influence their outcome. For example, to predict a
hurricane's path, it is important to collect data about multiple variables such
as wind speed, wind direction, temperature shifts, atmospheric pressure,
humidity, topological features like mountains, and many others. Collecting
all these measurements increases the amount of data considerably, and
makes the analyses more computationally challenging. However, in general,
the accuracy of predictions and forecasts improves when multiple variables
and their interactions are included in the study.
AI is ideal for studying multiple aspects since it excels at dealing with
vast amounts of data, and making complex calculations. Google research
and the Earth Fire Alliance use an AI tool, FireSat, for early detection of
wildfires. Current satellite tools cannot detect fires that are small because
they use low-resolution images that are updated only a few times a day.
FireSat can detect smaller fires sooner, by comparing current images with
previous images. In addition, FireSat factors in multiple variables about the
weather, environment and topology to detect and forecast the propagation of
fire. This provides firefighters and other first responders early warning
capabilities and an opportunity to take preventive measures to stop the
spread of fires, and save lives and property. FireSat is an example of how
AI uses geospatial analysis and multiple variables for forecasting wildfires.
The article FireSat Detects Wildfires Sooner and Alerts First Responders in
Section 1.6 provides further details.
1.3.7 Data quality, Chapter 8: AI-powered autonomous cars
and bad data
Incorrect data has serious consequences. What happens if a business ships
out products to an incorrect address? The customer will be annoyed because
they did not receive the product on time and will want their money back.
The person who received the item by mistake may decide to keep it as a
freebie. The company will lose revenues, customers, and time trying to
track down the incorrect address.
Data is AI's oxygen, without high-quality data AI will be ineffective and
possibly harmful. Figure 1.7 shows a vehicle's GPS tracks that seem to veer
off into the wilderness for a short stretch, due to bad quality GPS data,
discussed in the research paper Detection of outliers in crowdsourced gps
traces [23]. This anomaly is hopefully a harmless artifact of bad data, but
what if navigation systems use outdated maps or routes built on bad GPS
data? Motorists might end up in the middle of the woods or on newly
constructed cul-de-sacs. What if self-driving cars are trained on incomplete
data and only recognize people as obstacles, not animals or two-wheeled
vehicles? They might plow into dogs, bicycles and motorcycles.
FIGURE 1.7: Left: Examples of weird GPS traces caused by bad data.
From Detection of outliers in crowdsourced GPS traces. Right: A self-
driving car. What if it were trained on outdated maps? ⏎
While AI has made considerable progress in analytical algorithms and
computational power, it lags in the use of high-quality data. Many AI
hallucinations and biases can be traced back to poor-quality data. Recently,
there has been a strong push to improve the quality of data used by AI
applications, as discussed in the article Why It's Time for Data-Centric AI,
in Section 1.6. Chapter 8 is unique to this book. It provides a rigorous and
quantitative introduction to data quality, including discussion of types of
issues, cleaning strategies, quantifying data quality, and the potential
consequences of excessive remediation that lead to statistical distortion of
the data.
1.3.8 What's that AI?, Chapter 9: Digital Public
Infrastructure (DPI)
While examples of the connection between statistical concepts and AI are
discussed throughout the book, Chapter 9 focuses specifically on AI, with a
brief overview of AI and how it is used, from symbolic AI to machine
learning to recommender systems and natural language processing.
AI is now an important part of daily life, as well as an essential tool used
by countries, corporations and institutions to increase efficiency.
Developing nations are leaning into AI to leapfrog over intermediate stages
of development. They are focused on investment in digital public
infrastructure (DPI) to help the public navigate life in a frictionless manner,
whether it is interacting with the government or running a street-side food
stall. For example, in India there are 22 official languages with distinct
scripts and more than 1600 dialects, which make communication and
conducting business across the country difficult. The Indian government has
used natural language processing, a form of AI, to provide a digital
platform, Bhashini, to translate between any two languages or dialects to
help its 1.4 billion residents transcend language barriers.
Figure 1.8, left, shows a large multilingual crowd at the Kumbh Mela in
Prayagraj, 2019, which attracted around 200 million people.
FIGURE 1.8: AI is being used to enable India's 1.4 billion people (who
speak 22 languages and 1600 dialects) to communicate with each other.
Photo credit: Michael T Balonek. Right: Bias in mobile games like
Pokémon GO. Pokemon Go, Virginia State Parks. ⏎
Data Debate 1.3.3 What is an important problem that you'd like AI to
solve?
1.3.9 Responsible use of data and AI, Chapter 10: AI for good
Bias and prejudice seep into technology and data, both intentionally and
unintentionally. There have been instances in which machine learning
algorithms and AI bots amplify the bias embedded in their training data, or
they are manipulated into such behavior by users. An example of
unconscious bias is the augmented reality game Pokémon GO where the
locales for the game elements were crowdsourced, shown in Figure 1.8,
right. The contributors were predominantly gamers who played Ingress,
mainly from affluent suburban neighborhoods. This resulted in the game
elements being inadvertently concentrated in similar neighborhoods,
making them inaccessible to minorities from urban areas.
Chapter 10 focuses on ethical challenges in the use of data and AI,
including the issues of data privacy, data security and the spread of
misinformation and deepfakes. The chapter concludes with an overview of
extant guidelines, policies and measures adopted by countries, international
organizations, and corporations to support ethical practice of AI, and the
role of individuals to ensure that the power of data and AI is used for the
benefit of humanity.
1.4 Data Tools and How-to
Statistical analyses often require applying formulas and performing lengthy
mathematical calculations. Stepping through the calculations manually can
reinforce the understanding of concepts and formulas. However, given the
size of many data sets, manual computations may not be feasible, and it
becomes necessary to use advanced calculators and software tools. Popular
ones include:
Graphing calculators, such as the TI-84 Plus, are useful in classroom
settings for relatively small data sets, and for select statistical functions.
Web-based calculators are available online, sometimes for free.
Sophisticated analyses with larger data sets require tools such as Google
Sheets, a spread-sheet application that has built-in data analysis
capabilities that are easy to use, and it features an extension called
XLMiner Analysis Toolpak that can be downloaded and used to perform
data analysis tasks such as sampling, hypothesis testing, linear
regression, and exploratory data analysis.
Although Microsoft Excel is only available with a subscription, many
organizations provide free access to this tool. Excel too has many built-
in functions and formulas for data analysis and data visualization. In
most instances, step-by-step instructions on how to perform a particular
analysis are available in the Resources section (cid:0) of the book.
Programming languages like Python and R are not required for this
course. However, the Resources section (cid:0) of this book includes sample
code, specifically for figures in the textbook created using R code. R can
be downloaded for free at R: The R Project for Statistical Computing
[33] (cid:0) . R for Data Science by Wickham et al. provides a good
introduction to programming in R [44]. Software tools such as SPSS and
Minitab can also be used for statistical analysis at the discretion of the
instructor.
Regardless of how the computations are done, students must interpret the
results in the context of the problem, vet them for inconsistencies, and
communicate them effectively to others.
1.5 What's Next?
AI is a significant part of our daily life. It is embedded in the GPS
navigation of cars, in voice assistants that find recipes, and in chatbots that
provide customer service and health counseling. While we use many
sophisticated technologies without knowing their inner working, AI is
different for the following reasons:
AI has the power to influence opinions and change behavior of
individuals, communities and countries in an unprecedented manner. The
speed with which it enables the propagation of information in an easily
consumable manner, such as social media reels, real and fake, has
already changed the course of history in many ways.
AI can accelerate creation of scientific innovation and technologies and
improve the quality of life of humanity on a global scale: provide state-
of-the-art education to children in remote and underserved areas, enable
business and commerce at grassroots level, discover drugs to treat
cancer, create artificial organs including eyes and heart, sustain the world
on natural energy, …, anything is possible with advances in AI.
AI has the potential to reason and act autonomously. Just like humans, it
can do great good, or potentially cause existential-threat-level harm.
For these reasons, it is the responsibility of the public to be familiar with
the applications of AI and the foundations on which it is built, namely
statistics and data. This book provides that education.

About Data
Data is a description of an object or process in the real world. It is
essential to have good intuition for data in that specific context in order
to provide an effective solution. The objective of this chapter is to
cultivate such data intuition. Modern AI applications handle a great
variety of data types such as simple tables of numbers, text, images,
audio, video, webpages with multiple nested links, complex graphs of
connected objects, and continuous streams of readings from sensors.
Each data type requires a different treatment and understanding. Data
intuition develops that skill.
We rely on data to make decisions and predictions. For example, while
standing in line for ice cream as shown in Figure 2.1, it is natural to count
the number of people ahead to predict the wait time, or to turn on
notifications and gather data about online deals to get the best possible price
on a pair of jeans.
FIGURE 2.1: Data: The number of people ahead of you in an ice cream line
to predict wait time. Photo credit: “Ice cream line” by Roman Königshofer.
⏎
In general, collecting data is essential for answering questions in an
objective manner. Statistics, data science and AI rely on data to perform
complex analytical tasks and predictions, e.g., compute distances between
galaxies, predict the affect of changes in atmospheric temperature on ocean
currents, and assess the probability of occurrence of natural disasters like
wildfires. However, data is not just a matrix of rows and columns to be fed
in to a software tool. Data is a description of an object or process in the real
world and exists in that context. It is essential to have good intuition for
data in the context of that specific problem in order to provide an effective
solution.
ReferenceBox 2.0.1: Data intuition
Data intuition is the ability to understand data in the context of a real-
world problem, assess it for relevance and quality, choose an
appropriate analytical strategy for solving the problem, and interpret the
results in that specific context to provide an effective solution.
Data consists of information, gathered through observation,
measurement, or by asking questions as in surveys. For example, the
number of people waiting in line for ice cream is an instance of data
collected through observation. In order to find the most useful and relevant
answers in the quickest, most economical way, it is important to understand
what data to collect, how to collect, how much to collect, and how to
organize it. Section 2.1 discusses different types of data.
For the purpose of this book, we focus on structured data that consists of
well-formatted numbers and characters organized in rows and columns.
This is a popular and convenient type of data that is easily accessible by
most types of analyses and software tools. However, there are a wide
variety of data which require specialized tools and databases. For example,
corpora of free-form text, images, emails, webpages, audio and video,
connected graphs such as friend networks, DNA sequences and many
others. Such data are called unstructured data. AI applications make
extensive use of unstructured data.
In addition, the objective of this book is to study data as it relates to
statistics and AI. Therefore, operational aspects of data management, such
as data warehousing, data integration, federation, and optimizing data
retrieval, fall outside the purview of this book. However, data quality and
the responsible use of data do fall within the purview of statistics and AI, to
ensure the accuracy and reliability of analytical results. These topics are
covered in detail in Chapters 8 and 10.
ReferenceBox 2.0.2: Data and AI
Data is a collection of information gathered by observation, through
questions, or by taking measurements either manually or through
automated means like sensors and computers.
Data literacy is the ability to understand, manage, analyze and
interpret data accurately and responsibly.
Data Science is a cross-disciplinary field that uses methods from
statistics, computer science, software engineering and programming
for data-driven problem-solving, and pervades many domains of
application like climatology, science, and finance.
AI is a technology that uses statistical concepts, data, algorithms, and
computation to mimic human intelligence. Examples include self-
driving cars, voice assistants, and anomaly detection AI that can
identify abnormal cells in medical imaging scans.
Data Debate 2.0.1 Is the use of data always helpful? Can data be harmful?
What should one do to make data a force for good and reduce its potential
for harm?
2.1 Types of Data
In statistics, the type of data determines the type of computation and
analysis. For example, the average of a numeric quantity like number of
people in an ice cream line is meaningful, but the average of descriptive
characteristics like ice cream flavors is not. Data types play an important
role in AI since they allow humans to specify important properties of data
that help computers to process it efficiently.
There are numerous ways of categorizing data depending on the field of
study. Introduction to Data Mining [40] has a comprehensive discussion of
data categorization. In this book, we describe two ways of determining data
types: based on usage (shown in Table 2.1), and based on how data is
obtained.
TABLE 2.1: Examples of different types of data according to how
they are used: as identifiers, for comparison and ranking, for
counting and for measuring on the real number scale. ⏎
Qualitative Data Quantitative Data
Nominal Ordinal Discrete Continuous
(Identifying) (Comparison) (Countable) (Every
value)
Names of Scale of 1-5 No. of people Price
students stars in line
Ice cream Pass-Fail No. of soccer Distance
flavors goals
Flight Early-OnTime- No. of TikTok Volume
numbers Delayed likes
2.1.1 Data types based on usage
The following data types are based on the way data is used.
ReferenceBox 2.1.1: Data types based on usage
Qualitative data is descriptive information, such as names, labels,
tags. It is further categorized as: (i) Nominal data used as an
identifier, e.g., names, zip codes, movie genres, and flight numbers.
(ii) Ordinal data used for comparisons, ordering and ranking data.
For example, “mild”, “medium”, ‘hot” and “on fire” are ordinal data
used to describe the spiciness of hot sauces in increasing order.
Quantitative data consists of numbers such as counts and
measurements that can be used in arithmetic operations, e.g.,
addition and division. It is further subdivided into two types: (i)
Discrete data that can only take certain values, and it is countable.
(ii) Continuous data that can take any value on the real number line,
and it is uncountable.
Qualitative data is used to describe the qualities of people, objects, and
ideas, and is fundamental to our ability to communicate and understand the
world around us. Ice cream flavors, colors of flowers, names of students,
satisfaction index (on a scale of 1-5, for example) are all qualitative data.
Qualitative data can be used to put things into categories, e.g., “toys”,
“cars”, or “pillows”. For this reason, qualitative data is also called
categorical data.
Qualitative data is of two types: Nominal data which is purely for
identification, even if it is a number, e.g, an athlete's jersey number. Ordinal
data has a little more structure, and allows ranking, ordering and
classification. For example, a 1-5 customer survey rating index implies that
4 stars are better than 2, but not by how much. The Dewey Decimal
Classification (DDC) system used by libraries is a way of classifying books
by subject (and other sub-categories) to make them easier to locate.
Quantitative data is numeric and often the result of measurements or
counting, e.g., price of coffee, weight of a person, and the minutes until the
end of class. Quantitative data is critical for important endeavors, e.g.,
launching rockets, for monitoring hurricanes, and for tracking the
popularity of politicians and entertainers.
Quantitative data is of two types: Discrete data is countable. Examples
are the number of runs scored in a game of baseball, number of students in
a class, and number of stars in the sky. Continuous data, on the other hand,
can take any value on the real number line including rational and irrational
numbers. Examples of continuous data are duration of wait time for ice
cream, an athlete's height and weight, proportion of students who play team
sports, speed of a tennis serve, and water temperature at the beach.
Google's Gemini AI can help delve deeper into different types of data and
how they are used in specific fields such as fashion and studying public
spaces. First we provide a brief note on how to use this AI-assistant.
A word about Google's Gemini AI
AI tools make it easy to gather, collate, organize, summarize and interpret
relevant data and information from millions of sources, a task that is
impossible to accomplish manually. Google Gemini prompts are used
throughout this textbook to enable deeper understanding and self-learning.
Therefore, using the prompts in the Gemini boxes featured in this textbook,
and studying the generated content, is mandatory. Note that any reliable AI
chatbot can be used in Gemini's place.
An additional advantage of using Gemini is that it automatically provides
the most current material, ensuring that students always have access to
state-of-the-art information. However, the onus of verifying the output and
using it responsibly lies with the users of Gemini and other AI tools.
Screening and checking AI output for errors and inconsistencies is essential
because these tools are still evolving, and AI has been known to make
errors.
ReferenceBox 2.1.2: How to use Gemini AI
Gemini can be accessed either through a web browser at the URL
gemini.google.com, or through an app. Gemini prompts can be typed in
many human languages, for example, English, Spanish, Chinese, Arabic
and Japanese, and several Indian languages such as Hindi, Telugu,
Tamil and Kannada.
Gemini, in addition to a response to the prompt, provides a list of the
sources from which it has collated the response, and additional sources.
This is an excellent feature that helps validate the output and provides
opportunities for further research.
Gemini can even help collect data. Figure 2.2 shows the results of
uploading an image and prompting Gemini to describe and identify the
object in the image. The image is shown in Figure 2.2, left, the prompt is
shown in GeminiBox 2.1.1, and the output is shown in Figure 2.2, right.
FIGURE 2.2: Left: Image uploaded to Gemini (Photo: T. Dasu). Right:
Gemini output in response to the prompt in GeminiBox 2.1.1. ⏎

possible to collect data?
2.1.2 Data types based on how obtained
An alternate way of defining data types is based on how the data was
obtained, whether first-hand through design and intent, and by whom. This
type of categorization helps in determining the usability and reliability of
the data, the type of preprocessing it might need and the accuracy of the
results.
ReferenceBox 2.1.3: Data types based on how obtained
Primary data is collected by the person using the data, for the
purpose of a specific task such as predicting ocean currents.
Secondary data is collected by an external source, preferably of
repute, like NOAA, NASA or CDC. These organizations collect data
for their own projects and research, but they make it available for
public use under various open data initiatives.
Found data is a third data type based on its origin, so named by Sir
D. R. Cox, a famous statistician, who used the phrase “found data”
for non-primary data that was not collected as a part of an
experiment or designed with a purpose in mind. It is just
happenstance observation data whose origins are sometimes obscure,
e.g., a video that is forwarded millions of times on social media,
sometimes with alterations like filters or edits by intermediate users.
Primary data is considered the most reliable type of data because it is
specifically designed and collected with the problem in mind, for example,
a survey of a set of students on campus to identify the most frequently
played sport on a campus. On the other hand, primary data might be too
specific to be used generally. For example, sports survey data collected
from a campus in Alaska might not be useful for predicting sports
preferences on a campus in rural India.
A good example of reliable secondary data is a result of government
mandates called Open Data initiatives that specify that data collected using
government funding should be made available to the public. The data is
available to the world-wide community for analysis and research, and
therefore it is used and vetted quite rigorously. Figure 2.3 shows two
sources of high-quality secondary data: Prediction of Worldwide Energy
Resources (POWER) provided by NASA (National Aeronautics and Space
Administration) (cid:0) and CDC (Centers for Disease Control) data from a
variety of sources, e.g. the National Center for Health Statistics, on topics
such as smoking, vaccines and other health trends (cid:0). Several US federal
government agencies maintain high-quality research grade data which they
make available to the public.
FIGURE 2.3: Open data initiatives increase access to public information in
an effort to make governments more transparent and accountable. They are
an example of secondary data for external users. Left: Website to access
data from NASA's Prediction of Worldwide Energy Resources (POWER)
project. Right: Data dashboard for a variety of CDC health topics. ⏎
Found data tends to be passively observed data and often falls under the
category of Big Data. Data that are automatically recorded by sensors and
software platforms, e.g., automobile sensors, clicks on social media, third-
party audio/videos, can be termed found data. Such data tends to have
quality issues, and is often not usable because there is not enough
information about the data. For example, if you are given a spreadsheet
with columns of data but no column headers, would you be able to use it?
There are other data types as well, such as metadata and AI-generated
content (AIGC). Metadata is “data about the data,” that make it easy to
understand the data. For example, the Palmer Penguin data set refers to 344
penguins, with the variables species, island, flipper length, etc. Without
metadata, most data becomes less reliable.
In addition, AI has given rise to new types of data, AIGC. Output
generated by Google Gemini in response to prompts is an example of
AIGC. At this time, AIGC is not well understood, yet it has significant
impact. For example, AI-generated videos, called “deepfakes”, featuring
celebrities, or videos of one community provoking another community are
common on social media. Such fake videos create chaos and unrest. In
addition, AI algorithms are prone to making up facts, called
“hallucinations.” For example, if a user requests the names of five spy
thriller movies, AI might find three real ones and make up the names of two
others. We will learn more about AI generated hallucinations and deepfakes
Chapter 9.
ReferenceBox 2.1.4: Other types of data
Metadata consists of descriptions of data. Column headers,
descriptions of the variables, information about how the data was
collected and for what purpose, are all examples of metadata.
AI-generated content (AIGC) is a special type of data generated by
AI applications in response to user-provided prompts. The output of
the Gemini AI prompts in this textbook is an example of AIGC.
2.2 Data Collection
In the past, data was collected manually through painstaking observation,
experimentation and measurement. Dr. Jane Goodall (cid:0) collected data on
chimpanzees and their social structure and behavioral habits through
observation over a period of 30 years in East Africa. Gregor Mendel (cid:0) filled
numerous notebooks with measurements, descriptions and comments about
pea plants and their evolution over generations, laying the foundation for
modern genetics. Such manual data collection methods tend to be limited
by human capacity for observation and recording, resulting in small data
sets. In some instances such as the occurrence of rare diseases, the amount
of data available is inherently limited.
Just like Mendel and Goodall, modern-day marine biologists and
research scientists continue to collect data through observation. Figure 2.4,
left, shows a researcher gathering laughing gull chicks for observation and
measurement, collecting qualitative data (e.g., species, presence of
diseases), and quantitative data (e.g., weight), before the chicks are returned
to the wild. However, unlike Mendel and Goodall, these scientists have a
mind-boggling array of data collection tools available to them, because
trackers and sensors can be attached to any device and connected to a
computer for collecting the data. Figure 2.5 shows examples of modern
scientific data-collection tools for observation and measurement. On the left
hand side of the figure, marine scientists collect image data, and on the
right, scientists attach a tag to a whale. The tag will send the GPS
coordinates of the whale to the scientists so they can study the effect of
human-caused noise on whale behavior.
FIGURE 2.4: Left: A researcher from the National Audubon Society
gathers laughing gull (Leucophaeus atricilla) chicks from Eastern Egg
Rock, an island in the U.S. state of Maine. The chicks will be tested for
disease, as well as measured and weighed before being returned to the wild.
Right: National Environmental Satellite Data and Information Service
(NESDIS) provides data to monitor air quality, e.g., at Joshua Tree National
Park. ⏎
FIGURE 2.5: Left: NOAA's marine scientists collect image data about
white sharks in Greater Farallones National Marine Sanctuary. Right: A
drone approaches a sei whale to attach an acoustic recording tag. This is the
first time a drone was used to tag free-swimming large whales in U.S.
waters. Photo credit: Laura Howes under NOAA Fisheries Permit No.
18786-06. ⏎
Watch the video in Example 2.2.1 about how NOAA scientists collect
data about white sharks, and participate in Data Debates 2.2.1 and 2.2.2.
Example 2.2.1. ⏎ White Shark Research (NOAA Greater Farallones
National Marine Sanctuary): NOAA scientists collect data about white
sharks to study their coloration, feeding strategies and social behavior. (cid:0)
Data Debate 2.2.1 ⏎ What are the means by which researchers collect
data in this video? What kind of data do they collect? How will their
research help white sharks? (cid:0)
Data Debate 2.2.2 ⏎ What should marine scientists be concerned about
while observing animals in their natural environment? Are there any ethical
concerns as well as impact on the environment during the course of
observational studies for scientific research?
ReferenceBox 2.2.1: Methods of data collection
Manual observations and measurements are a common method of
data collection, particularly in specialized scientific experiments and
studies. These data can be quantitative, or qualitative, and tend to be
limited in size due to human capacity.
Automated data collection is done by software and AI applications,
e.g., through sensors, cameras and network probes, by monitoring
online activity on social platforms, or by extracting it from online
content like news websites.
Time-related measurements are special types of data that are
measured repeatedly over a period of time. Time series and data
streams are examples of such time-dependent measurements.
Over the decades, data collection has evolved with highly efficient
technology that can collect data automatically, economically and without
endangering humans. Data can be collected by satellites to capture images
and radio signals, by automatic sensors such as rain gauges, ocean buoys,
temperature and pressure sensors, and by air-quality monitors. Such
automated data-collection methods have led to amazing AI applications,
some of which are described below.
Figure 2.6, left, shows a NASA satellite image of Manhattan at night
used for studying the effect of light on climate and bird migrations.
Image data collected by satellites is used in many AI applications
including early detection of natural disasters.
Computerized machines like cars, rockets and robots generate petabytes
of data from millions of sensors that continuously collect data to monitor
and guide the functioning of these machines.
Drones collect images, audio and video of traffic for urban planning,
accident monitoring, and for assessing damage caused by tornadoes,
floods and wildfires.
Genetics, which has come a long way since Mendel, is another important
area where large amounts of data are used to study how diseases are
passed from generation to generation. Figure 2.6, right, shows a National
Cancer Institute's image of cancer cells and how they move and spread
through organs.
Social media platforms automatically capture data about online behavior
such as purchases, viewing behavior, likes and dislikes, friend networks,
and other preferences. They use such data to personalize online
experience and recommend shows, services and products that are most
likely to appeal to a user.
FIGURE 2.6: Left: NASA satellite captures Manhattan at night. Such
images are used to study the effect of light on migratory birds and climate.
Right: DNA and genomic data are used for gene sequencing and identifying
genes that trigger diseases like cancer. National Cancer Institute's image
shows how cancer cells move and spread. ⏎
Automated data collection by software probes, sensors, transaction logging
systems and other means results in millions of observations per second
leading to massive amounts of data, referred to as Big Data.
ReferenceBox 2.2.2: Big Data ⏎
Automated data collection has led to the creation of vast amounts of
data, often referred to as Big Data. It is characterized by:
Volume, massive quantities
Velocity, collected with great speed
Variety, from different sources, and of many types, e.g., text, images,
video, audio, unstructured data
In addition, it should have:
Validity, of relevance to the application and project
Veracity, of high quality
Specialized tools and software are required to manage Big Data. Data
streams that are collected and updated in real-time are examples of Big
Data, e.g., a live video feed of the Super Bowl that sends visuals as the
game unfolds in real life.
GeminiBox 2.2.1: Big Data ⏎
“Give me three examples of Big Data.” (cid:0)
Data Debate 2.2.3 Choose one example from the three provided by Gemini
AI in response to prompts in GeminiBox 2.2. Discuss with reference to the
five characteristics, or V's, of Big Data in ReferenceBox 2.2.2, and
determine if they meet the criteria.
2.2.1 Manual data collection methods
Manual data collection happens through observation and measurement, and
through posing questions with different objectives. We have discussed
examples of observational measurements such as those gathered by Jane
Goodall, Gregor Mendel, and researchers like the one in Figure 2.4, left.
Other methods of manual data gathering consist of asking questions and
seeking information. Common examples are surveys, focus groups and
interviews. These methods are popular in social sciences where people's
opinions, attitudes and other behavioral characteristics of interest are
measured.
For example, political polls are common around election season. They
are conducted by academic institutions, media companies and social
research organizations by sending out surveys or questionnaires to carefully
selected sets of people. Such surveys were frequently used during the
pandemic to gauge the health and well-being of the population, impact of
the lockdown on school-aged children, and attitudes toward vaccines. The
Pew Research Center, which describes itself as a “fact tank”, uses surveys
extensively for opinion polling and social science research. It provides
guidance on survey design in its article How to Design a Survey in Section
2.5. Designing Surveys: A Guide to Decisions and Procedures by Blair et
al. [5] provides a thorough introduction to survey design.
ReferenceBox 2.2.3: Methods of manual data collection
Questionnaires, surveys and forms: A questionnaire consists of a set
of questions created with a specific purpose to collect data from
individuals. Those who answer a questionnaire are called
respondents. Questions can be open-ended where the respondent can
answer in free text as they choose, or closed questions where they
will have to choose from a given set of options.
A survey is a method of collecting data using a questionnaire.
Forms are used to collect specific pieces of personal information.
Unlike surveys which are intended for learning about the whole
group and not any particular individual, forms are intended for
personal information in the context of a specific task related to the
individual, e.g., evaluation for school admission or eligibility for
participating in a sport.
Focus groups consist of hand-picked individuals whose opinions are
collected on a specific topic, product or service.
Interviews are one-on-one data collection methods used to gather
detailed, in-depth information, either in person or via a
teleconference.
The U.S. Census Bureau conducts a special survey every ten years, called
a census, to collect demographic data about the population of the United
States by state and county. A census requires that every single individual in
a population be included in the survey. The original purpose of the U.S.
Census (according to Article 1, Section 2 of the U.S. Constitution) was to
apportion representatives and to direct taxes among the states. Census data
is now commonly used for research, marketing, and planning. For example,
population and housing data might determine where schools are constructed
or where new bus routes are placed. The articles U.S. Census Bureau at a
Glance and What Is a Census and What Kind of Data Is Collected? in
Section 2.5 provide additional information.
Surveys, like the one in Figure 2.7, right, often use a Likert scale that
uses pre-defined categories like “Mostly positive”, “Neither positive nor
negative”, and “Mostly negative” to gauge the sentiment of participants.
Limiting responses to a handful of options makes it easier to process and
analyze the data from the survey respondents. For a hands-on experience of
surveys, explore the prompt in GeminiBox 2.2.1.
FIGURE 2.7: Left: Weather station in Liberty Park, Manhattan, gathers
weather data in real-time using sensors. (Photo credit: G. Murthy). Right:
The Likert scale used in this Pew Research Center's survey is based on
limiting the answers on a survey to a small number of ranked categories,
e.g., “Mostly positive”, “Neither positive nor negative”, and “Mostly
negative”. ⏎
Data Debate 2.2.4 What type of data is a Likert scale measurement?
GeminiBox 2.2.2: Survey design
“How do I design a survey to find the most popular starburst
flavor?”(cid:0)
Data Debate 2.2.5 What do you think of the output generated by Gemini in
response to the prompt in GeminiBox 2.2.1? Create and submit your own
Gemini prompts to learn more about survey design, e.g., beauty products.
Conduct the survey in your class.
Focus groups are used in marketing, pharmaceuticals, television and
politics as “trial balloons” for new ideas and products. For example, when a
new TV show is being produced by a network such as NBC, they select a
focus group of a specific age, e.g., 35-59, and compensate the participants
for sharing their TV viewing preferences. An ongoing list of focus groups
actively seeking participants at the website focus-groups.org in Section 2.5
features a list of focus groups actively seeking participants.
Interviews are perhaps one of the most labor-intensive data collection
methods, either involving face-to-face meetings between individuals or
sending a set of questions that require long-form answers. Ethical concerns
such as privacy, keeping the content confidential, e.g., psychiatric
interviews, and limiting access to the content become critical. Interviews
play a key role in medical research.

An online company trying to research the type of movies popular with

Which are the most likely to be biased and why? Is it good or bad to have
