2.2.2 Automated data collection methods
The explosive growth of AI has been fueled by automated methods of data
collection. They are the norm in many commercial and scientific endeavors,
and they result in a richer, more complete type of data.
Sensors, like the ones on the weather station in Figure 2.7, left, can be
attached to a variety of objects such as drones, balloons, ocean bouys, or
spacecrafts, and they can record measurements, audio and video. Any
method of deploying a sensor can be used for data collection. Here are
some examples.
Drones for collecting images: Drones have become important data-
collection tools across many fields of study and business. They can carry
different types of equipment including sensors, cameras and
communication devices. Drones can be used for studying changes in
oceans and snow caps, assessing impact of natural disasters like floods
and wildfires, migration patterns of animals, traffic flow on major
roadways, military movements, monitoring hard-to-reach equipment like
cell towers, and studying the health of herds and crops.
Satellites for monitoring air quality: NOAA's National Environmental
Satellite, Data, and Information Service (NESDIS) and the
Environmental Protection Agency collect hourly data via satellites to
provide continuous air quality data to the public at their portal AirNow (cid:0).
Figure 2.4, right, shows the information for Joshua Tree National Park in
California.
Balloons for gathering climate data: Weather instruments like radar
trucks, weather stations with sensors and gauges, ocean buoys, and even
balloons collect huge amounts of data continuously.
Feedback is another type of automatically collected data. It is an essential
part of training robots, building smart homes and predictive AI models.
Feedback actively changes the behavior of a system. For example, a smart
home's thermostat, like the one shown in Figure 2.8, left, keeps adjusting
the settings depending on time of day, whether the house is empty or
occupied, which floor the occupants are likely to be on, and other such
criteria. It can result in considerable energy savings. Another example of a
feedback loop, known as closed-loop monitoring, is used to monitor wind
turbines that harness wind power, shown in Figure 2.8, right. The angle of
the blades, speed of rotation and anomalies are continuously controlled
through a feedback loop to minimize wear and tear while maximizing
energy capture. Feedback loops are used by social media companies to
constantly tweak the look and feel of their apps and websites to increase
user engagement.
FIGURE 2.8: Feedback is a form of data collection where the output of a
system is used to monitor and influence the system's behavior. Left: A smart
thermostat adjusts settings depending on occupancy, external temperatures
and time of day to optimize energy use. (Photo: Raysonho). Right: The
angle of blades and speed of rotation of wind turbines are monitored using
feedback to minimize damage and maximize functionality. (Photo: Erik
Wilde) ⏎
ReferenceBox 2.2.4: Methods of automated data collection
Sensors attached to surfaces of drones, ocean buoys, spacecraft, for
collecting measurements about light, temperature, atmospheric
pressure.
Cameras and microphones for capturing images, audio and video
mounted on buildings or moving objects.
Feedback loop data to capture the output of a system continuously as
input to affect its future output to meet specified objectives, e.g.,
minimize energy consumption in a smart home.
GeminiBox 2.2.3: Feedback Loop
“Give me an example of a feedback loop.”(cid:0)
2.3 Storing, Managing and Accessing Data
Data storage has a long history. Humans have always wanted to
communicate and store data for future use. Consider the collage in Figure
2.9, starting at the top-left corner and examine the images in a clockwise
direction. One of the earliest examples of data stored for future generations
is in the form of cave paintings. Later, people developed language and
passed on data by memorizing it and teaching the next generation, often in
the form of stories, songs and lectures. Then came written scripts, where
information was chiseled on stone tablets or scratched out on papyrus or
written with ink on parchment. Manuscripts were written and copied
painstakingly for safety and distribution, which also led to beautiful
illustrations and art. The Book of Kells, displayed at Trinity College in
Dublin, Ireland, is an exceptional example. Subsequently, paper and
notebooks became common ways of storing data. The invention of the
printing press sped up the process of storing and distributing large amounts
of data, whether numbers, text or images.
FIGURE 2.9: Data storage methods over centuries, some milestones,
clockwise from top left: (1) Cave paintings. (2) Oral traditions of passing on
data through storytelling. (3) The development of written script to store data
on stone tablets, papyrus and other writable surfaces. (4) The printing press
sped up the process of storing large amounts data in newspapers and books.
(5) Computers and digitization resulted in Big Data, and cloud storage
resulted in the ability to access any type of data, any size, any shape, even
movies, from any place, anytime. ⏎
Now, thanks to digitization, the ability to represent any information as a
combination of 0's and 1's (called “bits”), unimaginable amounts and
varieties of data can be stored on computers and in the cloud, accessible
from any place, anytime. Without digitization, there would be no
smartphones or computers, no streaming movies, no addictive social media
apps; in fact, there would be no internet! Isn't that a remarkable journey,
from etchings on a cave wall, to any and all data, accessible all the time
from anywhere?
Management of data is highly formalized and studied systematically by
the field of database management systems. It is a vast topic that requires an
entire sequence of courses to understand the fundamentals. It is not the
focus of this book. However, Database Management Systems by
Ramakrishnan and Gehrke [34] provides an excellent introduction.
Example 2.3.1 Digitization of medical records: Digitization has
revolutionized the health care industry where medical professionals
increasingly use Electronic Medical Records (EMR) of patients at hospitals
or clinics. EMRs allow instantaneous sharing of information leading to
better diagnoses. It is believed that EMRs can improve the quality and
efficiency of health care, as well as make the healthcare process more
transparent to patients. To learn more, read the article Electronic Health
Records in Section 2.5.
Data Debate 2.3.1 Discuss examples of data storage. How do computers
and automation help? What are some challenges?
Data storage is a part of daily life. We take notes, store contacts and
photographs on our phone, create spreadsheets to track expenses, and
download interesting news articles. Some examples of common data
storage methods and tools include:
Manual handwritten records are rare now, but for centuries data was
recorded manually in notebooks and logbooks.
Spreadsheets are used to store tabular data, i.e., data organized in rows
and columns. Examples include Excel spreadsheets and Google Sheets.
Figure 2.10, left, is an example of a spreadsheet used to record the medal
tally at the 2022 Winter Olympics. Both qualitative and quantitative data
can be stored in spreadsheets. Some spreadsheets allow embedding
images and audio/video objects as well. Companies like Microsoft and
Google incorporate AI into their data toolsets. Figure 2.10, right, shows
an old-fashioned card catalog used to record, store and retrieve
information about books in libraries before the digital age.
Image libraries include iPhotos, GooglePhotos and Flickr where the
images are tagged with labels like “cat” or “flower” or “baby”, to make
it easy to search. Facebook, iPhotos, GooglePhotos automatically create
personalized memory albums from image libraries.
Multimedia libraries store music and movies, and they are used by
streaming companies such as Spotify and Netflix. Such databases can
store information about genre, artists, duration and popularity along with
the actual movie or music. They can also collect information about who
accessed any given title and for how long, and they make
recommendations to friend-networks based on an individual's viewing
patterns.
Knowledge bases typically store rules used by AI applications such as
Siri. The rules can be complex, involving logic and scenarios. For
example, self-driving cars function using training rules such as “if traffic
light is red, come to a total stop.” AI applications also learn strategy
games by ingesting vast amounts of data, e.g., Google's AlphaGo that
“trained” itself on millions of Go games and eventually beat the world's
best player, Lee Sedol. Watch the fascinating documentary Alpha Go
referenced in Section 2.5.
Databases or database management systems (DBMS) are special
software applications that permit storing and efficiently accessing large
volumes of different types of data. They come in different types and are
used by companies and scientists to quickly store, access and process
data for analysis. For example, Oracle, Hadoop and Teradata are
databases that are used for everything from marketing sneakers, to
observing the oceans and predicting tidal patterns. There are specialized
databases for spatial and temporal data, images, connection graphs like
friend networks, a mix of different types of data (unstructured), and
many other types. Database management is an entire course of study by
itself. Explore GeminiBox 2.3.1 for a brief overview.
Specialized database systems are the underpinnings of AI. Machine
Learning and AI applications use special databases that provide speed,
flexibility, analytics and natural language processing abilities to interact
with humans through human languages. Examples include Google's
Tensor Flow. Generative AI applications use vector databases that allow
both structured and unstructured data to handle alphanumeric data, text,
images, and audio and video.
FIGURE 2.10: Left: Spreadsheet of medal tally at the Winter Olympics in
Beijing, 2022, based on information provided by the International Olympic
Committee (IOC). Right: An old-fashioned database, a card catalog of
library books. Photo credit: “Original Database” by shindoverse. ⏎
GeminiBox 2.3.1: Overview of Database Management Systems
(DBMS) ⏎
“Give a brief overview of database management systems.” (cid:0)
In order to retrieve stored data efficiently and quickly, data are indexed.
For example, the index at the back of a book has a mapping between
keywords and page numbers where the content related to the keywords can
be found. The index, which is much more compact than the original data, is
searched to locate the appropriate page rather than the entire book.
Databases have an analogous way of indexing stored data to make locating
the relevant data quicker. Efficient indexing is the key to providing quick
search results.
GeminiBox 2.3.2: Indexing
“What is indexing? Give me an example. What is the Dewey Decimal
System (DDS)?” (cid:0)
Data from different applications, e.g., geospatial data versus videos, have
different structures, internal relationships and pose different types of
technological challenges in terms of storage and other processing. It might
not be possible to have a single, general purpose database management
system that can handle all different types efficiently, hence the need for
customized database applications to handle each type. Database
management systems use specialized software and languages to query the
database and retrieve relevant data of interest. SQL (Structured Query
Language) is a common database query language. Use the prompts in
GeminiBox 2.3 to learn more.
GeminiBox 2.3.3: Query Language
“How is data retrieved from a database? Give me examples.”(cid:0)
Database Management Systems by Ramakrishnan and Gehrke [34] is an
ideal introduction for those interested in studying this topic further.
2.4 Conclusion
The science of collecting, managing and analyzing data is continuously
evolving due to the dynamic nature of AI and its applications. Such
technology is also an effective means of cultivating data intuition which
helps unearth useful insights and creates “aha” moments. For example, the
turkey products company Butterball needed to modernize their data
infrastructure in order to integrate customer data with systems that housed
financial, operations and marketing data. During the process, they combed
through transcripts of decades worth of customer calls to Butterball
helplines, especially near Thanksgiving, and made an important discovery.
The biggest pain point for customers? Thawing the turkey. This data-driven
insight led their food scientists and researchers to create the “cook from
frozen” turkey that did not require thawing. Read Thank Big Data for
Killing Off the Worst Part of Thanksgiving Meal Prep in Section 2.5 for
details.
In the next chapter, we will focus on exploratory data analysis, another
important way of developing data intuition. Given that AI results are
famously opaque and not very human-friendly, developing a strong data
intuition helps us move closer to understanding and interpreting the results
of AI algorithms.

2.6 Exercises
1. Data relevance: Suppose that you want to find the best purchase price
for a pair of jeans.
(a) What data would you collect? What are the different ways you can
gather the data?
(b) Would AI be of help? If yes, how?
2. Qualitative data: In Figure 2.2, identify and write down as many
qualitative variables as you can from the description generated by
Gemini.
3. Metadata: Present to the class a spreadsheet with three pieces of
information that you have created without column headers or description
of the data. Ask them to guess what the data is about. How many can
guess correctly?
4. Data collection with drones: List three data collection activities that
drones can be used for.
5. AI for creating a survey: Use Google's Gemini AI Chatbot to come up
with questions for a survey about a topic of interest. Potential topics
include: healthy snacks, workouts, testing a new video game, pizza,
makeup, etc.
(a) What is your goal in designing this survey?
(b) Try different prompts to find the right questions to meet your goals.
(c) Are you satisfied with the questions that were generated by Gemini
for your survey?
(d) SurveyMonkey is a popular application that can create online
surveys using AI and collect and organize data. Try using this
application to create a survey about a topic that interests you. (cid:0)
(e) Compare the surveys created by Gemini and Survey Monkey.
(An example of a survey for determining the most popular Starburst
flavor using Gemini is available in the Resources section (cid:0).)
6. Census survey: Questionnaires can contain questions of different kinds:
open-ended questions (write a response), closed questions (yes/no),
Likert scale (strongly disagree, disagree, neutral, agree, strongly agree),
multiple-choice and check-boxes. Look at the census questionnaire in
this link: (cid:0).
(a) What are the different kinds of questions on this census survey?
(b) What type of data (qualitative, quantitative) is being collected in
this survey?
7. Focus group: You and two friends have created a new video game for
children aged 10-13. You would like to test this video game with a focus
group and get feedback to improve the game before it is released online.
(a) How would you form this focus group?
(b) What are examples of questions that would you pose to this focus
group?
8. Big Data: Big Data are measured, for example, in Megabytes,
Gigabytes, Terabytes. What comes next? What is the largest known data
quantity measurement?
9. Data streams: Write down three examples of data streams from
different settings. Think of social media, NASA telescopes, stock tickers
and so on.
10. Digitization of data:
(a) Write a short paragraph on how digitization, or the binary system,
is used to transmit data.
(b) What is the impact of digitization on the diversity, equity, access,
participation, inclusion, or fairness of music cultures? The article
“Music, Digitalization, and Democracy” discusses some of these
issues and can be found at this link (cid:0). Use Gemini to summarize this
article with an appropriate prompt. Do you think this is an accurate
summary of the article? Do you agree or disagree? Why or why
not?
11. Databases:
(a) Use Gemini to research different types of databases. Which one
would you use if you wanted to build a database to store ancestry
information which might include family relationships, property
ownership documents and photos?
(b) What kind of database would you use to store contact information
of all your family and friends?

3
Exploratory Data Analysis
Exploring data from different perspectives is essential to establish its
relevance to the problem, to unearth data quality issues, and to develop an
intuition for that data in its real-word context. This process is called
Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA), where data is analyzed outside the
framework of any specific task such as prediction. EDA is an essential
aspect of preparing data for AI. This chapter introduces the basics of EDA
such as descriptive statistics and visualization techniques. AI tools have
made it easy to perform EDA on a large scale, and AI applications rely
extensively on EDA to calibrate and make real-time decisions.
The United States Census is carried out every ten years and aims to count
every person living in the United States. That is, more than 300 million people,
resulting in 300 million observations. Studying each individual that lives in the
Unites States would not be possible or useful. However, simple summaries such
as counts and averages, and visualizations based on these summaries convey the
big picture about what is happening in the country, and identify general trends
and characteristics. For example, Figure 3.1 created by the United States Census
Bureau is packed with information based on statistics that describe residents of
the United States. According to the figure, the number of households with three
or more people dropped from 50% in 1973 to 36% in 2023. However, the
percentage of homeowners older than 75 doubled from 7% in 1973 to 15% in
2023. Such information is useful for planning housing, e.g., build smaller homes
for smaller households but with more amenities for the elderly.
Extended Description
FIGURE 3.1: Changes in American households between 1973 and 2023, EDA
based on visualization and descriptive statistics. Source: United States Census
Bureau. ⏎
The process of creating data summaries and visualizations to understand
general characteristics of data is called exploratory data analysis (EDA). John
Tukey, a famous statistician at AT & T Bell Laboratories, coined the term EDA
in 1970, and authored the book Exploratory Data Analysis [42]. He would
manually compute statistics and draw plots, sometimes with just a pencil and a
yellow notepad. That is no longer feasible, given the volume and complexity of
data. Specialized software like R and Python offer packages that perform large-
scale EDA and automate repetitive EDA tasks.
AI tools have completely changed the way data scientists approach EDA. For
instance, we gave the prompt in GeminiBox 3.0.1 to two AI bots, Google's
Gemini and OpenAI's ChatGPT, to plot a data distribution with more than one
peak, called a multi-modal distribution. It took the AI tools just a few seconds to
create the code and generate the plots. The output is shown in Figure 3.2.
Extended Description
FIGURE 3.2: Output generated by two AI tools in response to the prompt
“Draw a multi-modal distribution with thick plotting lines” in GeminiBox 3.0.1.
Left, OpenAI's ChatGPT. Right, Google's Gemini. ⏎
However, the onus of verifying and interpreting the results rests with the user.
One of the main objectives of this textbook is to build a strong foundation of
statistics and data intuition in order to use AI tools efficiently and responsibly.
The remainder of this chapter focuses on understanding and performing EDA
with simple tools. Section 3.10 at the end of the chapter provides a detailed
discussion of the connection between EDA and AI.
GeminiBox 3.0.1: EDA with AI tools ⏎
“Draw a multi-modal distribution with thick plotting lines.” Output is
shown in Figure 3.2.
Data typically consists of objects and attributes that either describe or
measure characteristics of the objects. Such information is often represented as
a table where the rows are objects, and the columns are attributes. For example,
in the US Census data, each individual living in America would be an object
and correspond to a row, and attributes such as age, gender, ethnicity,
homeownership status, etc. that describe characteristics of the person would
constitute columns.
ReferenceBox 3.0.1: Objects and Attributes
An object or entity or sample is a unit about which data is gathered.
A variable or attribute is an observation or measurement related to the
object that describes some characteristic of the object.
3.1 Data Sets
In order to illustrate EDA concepts through practice, we present two data sets,
one real and the other hypothetical. The Palmer penguin data was collected by
research scientists Katherine Gorman et al. [18] that studied the foraging habits
of penguins that inhabit the Palmer Archipelago in Antarctica, and whether that
behavior differed by gender. The data set was prepared and made available to
the R community by Allison Horst et al. [21]. The data is a mix of different
types of attributes, and has the richness and imperfections typical of real-world
data that are essential for developing data intuition. The second data set, of the
heights of middle school students, is hypothetical and is very small with just 11
observations and a single quantitative attribute. The purpose of such a simple
data set is to explore the data manually, without even a calculator, to understand
the concepts and logic behind EDA.
3.1.1 Real-world example: Palmer penguins data
Penguins are unique birds that live in the Southern Hemisphere, on the islands
of Antarctica. Scientists at the Palmer Station Ecological Research Program in
Antarctica collected data on penguins that inhabit the Palmer Archipelago to
study the impact of climate change on these birds, specifically changes to
gender-related foraging behavior.
Example 3.1.1. ⏎ The Palmer penguins data contains size measurements for
three penguin species, Chinstrap, Gentoo, Adelie, found on the Palmer
Archipelago, Antarctica, over a study period of three years from 2007 to 2009.
The data set contains 8 variables: species, island, bill length, bill depth, flipper
length, body mass and sex. Figure 3.3, right, shows how bill length and bill
depth are measured. The study includes 344 penguins.

3.2 Exploratory Data Analysis
Statistical summaries of attributes such as counts, averages, minimum and
maximum values, and visualizations that can highlight trends and patterns are
useful in understanding general characteristics of the sample of objects, and to
develop a strong intuition for the population of objects as a whole. Exploring
data also helps uncover data quality issues such as missing values, assess its
relevance and sufficiency for AI tasks, and identify unusual and abnormal
values of objects that could either merit special treatment or potentially derail
AI models.
ReferenceBox 3.2.1: EDA and Visualization
Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) is the process of summarizing general
characteristics of a collection of objects, through statistical summaries
and visualizations of the attributes.
Descriptive statistics are computations based on attributes that capture
important characteristics of a given collection of objects. The minimum
or the smallest value, maximum or largest value, average and median are
examples of descriptive statistics. No matter the size of the data,
descriptive statistics provide a compact set of statistical summaries that
capture important aspects of the collection of objects.
Visualizations are graphical and pictorial representations of statistical
characteristics. Bar charts, dotplots, line graphs, scatterplots and maps
are examples of visualization of data.
To understand the power of EDA, consider Figure 3.4 created by the United
States Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) through their interactive air
quality index dashboard. Ozone data is used to monitor and forecast ozone
levels and warn the public about hazardous air quality conditions which could
be life-threatening to vulnerable groups such as the elderly. The data (ozone
variations in Ann Arbor, MI during the year 2023) consists of thousands of
observations and would be incomprehensible as a collection of numbers, but
when summarized and visualized, immediately reveals key insights:
The ozone levels (dark blue line) in 2023 spiked to dangerous highs during
the month of July making the air unhealthy for humans.
However, the 2023 ozone levels were not the worst (or best) observed in the
past 24 years based on the high/low values (light-blue shaded region).
The ozone levels in 2023 were worse than the five-year average of 2019-
2023 ozone levels (thick gray line).
Extended Description
FIGURE 3.4: The United States Environmental Protection Agency collects air
quality data and makes it available through its interactive air quality index
dashboard. The plot shows ozone variations with respect to time during the year
2023 in Ann Arbor, MI. ⏎
This type of visualization is a time series plot, explained in Section 3.8.6. To
explore other ozone data on the EPA website, click (cid:0).
Data Debate 3.2.1 Discuss the plot of ozone variations in Figure 3.4. Were
there months in 2023 when the air was unhealthy for sensitive groups? What
type of statistical summaries were used to create the graph?
As another instance of the application of EDA, consider the data in Example
3.1.2 in Section 3.1. We can make the following observations:
The shortest student is 48 inches tall.
The tallest student is 70 inches tall.
The most frequent height is 51 inches.
The height range is 48 to 70, with a spread of 70-48=22 inches.
The middlemost observation when the heights are sorted in order is 55.
Computing quantities such as “shortest”, “tallest”, “most frequent” and “range
of values” are common ways of capturing data characteristics and patterns, and
good examples of descriptive statistics.
Statistical summaries and descriptive statistics depend on the type of
attributes. In this chapter we will discuss summaries suitable for quantitative
variables in Sections 3.3 (Typical values), 3.4 (Data spread) and 3.5 (Shape of
the data spread), and summaries appropriate for qualitative variables in Section
3.6 (Counts and proportions).
Important note: There is a distinction between data that we observe, such as
the 344 penguins in Example 3.1.1, and the data of all possible penguins,
possibly over 40 million in Antarctica. The subset that we observe is called a
sample, while the set of all possible penguins is called a population. We will
learn more about samples and populations in Chapter 4. Descriptive statistics
based on samples are used to learn general properties of the entire population. In
this chapter, we will define descriptive statistics in terms of computations using
the observed data (as opposed to the probabilistic notion of expected values)
since the focus is on data intuition.
3.3 Descriptive Statistics: Typical values
Descriptive statistics are used to convey important characteristics of a group of
objects, and to compare different groups. For example, the average height of a
middle-schooler is 54 inches, and the average height of a basketball player is 80
inches. Irrespective of the size of the group, whether 11 students or 4 million
students, a single number, the average height, represents the typical height of
middle schoolers. Averages can also be used to compare typical heights of
middle school students (54 inches) with typical heights of basketball players (80
inches) and infer that on average, middle school students tend to be
considerably shorter than basketball players.
The average, or mean, represents the characteristics of a typical or
representative object of the group. Intuitively, many objects in the group have
attribute values close to the typical value, while a smaller proportion of objects
would have attribute values farther away. The notion of close is based on a
numerical distance, e.g., 4 is closer to 3 than 10.
There are several good choices for describing typical values of quantitative
attributes. In this section, we discuss three popular statistics: the mean (also
known as the average), median and mode.
ReferenceBox 3.3.1: Mean, Median, and Mode ⏎
The average or mean of a set of attribute measurements

dividing the sum by the number of values, n, also known as the size of
the data set X.

A median is the “middlemost” observation of a set of ordered values.
After ranking the values in X in ascending or descending order, the
median is a value in X such that half the values in X are less than the
median value, and half the values are greater. Try the prompt in
GeminiBox 3.3.1.
A mode of a set of numbers X is the value that occurs most often.
GeminiBox 3.3.1: Median computation ⏎
“How is the median computed?” What happens when there is a tie for
middle values? (cid:0)
Review the definitions in ReferenceBox 3.3.1 and use the data in Example
3.1.2 to manually compute the three descriptive statistics to get an intuition for
their meaning. Compare your results with those below.
The mean or average height of the students is sum of the heights of all 11
students divided by the number of students:
608/11 = 55.27.
Note that while the average height is representative of the whole group, there
is no student in the group who is actually of average height.
The median height is 55, because it is exactly in the middle of the rank
ordered data, with 5 values less than 55 and 5 values greater than 55.
48,50,51,51,51,55,55,58,59,60,70.
It is 6th largest value among 11 numbers.
The mode of the 11 student heights is 51.
48,50,51,51,51,55,55,58,59,60,70.
There are three students that are 51 inches tall, making it the most frequently
occurring value of height.
Data Debate 3.3.1 Which of the three descriptive statistics, mean, median,
mode, is the easiest to compute? Which is the hardest to compute? Why? What
happens when there are a thousand data points, not just 11? A million?
The median requires ranking the data in ascending or descending order, called
sorting. The mean requires adding numbers together; the order of the data does
not matter. Use the prompt in GeminiBox 3.3.2 to learn more about the
computing process, and how it varies with the size of the data.
GeminiBox 3.3.2: Mean vs. median ⏎
“Which is harder to compute, mean or median, why?” (cid:0)
Averages and medians are used extensively in many fields because they are
easy to understand and useful for comparing different groups. In Figure 3.5, left,
changes in average temperatures are used by NASA to study changes in climate.
This type of figure where shades of color vary in proportion to the values of the
variable is called a heatmap, described in Section 3.8.7. The average
temperature over the period 2011-2021 is compared with the average
temperature during the 20-year baseline period 1956-1976. The difference in the
averages is plotted on the world map. Cooler blue shades represent regions
where average temperatures have dropped since the baseline period, and warmer
red shades represent regions where the average temperatures have risen. The
greater the change, the deeper the shades. We can make the following
observations:
The North Pole and regions around it have seen a dramatic increase in
average temperature from the period 1956-1976 to the period 2011-2021, an
increase between 3.6 ∘ F to 7.2 ∘ F.
A small region near the South pole has seen a cooling of average
temperatures, around -1.8 ∘ F to -0.9 ∘ F drop.
Most of the world has seen a general increase in average temperatures as
reflected by the preponderance of warmer hues.

(NASA). Right: Median ages are used in demographic studies (Our World in
Data). ⏎
Data Debate 3.3.2 Based on Figure 3.5, what happened to average ocean
temperatures between 1956-1976 and 2011-2021? Where are the regions in the
world that have seen the greatest increase, more than 7.2 ∘ F denoted by a deep
red? Where are the regions that have seen the greatest drop in average

and F? Is there an issue in comparing a 20-year average with a 10-year

Figure 3.5, right, shows median ages of the populations of six countries, plus
the world, over the period 1950 to 2100. This is a time series plot where the
values of a quantitative variable are plotted with respect to time, discussed in
Section 3.8.6. In Figure 3.5, right, future values of median age based on
historical values, called forecasts, are shown as dotted lines. We notice that:
The median age of the world population has been rising rapidly since the
1950s and 1960s, with Japan seeing the steepest increase.
Based on median age, Japan and the United States have the oldest population
at the moment, and India and Nigeria the youngest.
China will overtake both Japan and the USA by the 2050s in terms of an
aging population.
Nigeria will continue to have the youngest population based on median age.
Data Debate 3.3.3 What does an increase in median age over time mean?
(Hint: longevity) Why do you think that is? What are the consequences of
having an older population? Is it an advantage for a country to have a
predominantly young population? Would it have been better to use the mean age
to study the changes over time? Why or why not?
3.3.1 Robots and robust statistics
Companies like Amazon use robots and AI in their warehouses to perform
physical tasks such as lifting objects that could potentially cause repetitive
stress injuries to humans. AI also helps with the optimal placement in the
warehouse of goods, to sort them quickly, and to find the most efficient way to
deliver them. Read the article How Amazon Uses Robots in Its Warehouses to
Improve Shopping, Optimize Stocking and Delivering Products in Section 3.11.
In order to monitor the performance of robots and quantify the benefits of their
use, simple descriptive statistics are computed and updated continuously as new
data and measurements become available. What happens when a descriptive
statistic needs updating?
As a hypothetical example, suppose that the warehouse robots are assigned
numerical scores that reflect their ability to lift objects. The greater the score,
the greater the weight they can lift. Figure 3.6 shows a schematic depiction of
robots with their numerical strength score. The black scores represent the
original scores. Then two of the robots are upgraded and the new strength scores
are shown in orange beneath the original scores. Using the definitions in
ReferenceBox 3.3.1, the mean strength score before the upgrade is, 5, the
median is 5, and the mode is also 5. After the upgrade, the mean is 5.67, the
median and mode remain unchanged at 5.
FIGURE 3.6: Example of warehouse robots assigned scores based on their
strength, shown in black. Two of the robots are upgraded to newer models
(orange). The strength scores of the new robots are shown in orange.
Descriptive statistics such as mean and median robot strength will need to be
updated after the robot upgrade. Would that be easy? ⏎
What if the new strength score of 15 was mistyped as 150? The mean
becomes 20.67, while the median and mode remain the same. And if the
mistyped value is 15000, the mean shoots up to 1670.67 while the median and
mode remain unchanged. That is, the mean is highly sensitive to unusually large
or small values and can change drastically even if one data value is abnormal or
mistyped.
What would it take for the value of the median to change from 5 to 8? That
means exactly half the robots should have strength scores of 8 or more. This
would require at least 3 robots to be upgraded to have strength scores of 8 or
more. Therefore, in order for the median to change, almost half the data values
need to change, making it insensitive or robust to a few errors or abnormal
values.
Both mean and median are easy to understand and interpret. A single unusual
value can significantly change the value of the mean, while the median changes
only when a significant proportion of data values change. In other words, a
mean is more sensitive but less robust to abnormal values as compared to the
median. Which one to choose depends on the application. A rigorous
introduction to robust statistics can be found in Peter Huber's Robust Statistical
Procedures [22].
Data Debate 3.3.4 Why do we need both the mean and the median? What are
the advantages and disadvantages of each? When would you use the mean, and
when would you use the median? Give examples.
3.4 Descriptive Statistics: Data spread
In addition to typical values, the spread or range of data values is an important
characteristic. Data spread can be used to identify abnormal values, establish
acceptable ranges, and for planning purposes. For example, to prepare for a
family reunion, it would be useful to know the age ranges of attendees, e.g., 2
months to 99 years, in order to provide bassinets for babies, high chairs and
booster seats for young children, and wheelchairs for the elderly, if necessary.
Data spread is used in medical tests such as those that monitor blood pressure or
cholesterol, by specifying a range of normal values. Any values outside that
range are flagged as problematic. Such tests compute normal ranges of blood
pressure and cholesterol based on observation of millions of patients.
Dispersion is the statistical term for the spread of attribute values of a
quantitative variable. The greater the dispersion, the greater the span or spread
of values across the number line. We describe below common descriptive
statistics used to quantify dispersion of the values of a quantitative variable.
ReferenceBox 3.4.1: Min, Max, and Range ⏎
Dispersion refers to the spread of the values of a quantitative variable
along the number line, or in attribute space. Attributes whose values are
bunched together have small dispersion while attributes whose values
span larger parts of the number line have a high dispersion.
The maximum of a quantitative attribute X is its largest observed value.
The minimum of a quantitative attribute X is its smallest observed value.
The minimum and the maximum together are called extreme values since
they represent the two extremes of the observed spread of attribute
values.
The range of an attribute X is the difference between the maximum and
minimum values.
Range = Maximum − Minimum.
After reviewing the definitions in ReferenceBox 3.4.1, consider the data in
Example 3.1.2. The smallest height, known as minimum height is 48 inches, the
largest height known as the maximum height is 70, and the range which is the
difference between the maximum and the minimum is 70-48=22.
Data Debate 3.4.1 Do you think these statistics will change if you choose a
different set of 11 middle school students?
Data Debate 3.4.2 Do you think the minimum, maximum and range are robust?
Are they easy to compute? When should they be used, and when should they be
avoided?
3.4.1 Rank based statistics
Percentiles are popular descriptive statistics that measure dispersion. They are
used for ranking an individual relative to their peers, e.g., in pediatric growth
charts, educational testing such as SAT and ACT, athletic performances, and
measuring health of individuals. Percentiles are particularly useful for data sets
that contain millions of data points, such as the set of students who take
standardized tests, roughly 1.4 million each year. Students in the same
percentile have similar scores, while students in higher percentiles have higher
scores than those below. For example, a student with a composite ACT score of
24 would be in the 80th percentile of test takers, i.e., the student's ACT score is
higher than the scores of 80% of students who take the ACT.
Percentiles are computed by arranging the data in rank order, e.g., smallest to
largest, or vice versa, and dividing the ordered data into 100 intervals, each of
which contains 1% of the data values. The values at the boundaries of these
intervals are the percentiles. That is, the interval between two consecutive
(1/100)th
percentiles will contain one percent of the ordered data.
The notion of percentiles can be generalized to any fraction, e.g., deciles that
(1/10)th
divide the ranked data into tenths , quartiles that divide the ranked data
(1/4)th
into quarters , and so on. The term quantile refers to values that divide
the ranked data into specified equal portions.
ReferenceBox 3.4.2: Quantiles: Percentiles and Quartiles ⏎
A percentile of a set of quantitative attribute values is a number which
indicates the percentage of values that are less than that value. For
example, if you are in the 61st percentile of a group of runners, then 61%
of the runners are slower than you. Roughly 1% of the attribute values lie
in the interval between two adjacent percentiles.
Quartiles divide ordered attribute values into quarters. For example, the
first quartile, denoted by Q1, is greater than 25% of the ordered values
but less than 75% of values. The third quartile, denoted by Q3, is greater
than 75% of the data but less than 25% of data. The median is the second
quartile, denoted by Q2, and denotes the attribute value that divides the
ordered data in two such that each contains 50% of the ordered values.
In general, a set of q-quantiles divide the set of rank ordered attribute
1/q
values into q intervals that contain equal ( ) proportion of the data.
A common measure of dispersion based on quantiles is the Interquartile
Range (IQR).
IQR = Q3 − Q1.
Review the definitions in ReferenceBox 3.4.2 and verify that for Example
3.1.2, the first quartile of student heights is Q =51, the second quartile (median)
1
is Q =55, and the third quartile Q =58.5. The interquartile range is
2 3
IQR = Q − Q = 58.5 − 51 =
7.5.
3 1
Dispersion can be measured based on the distances between successive
quantiles. If the data is contained in a compact interval, the quantiles will be
close together on the number line and the distances between adjacent quantiles
will be small. Conversely, if the data is dispersed widely across the number line,
the intervals between consecutive quantiles will be larger. Furthermore, if the
distances between successive quantiles are roughly the same, then the data is
evenly spread. If not, the data is concentrated more in regions where the
quantiles are close together, and it is spread out where the adjacent quantiles are
farther apart. This intuition underlies the concept of probability distributions
discussed in Chapter 5.
Data Debate 3.4.3 What type of a percentile is the median? What type of
percentiles are the minimum and maximum values? Would it make sense to
compute percentiles when you have 30 data values?

flipper length falls in the interval on the X-axis corresponding to the bar. Deciles

Adelie and Gentoo penguins have similar dispersion of values of flipper
length, but in general the flipper lengths of Gentoo penguins are much
higher. Adelie and Chinstrap penguins have similar flipper lengths, but the
dispersion in flipper lengths of Adelie penguins is higher.
The collective shape of the dotplots on the left, i.e., the way the values are
stacked, is similar to the histogram on the right, with two peaks. The two
plots represent the same information but the dotplot breaks down the
distribution of flipper lengths by species.
The distances between adjacent deciles (red dots) and the height of the bars
of the histogram are inversely related, i.e., the closer the decile dots, the
higher the bars. Why?

3.4.2 Deviation based statistics
Ranking attribute values in order to quantify dispersion is difficult when the
number of values is large. Instead, it is easier to measure dispersion with respect
to a reference point. The distance of a value of a quantitative variable from a
reference point is called a deviation, e.g., how much does a value deviate from a
typical value? The greater the deviation of values, the greater dispersion in the
data.
Figure 3.8 shows a bar plot (discussed in Section 3.8.4) of the student heights
from Example 3.1.2. Blue dots represent the individual data points. The yellow
dot represents the mean. The distances of the blue dots from the yellow dot
(mean) along the X-axis represent deviations from the mean.
Extended Description
FIGURE 3.8: A horizontal bar plot of student heights where the dark line inside
the box is the median, and the left and right edges of the blue box are Q1 and
Q3, respectively. The two small vertical lines connected to the blue box with
dotted lines represent the whiskers. Any values outside the whiskers, such as the
dot circled in red at the extreme right, represent outliers, or extreme values. bar
plots are discussed in Section 3.8.4. An overlayed dotplot shows the 11 heights
of individual students. The yellow dot is the mean. The distances of blue dots
from the yellow dot (mean) represent deviations from the mean. The figure was
drawn using the R programming language. ⏎
σ2
Variance, , is a common measure of dispersion computed with respect to
deviations from the mean. It is the average of the squared deviations from the
mean. The formula is shown in ReferenceBox 3.4.3. The square root of the
variance is the standard deviation, σ.
In Example 3.1.2, the 11 deviations from the mean value of 55.27,
corresponding to the 11 values of height are given by:
{−7.27,−4.27,−4.27,−4.27,−5.27,−0.27,3.72,4.72,14.72,−0.27,2.72.}
The variance, computed using the formula in ReferenceBox 3.4.3 is σ2 = 39.62,
and its square root, the standard deviation σ=6.29.
The median absolute deviation (MAD) uses the median as a reference point.
It defines dispersion as the median of the absolute values of the deviations from
the median. For the data in Example 3.1.2, the deviations from the median value
of 55 are given by:
{−7,−4,−4,−4,−5,0,4,5,15,0,3}
and the absolute deviations from the median are:
{7,4,4,4,5,0,4,5,15,0,3},
and the ordered absolute deviations from the median are:
{0,0,3,4,4,4,4,5,5,7,15}.
The median of the absolute values of the deviations from the median is 4.
ReferenceBox 3.4.3: Deviations: Variance and Median Absolute
Deviation ⏎
The deviation of a point from the mean is the difference between the
mean and that value.
The variance is given by the mean of the squares of the deviation from
the mean. For a set X = x 1 ,x 2 ,…,x n of n values, the variance σ2 is
given by:

Data Debate 3.4.5 Compare the values of the standard deviation and MAD in
the above example. Are they the same? Why or why not?
Most of the statistics discussed in this section that measure dispersion are
easy to compute and understand. Variance is less robust than MAD, analogous
to the mean and the median. Percentiles and other quantiles are useful when
there is a sufficient amount of data, e.g., more than 500 values. However, it is
harder to rank order large amounts of data. The correct choice of a statistic
depends on the application, and the nature of data. The first five chapters of Ron
Pearson's Mining Imperfect Data [32] provide an intuitive and hands-on
introduction to descriptive statistics and EDA.
Data Debate 3.4.6 Can you think of additional ways of quantifying typical
values and data dispersion? Are they robust? Are they easy to compute? Are
they easy to understand?
3.5 Descriptive Statistics: Shape of the data spread
In addition to the amount of spread along the X-axis, the manner in which the
data is distributed between the extremes of the minimum and maximum values
is important as well. Is it all piled up in one place or is it spread out evenly?
Consider Figure 3.9, generated by Gemini AI. The horizontal X-axis represents
the values of a variable and the vertical Y-axis represents the proportion of data
that value accounts for. This is a high level, approximate interpretation of the
figure, we will learn more about probability distributions in Chapter 5.
FIGURE 3.9: This figure was generated using Gemini AI. It shows four data
spreads, each with a different shape depending on how the data is distributed
along the X-axis. Left to right: Evenly spread (Uniform), bell-shaped and
symmetric (Normal) that follows the 68-95-99.7% rule, more data away from
the typical value but still symmetric (Cauchy), asymmetric (χ2). ⏎
The first graph on the left shows an even distribution of values. For example,
if you hit a buzzer that pops up a letter of the alphabet, A through Z, 26,000
times, then each of the 26 values, {A, B, …, Z } will occur roughly 1000 times.
A plot of counts of each of the alphabet values will look like a table top, called a
Uniform distribution.
The second graph from the left is a Normal distribution, where typical values
occur more frequently than others but follow a particular set of rules so that
68% of the data falls in an interval on the X-axis within one standard deviation
of the mean, 95% within two standard deviations of the mean, and 99.7% within
three standard deviations of the mean. This is often referred to as a bell-curve.
The particular shape and spread of the data that follow the 68-95-99.7% rule for
dispersion of data are special because many natural phenomena based on
averages mimic this shape. We will learn about the Normal distribution, also
known as Gaussian, in Chapter 5.
The third graph is also symmetric but has more data concentrated away from
the center of the distribution relative to the Normal distribution. It is said to be
“heavy-tailed” since it has more data piled away from the center. Such
distributions do not follow the rule that a Normal distribution does. In fact,
some such distributions do not even have a well-defined standard deviation.
The last graph is an asymmetric dispersion of data, with a large proportion of
data piled up at the smaller values on the X-axis and tapering away to a lesser
proportion of data as the data values get larger. Many income distributions
demonstrate this shape.
ReferenceBox 3.5.1: Symmetry and Shape: Skewness and Kurtosis ⏎
Skewness of a set of attribute values measures symmetry in the data
spread relative to the Normal distribution. There are multiple ways of
measuring skewness as discussed in this Wikipedia article, but the most
intuitive one is Pearson 2 skewness, given by:
⎪ ⎪

where s is the data standard deviation. The Normal distribution has 0
skewness since the mean and median are the same.
Kurtosis refers to the presence of outliers in the data relative to the
Normal distribution. It is given by:
–⎧ n ⎫
∑(x − X)4

where s is the data standard deviation. In general, larger the value of κ,
greater the proportion of non-typical values in the data.
Skewness, defined in ReferenceBox 3.5.1, denoted by γ, represents the
asymmetry in the spread of data values. It measures the deviation of the median
from the mean relative to the dispersion measured by the standard deviation.
The Normal distribution has 0 skewness, since it is symmetric and its mean is
always equal to its median. A positive value of skewness means that the mean of
the data values is greater than the median, implying that the data values are
spread out farther on the higher side, while a negative skewness implies that the
data is spread out farther on the lower side. It takes just one value to change the
skewness, since it is based on distance rather than the proportion of values.
Kurtosis, defined in ReferenceBox 3.5.1 and denoted by κ, quantifies the
proportion and extent of outliers in the data. The value of κ is 3 for the Normal
distribution. If the kurtosis of a set of data values is less than 3, then the data has
fewer non-typical values, and a value of kurtosis greater than 3 implies a greater
preponderance of non-typical values relative to the Normal. Many software
κ − 3
tools report the excess kurtosis, i.e., , to make it easier to compare to the
Normal distribution whose excess kurtosis is 0.
Computing skewness and kurtosis from data, especially small data sets, is
tricky. The formulas need adjustments to keep the statistics representative of the
population values of skewness and kurtosis. The adjustment is based on the
asymptotic behavior of the sample statistics as the sample size gets larger. It is
best to compute skewness and kurtosis using software tools. For a detailed
explanation, read the description in the Engineering Statistics Handbook
provided by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). (cid:0)
Data Debate 3.5.1 We have used the package “moments” in R to compute the
following values of skewness and kurtosis.
The sample skewness for the data in Example 3.1.2 is γ=1.096311. Is the data
in Example 3.1.2 skewed? If so, in which direction?
The kurtosis is κ=3.669507, with an excess kurtosis of 0.669. Does it have a
propensity to outliers relative to the Normal distribution? Use Gemini or any
other software tool to compute γ and κ for the 11 student heights and compare
with the results above.
3.6 Descriptive Statistics: Qualitative variables
Descriptive summaries used to represent quantitative variables cannot be used
to describe qualitative variables. For example, what is the mean of the attribute
“color” that can take values “red”,“blue” or “green”? Typically, qualitative
variables are described using counts and proportions.
For instance, consider the penguin data set in Example 3.1.1. There are three
qualitative variables: species, island and sex. The variable sex can either be
“male” or “female”. Of the 344 data records, only 333 have legitimate values
for the variable sex. Of these 333, there are 165 females and 168 males. This
information is summarized in Table 3.2.
TABLE 3.2: Descriptive statistics for a
qualitative variable sex (Palmer penguins data):
counts, proportions and percentages. ⏎
Sex Count Proportion Percentage
Female 165 0.495496 49.55%
Male 168 0.504504 50.45%
Total 333 1.0 100%
Counts and proportions are necessary for visualizing qualitative variables,
e.g., bar charts and pie charts, as discussed in Section 3.8.
ReferenceBox 3.6.1: Counts, Proportions and Percentages
Counts represent the frequency of each value of a qualitative variable in
the data. For example, according to Table 3.2, the qualitative variable
“sex” was recorded as “female” for 165 penguins and “male” for 168
penguins in the Palmer penguin data described in Example 3.1.1.
Proportions represent the fraction of objects that have a particular value
of a qualitative variable. For example, the proportion of female penguins
is 0.495496.

3.7.1 Data quality issues
Data collection, storage and retrieval processes typically involve many stages,
and problems can arise at any of these stages, impacting the quality of data.
Data may be missing, mislabeled, or incorrectly recorded due to human error or
software glitches. For example, a response of “30 hours” to an online survey
regarding daily hours spent on social media is obviously incorrect. The first task
in EDA is to check for quality issues. Do you see any missing values in the
Penguin data set?
Data Debate 3.7.2 What could happen if we try to create charts, or perform
data analysis with missing or incomplete data? Should we discard the missing
data? What if there is too much missing data? What if the data is missing at
random? What if the data is missing only for a particular group?
For the purpose of this analysis, we have dropped missing values from
computations. We could have potentially guessed the missing values, using a
process called imputation. However, changing data values has consequences. In
Chapter 8, we will learn more about these topics and the potential for distorting
data characteristics while cleaning data.
Next, let us use an Excel spreadsheet and its built-in functions (formulas) that
calculate descriptive statistics such as mean, median, mode, range, and standard
deviation. The descriptive statistics for bill length of the Palmer penguins are
shown in Figure 3.11, from which we can infer that:
There are more Adelie penguins than other species, e.g., more than twice as
many as Chinstrap. This means that the characteristics of the Adelie penguins
will dominate any general statistics computed for all the penguins
collectively.
Adelie penguins have smaller bill lengths compared to Chinstrap and Gentoo
based on the mean and median values. There is less dispersion in the values
of Adelie penguin bill lengths based on range and variance of the bill lengths.
Gentoo bill length values have a small positive skewness implying that the
higher values are farther away on the number line, while the Chinstrap bill
length values have a small negative skewness implying that the lower values
are farther away making the mean value less than the median.

3.8 Exploring Data through Visualization
As computer hardware and software become more sophisticated, it has become
possible to draw brilliant and complex graphs, including with animation and
three-dimensional effects like those seen in AI-generated holograms. Every
field, from genetics to marketing to astronomy, has become increasingly reliant
on graphics to quickly and easily communicate findings. This is because the
human brain excels at picking up visual cues and patterns.
The power of visuals is such that AI has recreated the images of dead actors
to star in entire films to capture their magic even after their passing. See Section
3.11 for BBC's article How AI is bringing film stars back from the dead. While
the magic of AI can create holograms, there is a small set of canonical chart
types that is essential for EDA. These are commonly used in scientific journals,
in mass media, in marketing and financial analytics, and in many disciplines.
Such charts are intuitive and easy to understand, some of which we have already
explored in Figures 3.4, 3.5, 3.7 and 3.8. We detail below how to create and
interpret some popular visualization charts.
3.8.1 Circle (pie) charts
A pie chart is used to visualize qualitative variables, and to show the share of
each of its category values. The proportion of each category is represented by
the percentage of the area of the circular pie. This is one of the simplest
graphical representations of data. Here is the link to the step-by-step instructions
on how to use the Excel tools to create a pie chart. (cid:0)
Table 3.3 shows the count of penguins on each of the three islands, and the
counts of penguins that belong to each of the three species. Figure 3.12 is a
pictorial representation of these counts where the proportion of the area of each
slice of the pie to the area of the entire pie is the same as that of the count of that
category to the total. We can infer that:
Almost half the penguins live on Biscoe Island
Almost half the penguins belong to the Adelie species
The pie charts do not have any information about which species live on
which island

3.8.2 Bar charts
A bar graph or bar chart represents the share of each category of a qualitative
variable with rectangular bars whose heights or lengths are proportional to the
counts of values that they represent. The bars can be plotted vertically or
horizontally. The data represented by the bars can be counts, percentages or
proportions, or averages of a quantitative variable corresponding to a category.
Each data value should belong to only one category, and every category should
be represented by exactly one of the bars. Bar graphs are intended for
qualitative or discrete variables.
Figure 3.13, left, shows the same information as the pie chart in Figure 3.12,
right, but not as a share of a pie. However, the relative proportion of each
category is easier to perceive in a pie chart. Bar charts are arguably better than
pie charts when the number of categories of a qualitative variable is larger.
FIGURE 3.13: The bar graph on the left represents the counts of the three
species of penguins found on the islands. The figure on the right represents a
stacked bar graph of penguin species on each island. These bar graphs were
created by using the pivot table in Excel. ⏎
Here are step-by-step instructions on how to create bar charts using the chart
feature in Excel: (cid:0). Microsoft provides a tutorial on how to use the COUNTIF
function used in the creation of bar charts: (cid:0).
A stacked bar graph or stacked bar chart extends the standard bar chart from
one qualitative variable to two or more. Each bar in a bar chart is divided into a
number of sub-bars stacked end to end, each one corresponding to a level of the
second or third categorical variable. Figure 3.13, right, shows a stacked bar
chart of the number of penguins on each island, split further by species. The
advantage of a stacked bar chart is that we can visualize two or more qualitative
variables at once. From Figure 3.13, right, it is obvious that Torgersen Island
only has Adelie penguins living on it. It would have taken more effort to infer
this from merely examining rows and columns of an Excel spreadsheet.
Data Debate 3.8.2 What can you infer from the stacked bar chart in Figure
3.13? Did a visual representation of the data help? How many variables do you
think we can represent using a conventional bar chart?
3.8.3 Histograms
A histogram is the equivalent of a bar chart for quantitative variables,
particularly continuous ones. Data values corresponding to quantitative
variables can be grouped together into intervals of values in a meaningful way.
For instance, in Example 3.1.2 the student heights can be grouped into ranges
such as 45-50 inches, 50-55 inches, 55-60 inches and 60 inches or more.
The process of grouping data into intervals of values is called binning. Every
data value must belong to one bin, and exactly one bin. There are many ways of
determining appropriate intervals to create the bins. The easiest approach is to
take the range of data values in the data set and divide it by the desired number
of bins, and use this to determine the bin size, or bin length. Such histograms are
called equi-width since every bin is of the same width.
Another option is to choose the quantiles to define the bin intervals, e.g.,
quartiles. This approach will ensure that each bin contains roughly the same
proportion of data. Such histograms are called equi-depth since the height of the
bar corresponding to each bin is roughly of the same height (or depth). Once the
bins are determined, it is just a matter of counting the number of data values that
fall in each bin, called the bin count or bin frequency.
The book Multivariate Density Estimation: Theory, Practice, and
Visualization by David W. Scott [38] has an excellent overview of different
types of histograms and their relative merits and weaknesses.
Example 3.8.1 Suppose we wish to create a histogram with four bins using the
data in Example 3.1.2. We can compute the following:
range=max-min=70-48=22
number of bins=4
bin width=22/4=5.5
bin intervals (bins) = [48,53.5), [53.5,59),[59,64.5), [64.5,70], where we
start with the minimum value, add the bin width to get the upper boundary of
the first bin, which is the lower boundary of the next bin, and so on until we
equal the maximum value.
bin counts (in order): 5, 4, 1, 1. For example, the values 48, 51, 51, 51, and
50 lie in the interval [48,53.5] and contribute to the count of 5 for that bin.
Note that the bin boundaries overlap, but the bins are non-overlapping since by
convention, any value that falls exactly on the upper boundary is not included in
that bin, but in the next higher one. The exception to this rule is the maximum
value.
Figure 3.7, right, depicts an equi-width histogram based on the flipper length
measurements of all penguins. The programming language R was used to create
the histogram. Each bin is approximately 5 mm in width, hence equi-width. The
bin corresponding to the interval of flipper length 205-210 mm has roughly a
30-bin count, implying that around 30 penguins have flipper lengths between
205 and 210 mm. If the bin intervals had been determined using the deciles
represented by the red dots, there would be 10 bins, each with 34 or so penguin
count, but each bin would be of a different width as is evident from the
distances between consecutive deciles represented by the red dots.

3.8.4 Boxplots
A boxplot or box-and-whiskers plot is used to illustrate the spread of data. It can
be drawn horizontally as in Figure 3.8 or vertically as in Figure 3.16.
Figure 3.15, created in Microsoft PowerPoint, shows a schematic diagram of
a bar plot. The blue box is parallel to the number line and contains the middle
50% of the data. Since it is a horizontal bar plot, the blue box's left and right
edges correspond to the first and third quartiles, respectively. The box is divided
inside by a line that represents the median. The whiskers, shown as dotted lines,
extend out beyond the first and third quartiles to represent values outside the
middle half of the data. The whiskers end at what are considered acceptable
limits for data values, called “fences”, denoted by small lines parallel to the
quartile lines of the box. The acceptable limits can be determined in multiple
ways, e.g., the 5th and 95th percentiles to mark the end of the whiskers. The
three values outside the whiskers shown in red are called outliers. Figure 3.8
shows an outlier circled in red, corresponding tho the height of the tallest
middle schooler in Example 3.1.2.
FIGURE 3.15: How to interpret a bar plot. This figure was created in Microsoft
Power Point. ⏎
FIGURE 3.16: Box and whiskers plot created using Excel. The left chart
displays quartiles for the variable bill length for each of the three species of
penguins and the right chart displays the quartiles for flipper length. ⏎
The graphs in Figure 3.16, created in Excel, display three box-and-whiskers
plots, one for each species: Adelie, Gentoo and Chinstrap. The bar plots on the
left correspond to bill length, and the bar plots on the right correspond to flipper
length. The mean is marked inside each box with an “X”. The values of the
mean, median, quartiles and the end of the whiskers, and outliers are shown. We
can make the following observations:
Q1,Q2(median),Q3
The mean, of bill length of Gentoo penguins are
47.50, 45.30, 47.30, 49.55, respectively. The ends of the whiskers are 40.90
and 55.90. The bill length of 59.60 is an outlier for Gentoo penguins.
The flipper length of 210 is an outlier for Adelie penguins but not Chinstrap
or Gentoo.
The mean bill lengths of Chinstrap (48.83) and Gentoo (47.5) are close, but
much higher than that of Adelie (38.79).
The mean and median of flipper length are quite close for all three species
indicating that the distributions are not skewed, confirmed by the skewness
values in Figure 3.11, which are close to 0.
The lengths of the two whiskers are approximately the same for almost all 6
bar plots, implying, again, a symmetry in the distribution of the values of each
of bill length and flipper length for the three species.
The step-by-step process for creating a box-and-whiskers plot using Excel is
available in Supplementary Resources (cid:0), in Chapter 3.
3.8.5 Dotplots and scatterplots
Dotplots are ideal for depicting the spread of a quantitative variable and for
comparing multiple groups as shown in Figure 3.7, left. It is clear that Gentoo
penguins represented by orange dots have higher values of flipper lengths,
compared to the other two species represented by pink and blue dots. A simpler
instance of dotplots is in Figure 3.8 where each blue dot corresponds to the
height of one of the 11 students in Example 3.1.2. Note the three dots stacked
vertically that correspond to the mode at 51. Dotplots are useful for visualizing
smaller data sets. When the volume of data is large, dotplots are too cluttered to
be legible.
A scatterplot is a two-dimensional version of the dotplot. It is used to
visualize the relationship between two quantitative variables, as shown in
Figure 3.17, with one variable depicted on each axis. Each data point is an
ordered tuple of two values, one for each variable, represented as Cartesian
(x,y)
coordinates.
FIGURE 3.17: The scatterplot in Figure 3.17 was created with Excel
spreadsheets. It depicts the relationship between bill length and bill depth for
the three penguin species. Each species (categorical variable) has a different
color as shown in the legend. ⏎
In Figure 3.17, the X-axis depicts the bill length and the Y-axis depicts the bill
depth of penguins. Each dot corresponds to a penguin's bill length and depth,
resulting in 342 dots on this scatterplot. Each color represents a species, as
shown in the legend of the plot. Isn't it amazing that one look at the scatterplot
reveals that each of the three species occupies distinct regions in the X,Y
attribute space, resulting in clear clusters? What do you think that means?
Would you have noticed this clustering of species just by looking at a
spreadsheet of numbers?

the three species? What are some other facts you can gather by examining the
graph?
