when there are too many data points?
3.8.6 Time series
A time series is a special kind of plot which shows how a quantitative variable
changes over time. The X-axis of a time series plot is always time, and the Y-
axis is the quantitative variable being measured at a given instance of time.
Time series charts are widely used to track changes, study trends, i.e., a
sustained change over time in the same direction (e.g., increasing number of
likes on TikTok), and monitor volatility which is variability measured over time
e.g., stock prices. Figure 3.4 is an example of time series of ozone levels in Ann
Arbor, MI. It depicts dangerous spikes in ozone during the summer months.
Figure 3.18, left, depicts data from the United States Census Bureau that
reflects the change in college enrollment, measured in thousands of people,
from 1947 to 2020, by age group and gender. The red lines represent number of
female students, and the blue lines represent male students. Different line types,
e.g., solid, dotted, dashed, represent different age groups as marked on the
graph. We can make some interesting observations:
The number of students aged 14-24 enrolled in college increased steeply over
time for both men and women up until 2010.
Women surpassed men in enrollment in the 1980s in the age groups 25-34
and 14-25, but almost a decade earlier for the age group 35+.
The enrollment declines after 2010 among all age groups and all sexes. It
might have been a lagged effect of the financial crisis of 2008.
FIGURE 3.18: Time series plots depicts the values of a quantitative variable
measured over a period of time, e.g., time series of college enrollment by
gender and age group over the period 1947-2020. From the U.S. Census Bureau.
⏎
3.8.7 Heatmaps
Heatmaps show the intensity of a quantitative variable for several categories on
a colored scale that helps identify regions of high and low intensity. For
example, the map in Figure 3.5, left, shows the changes in average temperatures
during the period 2011-2021 across the globe compared to the average during
the period 1956-1976. Areas where the average change remained unchanged are
represented by white, while those that became warmer are shown in shades of
red, and those that became cooler on an average are shown in shades of blue.
The deeper the shade of red, the hotter a region became, while the deeper the
shade of blue, the cooler a region became. The color gradation is called an
intensity scale since it corresponds to the magnitude of change.
A heatmap is not limited to depicting changes in temperature. It can be used
to visualize the distribution of values of quantitative variable on a graded color
scale across the values of a categorical variable, for example, population density
across the globe or volume of posting on a social media platform. Certain times
of day and certain days of the week have a greater volume of social media posts.
Explore GeminiBox 3.8.1 to learn more.
GeminiBox 3.8.1: AI tools employ heatmaps ⏎
“Give examples of AI heatmaps.” (cid:0)
Heatmaps are used extensively by AI tools. For example, click heatmaps are
used by companies to identify regions of a webpage that have high user
engagement in order to optimize product placement. In the physical world,
cameras track shoppers and generate heatmaps to determine which shelves
attract most foot traffic for determining placement of products on shelves.
AI heatmaps are also used to track eye movements, to identify the most and
least viewed parts of a website as shown in Figure 3.19. Warm red hues denote
areas where viewers lingered, while cooler blue hues represent less viewed
areas. AI tools automatically generate such analytics. Explore AI heatmaps and
their applications using the prompt in GeminiBox 3.8.1
FIGURE 3.19: Heatmap shows eye movements across the webpage. Image
credit: Tschneidr, Wikimedia Commons. (cid:0) ⏎
3.8.8 Choice of graph
We have discussed a number of commonly used visualization techniques. There
are several articles in Section 3.11 that show how the boundaries of
visualization technology are constantly being pushed by technical advances in
graphics, rendering hardware, software and AI. Animation, holograms,
simulations, and 3D graphics are quite popular.
The choice of chart or graph depends on the type of data and amount of data.
Bar charts and pie charts are suitable for qualitative variables, especially when
the number of unique categories is limited. In order to reduce the number, of
categories, those that are not of interest or occur rarely could be grouped
together into a single category called “Other” or “Miscellaneous”.
Dotplots and stacked dotplots are useful for displaying single quantitative
variables, grouped by categories if needed. Scatterplots display the relationship
between two quantitative variables, potentially grouped by a categorical
variable e.g., in Figure 3.17 which shows the scatterplot of bill length vs. bill
depth for three species of penguins. Scatterplots too become cluttered and
indecipherable if there is too much data.
Line plots display changes in one continuous quantitative variable with
respect to another continuous quantitative variable. Time series are special type
of line plots where the X-axis is time.
Heatmaps highlight regions of high and low intensity of a quantitative
variable with respect to the categories of a qualitative variable. They are
visually arresting and convey complex information in an intuitive fashion. They
are used extensively by AI tools for monitoring online user engagement, for
identifying abnormal cell activity in human organs, and for many other
purposes.
The Visual Display of Quantitative Information by Edward Tufte [41] is an
excellent introduction to data and information visualization.
3.9 EDA and Big Data
EDA is particularly useful in handling Big Data since it summarizes large
volumes of data with just a small set of descriptive statistics. The sports industry
is an example where Big Data is utilized as well as generated. In sports,
organizing and analyzing data helps to find patterns, provide feedback to
players and managers, make important decisions, and predict future
performance of teams and players. Moneyball by Michael Lewis [27] is the true
story of data-driven sports management that stunned the world of baseball.
Fantasy sports have truly brought Big Data into the spotlight for sports
enthusiasts by supplying extremely detailed sports statistics about players and
games in real-time as the sporting events unfold. This has led to an
unprecedented participation of audience via fantasy leagues and sports betting.
According to the Fantasy Sports and Gaming Association (FSGA) around 62.5
million people played fantasy sports in the USA and Canada in 2022. In 2023,
the organization estimates that 19% of all American adults played fantasy
sports. Click here (cid:0) to read more.
In addition to sports, EDA of Big Data figures extensively in social media
analytics, real-time marketing and advertising, and studying the spread of
diseases. Unfortunately, EDA of Big Data analytics has also led to the use of
misinformation to influence important outcomes like elections. In Chapter 10,
we will discuss the responsibility of using the power of big data and AI for
improving rather than undermining society.
3.10 EDA and AI
A good foundation in EDA to cultivate data intuition is critical for the effective
use of AI tools. Consider the following general criteria to take into account
while working with large data and AI:
Ease of understanding: Choosing models and methods that are easy to
understand, interpret and communicate to others, without sacrificing
accuracy or efficiency, whenever possible, is a good practice. Almost
everyone intuitively understands the meaning of statistics like mean, median,
minimum and maximum. However, results of many AI models like neural
networks can be understood only by a very small group of experts. EDA is an
effective way to develop intuition for data in the context of the real-world
application it represents and to bridge that gap.
Computational considerations: Computers need memory to store data, and
processors with enough computing power to perform the necessary
computations. In general, the more data, the more the computations needed
to compute statistics and run models. EDA can guide the choice of
appropriate ways to summarize the data, e.g., histograms, that can reduce the
amount and complexity of data supplied to AI models while minimizing the
loss of accuracy and precision.
Ease of updating: The flow of data is constant. In order to reflect the most
current information, statistics, models and other results need to be updated
constantly. Thanks to AI, updating is done automatically whenever we
request information through a prompt. However, some statistics and models
are easy to update, while others are relatively hard, even in the simple case of
mean vs. median as explored by GeminiBox 3.3.2. EDA helps choose
appropriate statistics and models. Work through the following simple
example for an illustration of updating descriptive statistics.
Example 3.10.1 Consider the following example. Suppose you buy 7 pairs of
jeans whose median price is $50 and whose average price is $48. In your
next trip to the mall, you buy four more pairs of jeans whose prices are $20,
$30, $40 and $49. What is the new average price of the 11 pairs of jeans you
possess? Can you compute the new median price?
Robustness: Ideally, the results and output of a data task should not be overly
influenced by a small number of outliers that have extreme data values. This
is especially true when there are data quality issues, e.g., a malfunctioning
device can generate faulty 0's which could bias descriptive statistics like the
mean. EDA can guide the choice of robust statistics depending on the
application. Work through the following simple example to understand the
effect of a single abnormal value on statistics.
Example 3.10.2 Suppose your scores in five attempts in an athletic event,
say, long jump measured in feet, are 15, 14, 16, 16, 14. However, due to
human error, “16” is entered as “1” in your record. What happens to your
average? What happens to your median?
Beware of AI hallucinations: AI tools can generate faulty output. The article
Why Meta's Large Language Model only survived three days (cid:0) explains how
AI can write authoritative sounding “scientific research papers” on bogus
topics like “Bears in space”. We know from our real-word experience that
there are no bears living in space, so this article, however impressive
seeming, cannot be true. In these early stages of AI tools, EDA provides the
data intuition to spot inaccuracies.

4
Sampling: Less Is More
It is estimated that there are 10 quintillion insects, that is
10,000,000,000,000,000, 000 bugs, on this planet. We could not possibly
study all of them. How did they arrive at that number in the first place?
Sampling, where an appropriately chosen subset is used to make
inferences about the entire population of interest, makes it possible to
study insects without having to examine each and everyone individually.
Sampling is used extensively in AI. This chapter motivates the concepts
of populations and samples, the need for sampling, different types of
sampling, variations caused by sampling and the impact it has on results,
and how to ensure that results based on a sample are applicable to the
population in general.
4.1 Why Sample?
Suppose that a truck carrying millions of loose Skittles breaks down and
spills all its contents along a 100-yard stretch which is roughly the length of a
football field. The truck driver says he needs to know the proportion of red
skittles in the spill, urgently, and promises a big prize to the first person with
the correct answer. What would you do? What if you only had two minutes to
provide the answer?
Typically, when there are too many objects to study individually, whether
they are insects, penguins or skittles, it is not possible to study all of them. It
is enough to focus on a relatively small, appropriately chosen subset. The
process of choosing a subset, called sampling, is used extensively in almost
every area, by environmentalists that study changes in air, water, ice (Figure
4.1) and soil, to marine biologists who monitor migration patterns, to
influencers and marketers who use social media to increase user engagement.
FIGURE 4.1: Would it be humanly possible to analyze and study all the ice
and water on the planet? Left: Taking water samples, the U.S. Geological
Survey. Right: Ice core sampling in Green Bay, Lake Michigan by NOAA
Great Lakes Environmental Research Laboratory. ⏎
A population is the set of all objects of interest relevant to a problem. For
example, to study popular sports on American campuses, the population
would consist of all students enrolled in an American campus. A sample is a
carefully chosen subset of the population that is representative of the
population. The objective is to study just the sample and make inferences
about the population in general. Students selected randomly from campuses
across the United States balanced for gender, age and other factors would
constitute a representative sample.
Sampling has many advantages:
It is cost effective, since it is cheaper to observe a small set of objects. For
instance, chasing down every whale in the ocean to measure its weight
would take a lot of people in a lot of boats a lot of time. It might not be
possible to track down every whale even then.
Managing sample data is more efficient in terms of storage, processing
and computing, since it is only a small fraction of the entire population.
Sampling is better for the environment, in terms of physical impact as well
as in terms of the energy requirements. It would be extremely damaging to
dig up the entire ocean bed or all the mountain tops to study their mineral
composition.
In some instances, it is harmful to collect data. If a person is ill, it might be
dangerous to take too much tissue for analysis.
The classic Sampling Techniques by William G. Cochran [9] provides a
comprehensive coverage of sampling topics and their applications. Review
the terminology and definitions in ReferenceBox 4.1.1 thoroughly.
In the instance of the spilled Skittles, the population consists of the Skittles
in the truck that were spilled on the street. Examples of other “populations”of
Skittles include: all the Skittles sold in New Jersey, or all the Skittles
produced in 2023, or all the Skittles bought in the USA in 2023. The notion
of population therefore depends on the problem being studied.
ReferenceBox 4.1.1: Populations and Samples ⏎
The set of all objects relevant to a study is called a population. The
number of objects in the population is called the population size,
denoted by N.
The process of selecting a subset of objects from a population for the
purpose of gathering data and studying is called sampling. The subset
of data created through sampling is called a sample. The number of
objects in a sample is called the sample size, usually denoted by n.
The particular process used to create the sample from the population is
called a sampling method. There are numerous sampling techniques to
meet different types of study criteria.
n/N
The proportion of the sample size to the population size, , is
called the sampling proportion.
Data Debate 4.1.1 What are the advantages and disadvantages of working
with a sample rather than the entire population?
A popular method of sampling is to choose objects at random. For
example, in a raffle draw, the tickets have unique numbers, and a subset of
lucky winners is chosen randomly from among them. The “population”, in
this instance, consists of all the tickets sold, and the “sample” consists of the
winning tickets. What if the owner of the raffle wanted to reward his or her
friends and chose their ticket numbers as the winners of the raffle? Or, chose
the first (or last) five tickets sold to be the winners?
Data Debate 4.1.2 Can two different random samples have the same objects
in them? Is it possible for a sample to have bias?
Analyzing statistical results based on samples versus populations has
demonstrated that in most instances an appropriate sample approximates the
results of the entire population quite well. For example, descriptive statistics
such as the mean and the standard deviation computed from suitably chosen
samples are close to the descriptive statistics based on the data of the entire
population. We can even quantify how closely the results based on a sample
will approximate the results of the true population. Chapters 5 and 6 discuss
the statistical foundations of quantifying the confidence in results based on
samples.
Sampling is a common practice in many data-driven studies. For example,
Figure 4.2 displays the results of disparities in income based on education
attainment. The study uses only 1% of the census data. The graphic illustrates
that:
The median incomes, nationwide, for those with a high school diploma,
$28K,$50K,$62K,
bachelor's degree or graduate degree are
respectively.
The disparities in income by educational level vary from region to region,
shown by the heatmap.
The disparities in income based on education levels are greatest in states
depicted by the darker shades of blue, e.g., California, Texas and New
Jersey, of $20K or more per year.

census data to the public. The above visualization of income disparity based
on education attainment is based on a 1% sample of the entire US population
(335 million). Click for interactive graphics (cid:0). ⏎
4.2 Illustrative Examples of Sampling Applications
Given the amount of data that is being collected daily by sensors in space, on
the ocean, in cars and online, sampling is essential for real-time analytics and
decision-making. Streaming companies sample viewing patterns to identify
popular shows and segments. Different types of voices are sampled to create
universally appealing synthetic voices for AI-assistants such as Siri. Satellites
sample images of the earth to identify natural disasters like wild fires and
floods. In this section, we discuss three illustrative examples of how
sampling is used in the real world.
4.2.1 Sampling in environmental sciences
Sampling is widely used for research by biologists and environmental
scientists because it is often difficult to study entire populations, e.g., whales.
So, how would one collect biological samples from a killer whale swimming
through the ocean? Figure 4.3 shows how NOAA research scientists collect
samples from orcas and dolphins.
Extended Description
FIGURE 4.3: Left: NOAA's image of bathymetry data from ocean floor
mapping through sampling. Right: NOAA Research: Collecting samples from
a boat. A pantropical spotted dolphin surfacing alongside the NOAA Ship
Gordon Gunter. Credit: NOAA Fisheries (Permit No.14450). ⏎
Typically, biologists ride alongside whales or dolphins in a boat and use a
long pole with petridishes attached at the end. The samples are used by
marine scientists for killer whale conservation and research programs. For
example, in a 2017 research paper, Linda Rhodes (manager of the Marine
Microbes and Toxins Program at NWFSC) and her colleagues analyzed
microbes in breath collected from Southern Resident killer whales. They
found that some of the bacteria present were resistant to antibiotics, and
certain bacteria posed a risk of disease. Read about Southern Resident Killer
Whales and sampling in Section 4.6.
Sampling is also used in the Gulf of Mexico to study the effect of oil spills,
e.g., 2010 Deepwater Horizon oil spill and another one in 2016, on
pantropical spotted dolphins shown in Figure 4.3. According to NOAA
scientists, ship-based surveys recorded fewer sightings and smaller group
sizes. The survey team samples multiple groups or sightings to ensure an
appropriate representation of the genetic diversity in the region. Read more
about the pantropical spotted dolphins study in Section 4.6.
Marine research and study of oceans is supported by another instance of
sampling. Research organizations of NOAA map ocean floors through a
special type of sampling to discover new topological features and provide
accurate maps for safe navigation of ocean-going vessels. Ocean exploration
is expensive and dangerous; such maps give researchers the best possible
coverage of the ocean floor while remaining within time and resource
constraints.
4.2.2 AI and sampling
The use of AI and machine learning models has helped make sampling more
efficient. Machine learning models study patterns such as whale movements
and identify high-probability locations and high-likelihood times when the
pods are active. This enables scientists to place AI-equipped drones and
camera sensors with image recognition capabilities at optimal locations to
maximize the potential for capturing meaningful data rather than just an
empty view of the ocean. AI makes data collection more cost-effective
instead of wasting long periods of time and resources monitoring locations
when there is no activity. To learn more, try the prompt in GeminiBox 4.2.1.
GeminiBox 4.2.1: Sampling and AI ⏎
“How can AI be used for sampling in environmental sciences studies?”
(cid:0)
Sampling is used extensively in driving user engagement on social media
sites. Given the volumes of data generated by digital platforms, it would be
impossible to react in a timely manner without sampling. Latency in data-
driven decision-making such as those used by autonomous vehicles, e.g.,
“what is the speed of that moving object and what path is most likely to avoid
a collision”, could potentially have life-threatening consequences. Sampling
reduces such latency by reducing the amount of data that needs to be
processed.
4.2.3 Monitoring quality control through sampling
Occasionally the products we buy, like shirts, hair brushes or toasters, have
little stickers that say “inspected by 007,” implying that they were inspected
and cleared by a quality supervisor, Inspector 007. Sampling is used in
manufacturing and many industries to ensure that products meet
specifications, e.g., a hairbrush must have 20 bristles per square inch. Given
how many thousands of hairbrushes are manufactured, it would be
impossible to examine each and every one of them.
Industrial manufacturing has a systematic and statistical way of monitoring
the quality of processes and goods. Edward Deming is considered to be the
founder of the field of statistical quality control, and for revolutionizing
industrial manufacturing monitoring through continuous sampling. Douglas
Montgomery's Introduction to Statistical Quality Control provides a
comprehensive overview of the topic [30].
Consider the following hypothetical example to understand how sampling
is applied in the food industry. Such customer experience criteria are often
used in restaurant rating apps such as Yelp.
Example 4.2.1 The Farmhouse Bakery is famous for its cookies and cakes.
In order to maintain their sales and reputation and grow their business, the
owners want to ensure the quality of their food, the performance of their
employees, the atmosphere and cleanliness of the premises, the quality of the
ingredients, and the taste and repeat-purchase appeal of the baked goods.
Here are some actions the owners could take, specific to each goal:
Efficiency and performance of staff: randomly observe employees each
week/month
Cleanliness and atmosphere: inspect the premises several times a week,
survey customers randomly to get their input
Quality of ingredients from suppliers: select ingredients each week to
check for freshness and expiration date
Taste: select a batch of baked goods to see if they meet the flavor and taste
that is expected, and repeat this step periodically
It is evident that sampling plays a big part in Farmhouse Bakery's effort at
maintaining high quality. There are different types of sampling that the
bakery can choose from, and selecting the appropriate one is the key to
success.
Data Debate 4.2.1 Why does quality control matter? Discuss a specific
example.
Data Debate 4.2.2 Is sampling a good option for quality control? Are there
other means of quality control in manufacturing?
AI has brought new innovation and technology to industrial quality control
that can both speed up the process as well as reduce human error in manual
inspections. Read Visual Inspection AI: a purpose-built solution for faster,
more accurate quality control in Section 4.6.
4.3 Types of Sampling
To understand different types of sampling, consider the following
hypothetical example. A robot factory makes five different types of robots in
five colors, with five different functions: play games (blue), perform
household chores (brown), inspect cell towers (pink), take care of invalids
(green), or monitor traffic (purple).
A team of research students interested in computer gaming strategies visit
the factory and are allowed to interact with 5 robots from a room full of 25
robots, numbered 1 through 25 as shown in the box on the left in Figure 4.4.
The population size is N=25 since there are 25 robots in the room, and the
sample size is n=5 since students are allowed to choose 5 robots to interact
with.
FIGURE 4.4: The box on the left shows a population of 25 robots, of 5
different types, denoted by a different color. They are numbered 1 through 25
in no particular order. Suppose you need to choose a sample of 5 robots. On
the right-hand side there are 4 samples of 5 robots each, each created using a
different type of sampling. ⏎
How should the students choose the 5 robots? One student suggests
choosing 5 numbers between 1 and 25 randomly, by using a random number
generator such as the Number Picker Wheel here (cid:0) . The random number
generator spits out: { 12, 14, 23, 4, 2}, and the students choose the robots
with those numbers from the box on the left, to create the first sample on the
right in Figure 4.4. Such a sample is called a random sample.
Another student suggests that since the random sample did not include
each type of robot (brown and purple are missing), in order to experience
interacting with all the five different types of robots the students should
choose one of each type reflecting the proportion of each in the population,
creating the second sample in Figure 4.4. This is a stratified sample, which
ensures that each group is represented in the sample. It is also a proportional
sample since the proportion of each type is the same as that in the population.
Another student mentions that the blue robots are trained to play games
with the students, and since that is the research focus of the student group,
they should choose the entire group of blue robots as the sample, as shown in
the third sample on the right in Figure 4.4. Such a sampling method is called
cluster sampling. Cluster sampling is useful when the sample is meant to
serve a very specific goal, for example researching robots that play games. In
such an instance, robots that are meant for other tasks would be of no interest
to the sampling process.
Yet another student argues that the blue robots are very expensive, so the
factory might not allow the students to use all five of their blue robots. The
student goes on to suggest that the robots should be lined up any which way
and every fifth robot should be chosen. In Figure 4.4, the fourth example on
the right is a systematic sample of every fifth robot starting with robot no. 2.
(Periodic sampling is a type of systematic sampling where samples are
selected at regular intervals in time. For example, it is used to poll
telecommunications network systems periodically to monitor performance
and detect unusual activity. Periodic sampling can be misleading if not
applied properly, e.g., if the sampling interval matches the periodicity of the
phenomenon being studied. For example, if we sampled a person's behavior
at 3 AM every 24 hours, we would conclude that the person's sole function is
to sleep.)
Data Debate 4.3.1 Does the starting point make a difference to the sample?
How many distinct systematic samples are possible for a sample of size n
from a population of size N?
Another student suggests that they keep choosing robots until there are at
least two blue robots in the sample. This could happen in just two tries if the
first two robots that are picked happen to be blue, or might take more than 5
samples. Such a method of sampling is called progressive sampling.
Data Debate 4.3.2 The worst case, i.e., the largest size of a sample needed
for the progressive sampling of at least two blue robots is 22. Why?
Review the definitions in ReferenceBox 4.3.1.
ReferenceBox 4.3.1: Types of Sampling ⏎
A random sample consists of objects selected from the population in
no particular order or pattern, e.g., by using a random number
generator or by drawing numbers blindly from a box like in a raffle
lottery.
A stratified sample consists of objects selected from each group in the
population to make sure that every group is represented. If the
proportion of each group in the sample is the same as the proportion in
the population, it is a proportional sample.
Cluster sampling involves choosing an entire group to be included in a
sample, in contrast to proportional sampling.
kth
Systematic sampling involves choosing every object in the
N/k
population, resulting in a sample of size .
Adaptive or progressive sampling requires adding objects to a sample
until a particular goal, e.g., at least two blue robots, is met.
The classic Sampling Techniques by William G. Cochran [9] provides a
comprehensive coverage of sampling topics and their applications.

instructions below:
First, compute the population proportion of each species by dividing the

shown in Column 3 of Table 4.1.

It is important to work through the hands-on example above to get an
intuition for proportional sampling. However, in practice, most data analysis
software tools provide options for choosing samples automatically, e.g.,

data set. Step-by-step instructions on how to generate random samples using

4.3.2 To replace or not?
In the robot example, suppose we wish to study the strength of robots.
Measurements regarding strength, e.g., maximum weight that the robot can
lift, are collected from each robot in the sample. A robot is set aside once it
has been chosen for inclusion in the sample and will not be selected again.
However, sometimes it is useful to return an object to the population to be
included again in the next sample selection.
To demonstrate, consider the following example, where the robots are
numbered from 1 through 5. The numbers refer to the robot's ID number, not
its strength measurement. Suppose that there were only 5 robots in the
population. If we need measurements from a sample of 4 robots, we only
have 5 possible sample choices, robots no. (1,2,3,4), (2,3,4,5), (3,4,5,1),
(4,5,1,2) and (5,1,2,3). Most of these samples resemble each other since any
two samples have at least 3 robots in common, hence three strength
measurements in common. Each sample object selection reduces the number
of remaining robots to be chosen for the sample. That is, after the first sample
robot is selected, there are 4 robots to choose from for the second sample
object, three robots for the next sample object selection, and so on until only
two robots are left for the selection of the last and fourth sample object. This
method of sampling, where once an object is selected for a sample it is
removed from sampling contention, is called sampling without replacement.
In order to produce reliable models and analytical results, AI and statistical
studies often need more samples, and samples that are quite different from
each other in order to represent as much of the population as possible. In such
a situation, particularly if the population is small, the measurements of a
sample object are allowed to be duplicated in the sample. This is done by
replacing each robot that is chosen for the sample back in the population so
that its measurements could be potentially included in the next sample
selection. There will always be 5 robots to choose from. This method of
sampling where an object is returned to the population after being selected
for a sample is called sampling with replacement.
In the example of five robots, suppose that during the first sample
selection, robot number 4 is selected. Then, for the selection of the next robot
for a sample of size 4, the Population is {1,2,3,5}. If we continue to sample in
this manner without replacing the robots back in the population, the
population size will decrease by 1 each time. But if we restore the sampled
robots back to the population after each selection, each robot's strength
measurements have an equal chance of being selected during each sample
selection. If possible, the measurements are taken afresh each time the object
is selected for the sample, called repeated measurements, as a way to control
measurement error. We can continue to choose as many robots for as many
samples as needed since the same robot's strength measurements can appear
more than once in the sample.
GeminiBox 4.3.1 shows an example of sampling with replacement. Robot
#3 appears twice in Sample #1, so its strength measurements will be
replicated in that sample. Explore further by using different populations
instead of the number set (1, 2, 3, 4, 5), and different sample sizes.
GeminiBox 4.3.1: Sampling with replacement ⏎
In response to the prompt, “Create three samples of size 4 with
replacement from (1, 2, 3, 4, 5),” Gemini AI-bot created the following
output:
Sample 1: (2, 3, 3, 5)
Sample 2: (1, 1, 2, 4)
Sample 3: (3, 4, 5, 5).
ReferenceBox 4.3.2: Sampling with and without replacement
Sampling without replacement refers to the process where an
observation about an object in the population can be included at most
once in a sample. Once an object is chosen for a sample, it is removed
from sampling contention.
Sampling with replacement refers to the process where an object in the
population can be included multiple times in a sample. Once an object
is chosen for a sample and its information is recorded, it is returned to
the population and is available to be sampled again.
Resampling is the process of creating multiple samples from one
single sample using sampling with replacement. Samples created in
this manner are used for studying variability of the results of statistical
and AI models.
Bootstrapping is a type of resampling that samples with replacement
when there are too few objects in the population.
Figure 4.5 shows 10 samples of size 5 drawn with and without replacement
from the set of numbers 1 through 25. When the samples were created with
replacement, numbers repeat and occur multiple times in a sample, as in
Samples 3 and 6. The samples on the right were created without replacement
so that no number occurs more than once in any given sample. Gemini
computed the mean of each sample as well. The results are shown in Figure
4.5. Here the population consists of the numbers 1 through 25. Notice that
each sample is different. Since the samples are different, the descriptive
statistics computed from the samples are also different.
FIGURE 4.5: Ten samples of size 5 from the set of numbers 1 through 25,
and the corresponding sample means, created using Google Gemini. Left:
With replacement. Right: Without replacement. ⏎

for sampling when sampling with replacement and sampling without

with replacement and sampling without replacement.

GeminiBox 4.3.2: Cluster Sampling with replacement
“Explain cluster sampling with and without replacement.” (cid:0)
The most significant use of sampling with replacement is studying the
variability of the performance of statistical and AI models. Resampling is
used to artificially create multiple samples from an existing sample using
sampling with replacement. Analyses and statistics based on these multiple
samples are used to study possible variation of results across different
sections of the data. Bootstrapping is a particular type of resampling that is
used in machine learning and AI to understand the variability in performance
of AI models. For those interested in pursuing machine learning, Bradley
Efron's original work, The Jackknife, the Bootstrap and Other Resampling
Plans [15] is an excellent introduction to resampling.
4.4 Sampling Variability and Distributions
The variation in the mean from sample to sample is called sampling
variability. This is true of any descriptive statistic, model or visualization
computed from samples, since samples are subsets of the population and each
subset is bound to be different from others. Being able to quantify the
variability, and guarantee that the true population values can be approximated
by the sample-based values is critical. For example, when an autonomous car
is trained on a sample of highways, it is important to be able to guarantee that
it will perform equally well on highways it has not been tested on. Statistics
provides a strong foundation for providing such guarantees. In this section,
we will provide an empirical explanation of sampling variability and
sampling distributions. Chapters 5 and 6 will present a formal probability-
based discussion.
Sampling variability of the sample mean can be studied with EDA
techniques from Chapter 3. For example, compute the mean of the sample
means, check the dispersion of the values of sample means by computing
their standard deviation, and build a histogram to see how the sample mean
values are spread across the number line.
To demonstrate this approach, we created a 100 samples of bill length from
the Palmer penguin data, each sample of size 100, using the R programming
language. We computed the means of each of these 100 samples and plotted a
histogram of the 100 sample means, shown in Figure 4.6, left. The vertical
blue dashed line represents the population mean of all 342 penguins, an
average bill length of 43.8. From Chapter 3, we know that the bars of the
histogram represent the proportion of values, in this case sample means, that
fall in the corresponding bins. According to the histogram, most values of the
sample mean are close to the population mean, and fall in the interval [43,
45].
FIGURE 4.6: Left: Histogram of 100 sample means of size 100 each, is
symmetric around the true population mean of penguin bill length denoted by
the vertical dashed blue line. As the sample size increases, the histogram will
resemble the red bell-shaped curve. Right: Histogram of trimmed sample
means where the smallest 10% of values were dropped from the computation
of the mean. Most of the values are higher than the population mean as
evidenced by the shift of the histogram to the right, away from the true
population mean denoted by the dashed blue line at 43.8. The figure was
created using R. ⏎
If we were to increase the sample size of the means from a 100 to a very
large number, the histogram would eventually resemble the red bell-shaped
curve which, as we will learn in Chapter 5, follows the 68-95-99.7 rule. The
rule states that for any variable whose data values are spread across the
number line in the bell-shaped distribution, 99.7% of the values will fall
within three standard deviations of the mean of the values. In this instance
each value is a sample mean. Therefore, if we compute the mean of the
sample means, i.e., the mean-of-means, and the standard deviation of the
sample means, known as the standard error, we can expect that 99.7% of the

sample means will fall within 3 standard errors of the mean-of-means. The
computations are shown below.

Just as the sample mean has a data distribution associated with it, every
statistic computed from a sample will also have an associated sampling
distribution due to sampling variability. Try the prompt in GeminiBox 4.4.1
to study the sampling distribution of the sample maximum. Explore sampling
distributions of other statistics such as the median and quartiles.

4.4.1 Effect of sample size
In general, if the samples are large, they will tend to resemble each other as
well as the population, resulting in lower variability. This is to be expected
since large samples contain a larger chunk of the population. On the other
hand, the smaller the size of the sample with respect to the population, the
greater the difference between the samples, and the greater the variation in
descriptive statistics computed from the samples. As the sample size
increases, the descriptive statistics in most cases increasingly resemble the
population values, because the sample, as it gets bigger, will increasingly
resemble the population. In fact, descriptive statistics that have this property
are desirable since the goal of using samples is to estimate the population
values as well as possible with less data.
In order to study the effect of sample size on sampling variability, we
conducted the following empirical studies:
For a given sample size, we created 20 samples of bill length from the
Palmer penguin data
–
x
Computed the mean of each of the 20 samples as shown in Equation 4.2
––
X
Computed the mean-of-means of the 20 sample means as shown in
Equation 4.1
s
–Computed the standard deviation of the 20 sample means, also known
x
as standard error, as shown in Equation 4.3
Computed the error bounds shown in Equation 4.4
We repeated the above steps with different sample sizes and created Figure
4.7, left, where the X-axis represents the size of the sample, the blue curve
represents the mean-of-means from Equation 4.1 for the corresponding
sample size n on the X-axis, the red dotted lines represent margin of error
from Equation 4.4, above and below the mean-of-means. The horizontal
purple line represents the true population mean of the bill length of all 342
Palmer penguins.
FIGURE 4.7: Bill length of Palmer penguins. Left: As the sample size of the
sample means increases (X-axis), the average of 20 sample means gets closer
to the true population mean represented by the horizontal purple line, and the
margin of error (represented by the 3-standard error limits (dashed red lines))
decreases. Right: The experiment is repeated, but with only the top 90% of
values in the sample. Such a mean, called a trimmed mean, is a biased
estimate of the population mean and will never approximate the true
population mean (horizontal purple line), no matter how big the sample size.
The figures were created using R code. ⏎
Intuitively, Figure 4.7 shows that as the sample size increases, the mean-
of-means (dark blue line) gets closer to the true population mean. This is to
be expected since larger samples contain a bigger subset of the population
and will resemble the population more. In addition, the variation in the
sample means lessens as the sample size increases, denoted by the narrowing
of the margin of error, the dashed red lines around the mean-of-means. This
implies that for a large enough sample size, we can expect margin of error to
be smaller. Such a descriptive statistic whose value gets consistently closer
and closer to the population value as the sample size increases is said to be an
unbiased estimator of the true population value.
4.4.2 Statistical bias
Next, we repeated the experiment but dropped 10% of the smallest values
from each sample before computing the mean. Such a mean is sometimes
called a trimmed mean since we trimmed the lower 10% of the data from the
sample. The histogram of a 100 trimmed means based on samples of size
100, Figure 4.6, right, shows that most of the sample mean values are higher
than 43.8, the true population mean denoted by the dashed blue line. This is
to be expected since we dropped the smallest 10% of values from each
sample. No matter how big the sample, the histogram will always be shifted
to the right of the true population mean, and never be representative. Figure
4.7, right, shows the changes in the trimmed sample means as the sample size
increases. Compared to the plots in Figure 4.7, left, the average of the
trimmed sample means is consistently higher than the true population mean
represented by the horizontal line, irrespective of the sample size. This is to
be expected since we only choose the top 90% of sample values and ignore
the bottom 10% of sample values. The sample trimmed mean is a biased
estimate of the population mean and will never be representative of the true
population mean no matter how large a sample we choose.
Data Debate 4.4.1 What do you think will happen if we compute the sample
median with only the top 80% of the values in a sample?
ReferenceBox 4.4.1: Sampling variation, error bounds and bias
Sampling variability is the variation in statistics and models from
sample to sample. Each sample contains a different set of objects from
the population, hence any statistic or model computed from a sample
will vary from sample to sample, as well as differ from the population
value.
Sampling distribution is the data distribution of the values of sample
statistic for a fixed sample size, e.g., a histogram, of the values of the
sample statistic over multiple repetitions of samples of the same size.
The most well-known sampling distribution is that of the sample mean
which has the classic bell curve. Figure 4.6, left, shows the sampling
distribution of a 100 sample means, each based on a sample of size
100. A formal probability-based definition of sampling distributions is
presented in Chapters 5 and 6.
Margin of error is the interval of the sampling distribution that is
likely to contain a high proportion of values of the sampling statistic.
The boundaries of this interval are called error bounds. A formal
probability based definition of error bounds and margin of error is
presented in Chapters 5 and 6.
Statistical Bias is the difference between a statistic computed from a
sample and the population value. It is desirable that as the sample size
increases, the descriptive statistics computed from the sample should
be closer to the population values, and the statistical bias of the
descriptive statistic should approach 0.
In Chapters 5 and 6, we will learn the probability-based explanation of the
bell-shaped distribution of sampling means and use it to compute error
bounds and confidence intervals.
4.5 Bias and Ethics of Sampling
Sampling involves choosing subsets. It is possible to choose the subset to
reflect certain desired outcomes. For example, in order to determine the most
popular sport on campus, the soccer captain could just sample everyone in the
team. What would happen to the outcome? Sometimes, the consequences of
bias in sampling could be quite serious, more than just propping up soccer as
the most popular sport on campus. Choosing samples from poor countries to
conduct clinical trials for new drugs leads to under-reporting of side effects,
because the test subjects do not want to lose access to the free medicines.
Once the medicine is released to the public it could have serious side effects
and the medicine could be recalled. Or, a social media or television channel
can show selective content of a certain kind to mold public opinion. When
samples are biased, the AI applications that use the sample data will also
exhibit bias. For instance, an AI algorithm trained only on male resumés will
disregard female-seeming resumés. We will discuss the consequences of
using biased data for training AI in Chapter 10. Here are some examples of
how samples might be biased.
Example 4.5.1. ⏎ Selection Bias: Suppose an experiment was conducted to
find the distribution of sizes of fish in a lake. The net that was used to catch a
representative sample of fish had a mesh size of 1 cm so that fish smaller than
1 cm would not be represented in the sample. This is an example of selection
bias in sampling where only the big fish make it into the sample. Check (cid:0) for
details.
Note that selection bias is due to the way the sample is selected. It is
different from statistical bias which refers to the bias in the descriptive
statistic with respect to the population value. Selection bias would manifest
as statistical bias that cannot be fixed with larger sample sizes.
Example 4.5.2. ⏎ Response bias: There is a food festival on campus and
you want to operate a taco stall to raise funds for your soccer team. You give
the students who buy tacos a little card to fill out with a box to tick against a
(cid:0) or (cid:0). The students who love the tacos don't bother to fill it out; they just
walk off with the food enjoying their snack. But some students complain that
they got too little filling or that the taco was not fresh. Such students do fill
out the card and tick the (cid:0) box. What do you think about the responses you
collected?
This is called response bias since typically unhappy customers respond to
surveys with greater frequency than happy customers.
Example 4.5.3 Unconscious bias: It is your parents' 35th wedding
anniversary and you are organizing a surprise celebration to include your
large extended family. You borrow your sibling's guest list of relatives that
she has made for her wedding planned for later that year. After all, you share
the same grandparents, aunts, uncles, cousins and other extended family.
What you don't realize is that she has dropped your Cousin Kate and her
family from the guest list. You go ahead and use her guest list, and then your
father uses your list for your engagement celebration and so on. Cousin Kate
and her family are now excluded from numerous family celebrations without
your being aware of it or intending to. People fault her for not showing up
(unaware that she wasn't on the guest list to start with) and drop her from all
future family events.
Unconscious bias is more insidious than overt bias because it frequently goes
undetected. It might seem innocuous at face value but accounts for many
unintended yet unfair practices.
ReferenceBox 4.5.1: Bias in samples
Selection bias refers to a bias built into the sample during the sampling
process through judgment or for convenience, and it results in
conclusions that are not representative of the population of interest.
Response bias refers to the bias in responses on surveys,
questionnaires, polls, focus groups and one-on-one interviews, e.g.,
people are more likely to respond when they are dissatisfied rather
than when they are happy, skewing the responses in the negative
direction.
Unconscious bias refers to bias that is not intentional and often goes
undetected for that reason. It could be due to oversight, or even data
quality errors where data records are dropped by mistake and excluded
from the sample. In Chapter 8 we will learn about two famous
economists that unintentionally wreaked havoc on the economies of
some European countries by inadvertently dropping 15 rows of data
from an Excel spreadsheet.
Bias in sampling, training data, selection of experimental subjects, or while
framing questions for responses, can all lead to conclusions that are not
representative of the population of interest. While it is not possible to avoid
all bias, and sometimes it is unconscious, it is important to vet samples and
data as much as possible.

5
Probability in the Age of AI
Uncertainty is a big part of our lives, whether it is the outcome of an election, a football
game, a medical prognosis, or the performance of a stock. Probability is a way of
quantifying the uncertainty. AI-powered applications use probability laws extensively to
quantify uncertainty, make predictions, recommend decisions and provide margins of
error. For example, in order to choose the best route, an AI-powered autonomous car
has to assign probabilities to different routes, to the occurrence of events such as traffic
congestion, accidents and roadwork, take into account the desired time window of
arrival, and finally recommend a route that has the highest chance of meeting the
criteria. This type of decision-making is called probabilistic reasoning and is the
foundation of AI.
This chapter covers the fundamentals of probability distributions, their applications
in AI, and their use in providing confidence guarantees with respect to sampling
variability.
5.1 Uncertainty, probability and AI
Uncertainty arises in many ways. A simple example is a coin toss shown in Figure 5.1 where
there are two equally likely outcomes, heads or tails, but the coin can land only on one of the
two faces. Until the coin lands, the outcome is unknown. AI applications like the self-driving
car shown in Figure 5.1, right, use probabilistic reasoning to integrate knowledge from
different sensors, predict the trajectory of other moving vehicles, and assess risk associated
with environmental factors to choose the best route as described in detail by AI pioneers
Russell and Norvig [37]. The following example gives a brief summary.
FIGURE 5.1: Left: A coin toss has equal chances of two outcomes. Right: A self-driving car
uses probabilities for making complex decisions. ⏎
Example 5.1.1 A self-driving taxi needs to deliver a customer to the airport on time. It can
leave 90 minutes ahead of time, drive within speed limits so as not to get a ticket, and deliver
the customer so that the wait time at the airport is not too long. But the outcome cannot be
guaranteed since there could be circumstances that could get in the way, e.g., accidents,
traffic congestion en route, early flight departure, and even a meteorite strike. The taxi could
leave 180 minutes ahead of time and ensure getting to the airport on time, but that would
most likely entail a long unproductive wait at the airport. So weighing all the probabilities of
uncertain events, including the unlikely event of a meteorite collision, the autonomous taxi
will correctly choose the 90-minute plan. Explore GeminiBox 5.1.1 to learn more about the
use of probabilities by autonomous cars.
Sports betting, fantasy football, and office pools all use probabilities to make predictions
about uncertain outcomes. Sampling variation discussed in Chapter 4 is an example of
uncertainty in how well the sample estimates the true population value.
GeminiBox 5.1.1: Probabilities and Autonomous Cars ⏎
“How is probability used in autonomous cars?” (cid:0)
In this chapter, we will discuss fundamental concepts in probability and focus on their
relationship to AI with real-world examples. Additional detail and further reading include
Introduction to Probability Models by Sheldon M. Ross [36], an excellent and comprehensive
introduction to probability theory and probability distributions.
GeminiBox 5.1.2: Uncertainty and Probability
“What is the connection between uncertainty and probability?” (cid:0)
5.2 Events and Outcomes
Coin tosses, medical prognoses, and stock movements are examples of events that could have
one or more outcomes, e.g., heads-tails, recover-remission-terminal, up-down-steady. Some
events have fixed or predetermined outcomes, e.g., an object, when dropped will fall to the
ground on our planet. For other events, the outcomes are uncertain.
The chance of correctly guessing an outcome is related to the uncertainty of an event. The
greater the uncertainty, the lower the chance of a correct guess and vice versa. For
deterministic events, uncertainty is near 0, and the likelihood of guessing the correct outcome
is close to 1. In the coin toss example, heads and tails are equally likely, guessing the correct
outcome has only a 50% chance of success. On the other hand, for a common cold, the
likelihood of recovery is close to 99% so the chance of correctly guessing the outcome is
high.
The uncertainty of the outcomes of an event is quantified through a probability distribution
that captures the relative chances of the occurrence of each outcome. Probability distributions
can range from simple, e.g., 50-50 for a fair coin toss, to complex mathematical functions like
the Normal, or empirically constructed algorithms like neural networks. They are used
extensively in AI applications, for example, in assisting robots to perform tasks such as
cleaning and reconnaissance, as shown in Figure 5.2.
FIGURE 5.2: Robots use probability distributions to recognize objects and navigate
surroundings. Left: Heathrow Airport has eight ultraviolet-disinfecting (UVD) robots that
roam the terminals to clean and disinfect (Mx. Granger, Wikimedia Commons). Right: A
Vision 60 Q-UGV ground robot does a simulated route mission at Holloman Air Force Base,
New Mexico, April 17, 2023 (U.S. Air Force photo by Airman 1st Class Isaiah Pedrazzini,
Wikimedia Commons). ⏎
ReferenceBox 5.2.1: Probability Terminology ⏎
An event E is any activity or phenomenon whose outcome is of interest, e.g., a coin
toss, a sporting event or election, movement of a stock, or getting a job after
graduation.
A Sample Space, S, is the set of all outcomes of an event E. For example, the sample
S = {H,T}
space of a coin toss since there are only two outcomes, heads (H) or tails
(T),
1. The outcomes in S are mutually exclusive, i.e., non-overlapping. The coin has to
land either on heads or tails.
2. The set of outcomes in S is exhaustive to include every possible outcome.
3. Ideally, the sample space must not include irrelevant or impossible outcomes. For
example, the outcome “6” is relevant for the roll of a dice but not for a coin toss.
An event E is deterministic if its outcome is known in advance, or known with near
certainty. For example, the sun rises every morning, a ball falls down toward the
ground on our planet when dropped, and a human will die without oxygen.
An event E is probabilistic if its outcome cannot be predicted with a high degree of
certainty.
Events can have multiple outcomes associated with them, e.g., rolling more than a “4” on a
single roll of dice. Can you determine the sample space of the roll of dice and outcomes that
support the event above?
Data Debate 5.2.1 Equally likely outcomes: What is an example of an event that has equally
likely outcomes? If you wanted to place a bet on a sporting event, would you prefer equally
likely outcomes? Why or why not? In what situation would you prefer an event with equally
likely outcomes?
To understand the difference between deterministic and probabilistic events with examples,
explore GeminiBox 5.2.1.

5.3 Probability Distributions
Suppose that it is summer and that you are a frequent visitor to that ice cream parlor we
mentioned earlier. On one visit, you might choose chocolate; on another, you might choose
strawberry. However, you are more likely to choose some flavors over others. You might
choose chocolate on 80 visits, strawberry on 15, and mocha on 5. Can you figure out the
flavor you are most likely to pick? The one you are least likely to pick?
5.3.1 Random variables
Your choice of ice cream flavor is a random variable whose value varies from visit to visit.
However, you can associate probabilities with your flavor choices by examining the history of
your choices during your past visits to the ice cream parlor. The probabilities of outcomes of a
random variable can also be governed by mathematical functions.
ReferenceBox 5.3.1: Random Variables and Probabilities
A random variable X represents a variable whose value is uncertain and is determined
by the outcome of a probabilistic event, e.g., choice of a flavor of ice cream.
The domain D of a random variable is the set of all possible outcomes. D is said to be
exhaustive since it includes all possible values of X. The domain is related to the
sample space S discussed in ReferenceBox 5.2.1. If X is associated with a single event
E, then D and S are the same.
The values of X and the associated probability p of occurrence of the outcomes that
result in that value of X together constitute the probability distribution of X.
Probabilities range between 0 and 1 since they represent the chances of a particular
outcome. A value of 0 means that an outcome will never occur, while a value of 1
implies that an outcome is certain.
The sum of probabilities associated with all the possible values of the random variable
X must sum to 1, since D is exhaustive and includes every possible outcome.
The probability that X takes a particular value can be determined from observed data.
For example, the probability that you will pick chocolate ice cream is 0.8. Such a
probability distribution based on empirical observation is called an empirical
probability distribution.
In some instances, if the nature of the process that determines the outcome is well
known, e.g., that of a fair coin toss, the probability can be expressed theoretically by a
mathematical function without the need for empirical observation.
Data Debate 5.3.1 If X is the random variable corresponding to your friend B's flavor
choice, what is the domain D of X? Is it the same as the sample space S?
5.3.2 Probabilities of random variables
The values of a random variable X and the probability of occurrence of those values together
are called a probability distribution of X. If X is a continuous variable, it is called a probability
function. For example, the probability distribution of the random variable X that represents
your choice of ice cream flavors (mentioned at the beginning of this section) is:
P(X = Chocolate) = 80/100 = 0.8
(5.3)
P(X = Strawberry) = 15/100 = 0.15
(5.4)
P(X = Mocha) = 5/100 = 0.05
(5.5)
P(X = OtherFlavors) = 0
(5.6)
Other Flavors (OF) represents flavors other than strawberry (S), chocolate (CC) and Mocha
(M). Such an estimate computed from data gathered through observation or experimentation
is called an empirical probability distribution. Here are some interesting properties of
probabilities:
If an outcome is certain, then the probability of that outcome for that event is 1. That event
is deterministic and not random.
If there is no chance of a particular outcome occurring, e.g., going home without buying
ice cream, then the probability of that outcome is 0, the smallest value a probability can
take.
The sum of probabilities of all the values in the domain of a random variable X, e.g.,
choice of ice cream flavors, is 1. This is because the domain of X must by definition
include every possible outcome and therefore every value of X.
Data Debate 5.3.2 Is it meaningful for a probability to be negative? Why or why not?
ReferenceBox 5.3.2: Types of Probability Distributions
The probability distribution of a random variable X is the collection of probabilities of
occurrence of its values. It is denoted by:
P(X).
Equation 5.3 represents the probability distribution of your choice of ice cream flavors.
Probability distributions and their estimation are key to data science and AI.
The joint probability distribution of two random variables X and Y specifies the
chances of the co-occurrence of the set of all possible joint outcome values of X and Y.
It is denoted by:
P(X,Y).
For example, Figure 5.5 shows the joint probabilities (in black) of ice cream choices of
you and your friend, inside the cells of the table. Section 5.3.3 describes joint
probability distributions in detail.
The marginal probability distribution of a random variable Y is simply its probability
distribution irrespective of the values of other random variables, e.g., your flavor
choice irrespective of what your friend chooses. It is described in Section 5.3.4,
denoted in red in Figure 5.5.
The conditional probability distribution of a random variable Y is its probability
distribution given a specific value of another random variable. For example,
probabilities of your friend's flavor choice, given that you chose CC, is shown in
Figure 5.5, first row, in green.

chose mint (Y=Mint).

5.3.4 Marginal probabilities
The probability distribution of a random variable Y, irrespective of that of other random
variables, is called the marginal distribution of Y. When we refer to the probability
distribution of a random variable, it is by default its marginal distribution since we ignore the
probability distribution of its co-occurrence with other random variables. The bottom row of
red probabilities in Figure 5.5 represents the marginal probability distribution of Y, your
friend's ice cream flavor choice, and the last column of red probabilities represents the
marginal probability distribution of X, your ice cream flavor choices.
5.3.5 Conditional probabilities
Is your friend's ice cream flavor choice affected by yours? This is an important question in
marketing where taste-makers and influencers are hired to pump up sales. Put another way,
what flavor would your friend choose given that you chose chocolate chip? This is called a
conditional probability distribution because it refers to your friend's choice conditional upon
your choice. It is possible that your friend would choose a different flavor so that the two of
you can swap halfway through and enjoy both flavors.
In the instance shown in Figure 5.5, you chose chocolate on 80 visits (marginal distribution
of 0.8 in red), and your friend chose chocolate on 10 of those 80 visits (joint distribution of
CC and CC in black) when you chose chocolate. Therefore, the probability that your friend
0.1/0.8 = 0.125
will choose chocolate given you chose chocolate is shown in green. See
Appendix 5.9.2 for the full conditional distribution of your friend's choice Y given you chose
CC.
Conditional probability of “Y given X” can be written in general as:
P(Y,X) P(X ∩Y)

Conditional probabilities are used extensively in AI, e.g., by autonomous vehicles, by lunar
landers like the Blue Ghost described in Figure 5.7, and even for predicting dolphin
communications as shown in Example 5.3.2.

5.3.6 Bayes' rule
The conditional and marginal probability distributions of random variables are related by a
famous rule called Bayes' Rule:
P(X|Y)P(Y)

[P(X ∩Y)/P(Y)]P(Y) P(X ∩Y)

P(Y)

Bayes' Rule plays a big role in machine learning and in updating hypotheses when new
information becomes available. For example, if we know that your friend chose the flavor
OF, we can make a more accurate guess as to what you had chosen. Using Equation 5.10 and
the data in Section 5.3.3, the probability distribution of your flavor choice given your friend's
choice is given by:
P(X = CC|Y = OF) = 0.5/0.55
= 0.91
P(X = S|Y = OF) = 0.05/0.55
= 0.09
Note that the fact that your friend chose the flavor OF implies an increased likelihood of 0.91
that you had chosen chocolate compared to the marginal probability of 0.8 described in
Section 5.3.2. The updated probabilities of your choice of ice cream, after your friend's choice
is made, are shown in Figure 5.5, highlighted in yellow. They are called posterior
probabilities since they reflect the updated probability after a particular event has occurred.
For example, given that 90% of the traffic is flowing through a side street as opposed to the
usual 10%, what is the posterior probability that an accident occurred on the highway? Would
it be higher or lower than if you had not had the information about the 9-fold increase in
traffic along the side street?

Example 5.3.3 Robots and conditional probabilities in AI: Robots use conditional
probabilities to estimate their location, to navigate around objects, and to choose actions
based on the most current sensor reading. See Figure 5.2 for two great examples. Robots are
used to clean airports, to load and unload objects, for reconnaissance purposes, and even to
deliver food. Read the article Robots in Everyday Life in Section 5.6.
The game show Let's Make a Deal originally hosted by its creator Monty Hall is an
excellent illustration of Bayes' Theorem. Contestants have to choose one of three closed
doors, one of which conceals a car and the other two conceal goats. A contestant chooses a
door, and Monty opens another door. If that reveals a goat, the contestant is given the option
of sticking with the original choice or switching doors. What do you think would be a good
strategy? Use GeminiBox 5.3.3 to explore.
GeminiBox 5.3.3: Monty Hall and Bayes' Theorem ⏎
“Bayes' rule three-door problem.” (cid:0)
Bayes' rule is widely applied in AI, particularly in Machine Learning. A popular algorithm
that uses Bayes' rule is called the Naive Bayes Classifier. It is trained on data with labels and
associated attributes, and creates patterns of attributes that co-occur with label values with a
high probability. When new information becomes available, the attribute patterns are used to
predict the labels. This is known as supervised learning since the labels are provided to train
the model.
5.3.7 Independent events
Dependence between random variables can be quantified. Intuitively, if two random variables
P(Y|X)
X and Y are completely independent, we would expect the conditional probability of
P(Y)
to be the same as the marginal distribution of because we would expect the values of X
to have no influence on the values of Y. For example, the number of letters in a person's first
name and their ice cream flavor choice. Which means that:
P(Y) = P(Y|X)
P(Y ∩X)

P(Y ∩X) = P(X)P(Y)

P(Y ∩X) P(X ∩Y)
is the same as ,
P(X ∩Y) = P(X)P(Y).

This is the multiplicative rule of probabilities of independent events. Equation 5.11 provides a

5.4 Common Probability Distributions
There are several ways to define a probability distribution. Two common ways are through
empirical observation and through compact mathematical functions. Explore the prompt in
GeminiBox 5.4.1 to learn more.
GeminiBox 5.4.1: Probability Distributions ⏎
“What are the different ways of determining a probability distribution?” (cid:0)
5.4.1 Empirical distributions: learning from data
Empirical probability distributions are based entirely on observed data rather than pre-defined
theoretical formulations. Section 5.3.2 is a good example of an empirical frequency
distribution of ice cream flavors based on the observation of 100 visits to the ice cream parlor.
This approach is essential because, in many real-life scenarios, the distribution of a random
variable is unknown. In Chapter 3, we learned about frequency distributions, counts,
proportions, bar charts (Section 3.8.2) and histograms (Section 3.8.3). These are the
foundations of empirical probability distributions. Figure 5.5 is another example of an
empirical distribution.

5.4.2 Probability distributions and AI
AI relies heavily on probability theory and distributions. Empirical distributions are used
extensively by AI applications. Probability distributions underlying real-world phenomena are
often unknown or too complex to capture accurately with predefined mathematical models.
Some examples of the ways in which AI uses empirical distributions are:
Neural networks use empirical distributions learned entirely from data to approximate
complex probabilistic distributions of hundreds of attributes. For example, neural networks
can remove noise from an image as shown in Figure 5.6 by learning the noise distribution,
and use that knowledge to de-noise images. The figure in the middle is the noisy image
with a grainy background. After de-noising, the background is no longer grainy, but the
spotted texture on the bird, e.g., its wings and head has been smoothed out as well when
compared to the orginal image on the left.
AI language models such as Natural Language Processing (NLP) models are trained to
learn the empirical probability distribution of sequences of words from data such as articles
and books to mimic human language. We will discuss NLPs briefly in Chapter 9. Large
language models (LLMs) that are behind the current explosion in the use and integration of
AI into work productivity tools are powered by empirical probability distributions
computed from vast corpuses of text.
AI uses empirical distributions to create baseline distributions and identify low probability
events with respect to this distribution as anomalies. For example, the interconnectedness
of sensors in power grids is modeled using empirical distributions and low probability
occurrences, e.g., power surges and outages are detected as departures from the normal
baseline patterns of the power grid. Read an MIT News article, Using artificial intelligence
to find anomalies hiding in massive datasets: pinpointing potential power grid failure on
this topic in Section 5.6.
FIGURE 5.6: Empirical distributions are used by neural nets to learn noise distributions to
de-noise images. Photo credit: Kriomet, CC0, via Wikimedia Commons. ⏎
FIGURE 5.7: Blue Ghost, a lunar lander from FireFly and NASA used sensors to stick its
autonomous moon landing on the first attempt without toppling over. Right: Binomial
probabilities of hypothetical sensors failing. ⏎
5.4.3 Theoretical probability distributions
Random variables can be discrete or continuous. Probability distributions too are discrete or
continuous depending on the nature of the random variable they represent. Mathematical
equations are often used to express probability distributions and functions associated with
random variables. Such theoretical formulations are compact, and when applicable, enable the
derivation of important properties of the distribution without the help of massive amounts of
data and computation. Prior to the increase in computing power, theoretical results were the
mainstay of probabilistic approaches. We will review one discrete (Binomial) and one
continuous probability distribution (Normal) as examples. Introduction to Probability Models
by Sheldon M. Ross [36] has a comprehensive discussion of probability distributions.
5.4.4 Illustrative example: Blue Ghost and the Binomial distribution
Recently, a lunar lander called Blue Ghost made a perfect autonomous landing on the moon in
its first attempt, a rarity, and collected valuable data for research. A rendering of the lander is
shown in Figure 5.7. In order to do so, Blue Ghost had to use many sensors to gather data
about the landing surface and use AI to find a proper spot and trajectory so as not to topple
over and jeopardize its mission.
The Blue Ghost had four sensors on each of its footpads but ignored the data from one of
them and still landed successfully. Suppose, hypothetically, that a three-legged lander needs at
least two of its three sensors to successfully function in order to avoid smashing into the
surface of the moon. Each sensor functions independently of the other and has a 10% chance
of failing at any given time.
The probability distribution of the number of functioning sensors is shown in Figure 5.7,
right. Sensors in red denote malfunctioning sensors, while those in green are functional. The
first row represents the probability of all three sensors failing (red) independently of each
other, i.e., 0.1 x 0.1 x 0.1= 0.001 using Equation 5.11 of probabilities of independent events.
The next three rows represent the probability of exactly two sensors failing, given by 0.1 x
0.1 x 0.9=0.009 for each row. There are three such scenarios, since sensor pairs (1,2) or (1, 3)
or (2, 3) could fail, leading to exactly two sensors failing. (The number of combinatorial
(3)
scenarios of choosing exactly two from three is given by .) Therefore, the probability of
2
exactly two sensors failing is:
3
( )∗0.009 = 3∗0.009 = 0.027.
2
(3)
The next three rows represent the probability of exactly one sensor failing ( ), and
1
(3)
finally, the last row represents the probability of none of the sensors failing (all green) ( ).
0
The lander will be successful as long as none of the sensors fail (probability = 0.729), or
exactly one sensor fails (probability=0.243). Therefore, the probability that the lander will
succeed is 0.729+0.243=0.972, using Equation 5.2 and noting that “exactly one sensor” and
“none of the sensors” are mutually exclusive events, and the probability of their intersection is
0.
Lunar landers (and autonomous cars) have hundreds of sensors, and it is important to
compute and monitor the probability that there are no mission critical failures. The Binomial
distribution is widely used in such circumstances.
5.4.5 Binomial distribution
An event that has only two outcomes is called a binary event, e.g., a coin toss. The probability
distribution of a random variable with binary outcomes, say 0 or 1, is given by:
P(X = 0) = p
(5.12)
P(X = 1) = q
(5.13)
= 1−p
(5.14)
Since there are only two outcomes, one of which must occur, the sum of the probabilities of
the two outcomes is 1. That is:
P(X = 0)+P(X = 1) = 1 ⟹ q = 1−p.
In general, a random variable X which represents the number of outcomes of type 0 (or 1)
of n independent binary outcomes, all with the same probability, can be represented by a
Binomial distribution. The probability of k of n outcomes being 0, where each has an
independent probability p of being 0, is given by:
n
P(X = k) = ( )pk(1−p)n−k
k
(5.15)
where X is the random variable that represents the number of 0's.
In the above hypothetical example of Blue Ghost, 0 represented the outcome that a sensor
was functioning (not failing), and the Binomial probabilities were computed for various
numbers of the three sensors failing.
The Binomial distribution is critical to many AI applications where independent binary
inputs of numerous AI-agents are combined by complex probabilistic reasoning models to
predict the next state of a system, and recommend an action, e.g., autonomous cars. The
distribution has a nice symmetric shape when the two outcomes are equally likely as in a coin
toss. Figure 5.8, left, generated using the prompt in GeminiBox 5.4.2, shows the distribution
of the number of heads in 10 coin tosses where the probability of heads=1/2 for any given
toss.

5.4.6 Illustrative example: Hurricanes and the Normal distribution
Hurricane forecasts are some of the most useful statistical data. They are closely watched to
protect human lives by issuing evacuation orders, and to contain damage to property through
preventative measures, e.g., deploying storm shutters and sandbags. Figure 5.9, left, created
by Nilfanion, shows storm tracks superimposed over a NASA earth image. Figure 5.9, right,
shows the projected path of Hurricane Earl. The white bubble represents the uncertainty in the
hurricane trajectory factoring in a complex set of dynamic conditions like vertical wind shear,
humidity and other complex factors. Read the article Determining uncertainty: a review of
hurricane intensity predictability published by NOAA in Section 5.6.
FIGURE 5.9: Left, tracks of all Atlantic hurricanes which formed between 1851 and 2019.
The points show the locations of the storms at six-hourly intervals. (Image credit: Nilfanion).
Normal distributions are used to model hurricane tracks and to predict potential future
trajectories (right) shown by the white area around the storm track. (NOAA.) ⏎
Continuous probability distributions are used extensively to estimate uncertainty in storm
trajectories and in many other situations that involve quantifying uncertainty. While there are
many continuous probability distributions, the Normal or Gaussian distribution is one of the
most widely used.
