5.5 Margin of Error and Confidence Intervals
The Normal distribution is the rock star of probability distributions because it allows us to
provide margins of error in a variety of applications from descriptive statistics to deep
learning AI models. Chapter 4 discussed statistical bias, the difference between a statistic (or
models) estimated from the sample and the true population value, and the standard error in the
estimation. In this section we will discuss confidence intervals, which are based on the
probability distribution of the sample statistic.
Error bounds are used extensively in AI. For example, FrontFinder AI, an AI tool, detects
cold, warm, stationary and occluded weather fronts across the continental United States and
provides error bounds in the form of confidence intervals. Other AI models like deep learning
networks use confidence intervals for model selection, model accuracy, model performance
and generalization. Such bounds are grounded in probability theory and Normal distributions.
The process of using samples to estimate the population mean and specifying the margin of
error in the form of confidence intervals using the Normal distribution is outlined in Figure
5.11.
Step 1: A population consists of many instances; we cannot measure each one and compute
the population mean, standard deviation or other descriptive statistics. We typically rely on
samples from the population.
Step 2: We learned in Chapter 4 that each sample is different, and hence descriptive
statistics vary from sample to sample. This is called sampling variation. Figure 5.11
demonstrates sampling variation shown in Samples S1 through S5, and sample means T1
through T5.
Step 3: If we choose many such samples, the sample means are distributed across a range
of values. The distribution of the values of a sampling statistic is called its sampling
distribution. Sample averages of any random variable, e.g., wind speed, humidity, weight,
IQ, home prices, all follow the Normal probability distribution, no matter what the
underlying distribution of the random variable. This is important because that means we
can always use the Normal distribution as the sampling distribution of the sample mean no
matter what continuous random variable we are measuring! This is formalized by the
Central Limit Theorem.
FIGURE 5.11: Normal distribution is used to estimate the error in using a sample to
estimate population characteristics in the form of confidence intervals. Step 1: Sample
from the population to estimate population characteristics without having to use all the
populations data. Step 2: Each sample is different and results in different values of
descriptive statistics, e.g., sample mean. Step 3: Convert the statistics to their standard
values known as Z-scores by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation
(SD). Step 4: The distribution of Z-scores (red lines) is the sampling distribution of the
sample statistic. For the sample mean, because of the Central Limit Theorem, the sampling
distribution is a Normal distribution. Step 5: Use the properties of the Normal distribution
to find the confidence intervals that enclose a specified proportion of the Z-scores. The
confidence intervals quantify the inaccuracy in approximating the population mean using
the sample mean. ⏎
X
Definition 5.5.1 The Central Limit Theorem (CLT) states that the sample average of n
independent values, sampled randomly from the same population, has a Normal
σ2/n
distribution for large n, with mean μ and variance where μ is the population mean,
σ2
and is the population variance, irrespective of the distribution of the population values.

is the standard error in approximating the population with the sample mean.

distribution with mean μ and standard deviation σ, the quantity

called a Z-score, is distributed as a Standard Normal distribution with mean 0 and standard

The sample means T1 through T5 in Figure 5.11 are “standardized” to Z-scores Z1 through
Z5. These Z-scores are shown as red ticks on the sampling distribution of the sample mean.
Since the sampling distribution is the Normal distribution (bell-curve), 68% of Z-scores
[−1,1] [−2,2] [−3,3]

Step 5: The fact that the sample mean's Z-scores are distributed according to the Normal
(bell) curve means that the probability that the interval
[X −3σ/√n,X +3σ/√n]
encloses μ is 99%. This interval is called a 99% confidence interval. That is, if 100
intervals are computed from a 100 samples, then 99% of them will contain the true value
of the population mean, μ. (Additional properties of the Normal distribution are described
in ReferenceBox 5.4.1.)
ReferenceBox 5.5.1: Sampling Variation and Margin of Error
(Figure 5.11)
Sampling variation occurs because each sample is chosen to contain only a subset of
the population values, and each subset is different. Therefore, descriptive statistics,
e.g., the sample mean, computed from the samples will also vary from sample to
sample.
The sampling distribution of the sample mean captures the distribution of the sample
mean values across different samples. It is a probability distribution that quantifies the
uncertainty in the value of the sample mean caused due to sampling variation.
X
The Central Limit Theorem (CLT) states that the sample mean of n independent
values, sampled randomly from the same population, is a random variable with a
σ2/n
Normal distribution for large n, with mean μ and variance where μ is the
population mean, and σ2 is the population variance, irrespective of the distribution of
the population values.
A Z-score refers to a standardized value of a random variable by subtracting its mean
and dividing by its standard deviation. Standardization creates a unit-free measurement
that makes different variables comparable. For example, atmospheric pressure,
temperature, wind speed when standardized are free of units of measurement like
inches of mercury (Hg), Fahrenheit, meters/second, etc. and can be used in
multivariate analytics like clustering. The Z-score of a random variable X is given by
X −μ
Z = ,
σ
where μ is the population mean of X and σ its standard deviation.
Since, according to the CLT, the sampling distribution of the sample mean is a Normal
distribution, then its Z-score,
X −mean(X) X −μ
Z = = ,
SD(X) σ/√n
is a Standard Normal distribution, and has a bell-shaped probability distribution like
Figure 5.10.
The bell shape of the Standard Normal distribution implies that the probability that the
sample mean falls in certain ranges called confidence intervals can be theoretically
determined by the area under the Normal curve.
The upper and lower bounds of the confidence are called error bounds and quantify the
inaccuracy, or the margin of error, in estimating the population mean by the sample
mean.
5.5.1 More on confidence intervals
The US Census Bureau has a succinct explanation of confidence intervals. Check A Basic
explanation of Confidence Intervals in Section 5.6. Here are some salient observations:
[X −3σ/√n,X +3σ/√n]

Note 2: The acceptable uncertainty or inaccuracy in the estimation of the population mean by
the sample mean is quantified by the probability limit

known as level of significance. These are often prespecified by the applications. In quality
control in manufacturing, the error tolerance for estimating the average reliability of a part
could be as low as 0.000001.
Note 3: The upper and lower limits of the confidence interval are called error bounds or
tolerance limits since they represent the amount of error tolerated in the estimation of the
population mean.

5.9 Appendix

5.9.1 Sets of outcomes
Simple concepts from set theory provide a useful way of thinking about outcomes and
probabilities. ReferenceBox 5.9.1 provides definitions of commonly used terms in Set theory.
Herbert B. Enderton's Elements of Set Theory [16] is a great introduction. The definitions
presented here have many variations, e.g., when applicable to more than two sets, but the
discussion here is limited to concepts that are useful for explaining probability theory.
ReferenceBox 5.9.1: Set Theory Terminology ⏎
A set A is a collection of distinct objects, called its elements.
|A|
The cardinality of a set A is the number of elements in the set. It is 0 for an empty
set with no elements.
The complement A of a set A consists of all the elements in the sample space S that are
NOT in the set A.
A∩B
The intersection of two sets A and B, denoted by , consists of all the distinct
elements that are present in both A AND B.
A∪B
The union of two sets A, B denoted by , consists of all the distinct elements that
are in A OR in B, i.e., present in at least one of the sets.
A ⊂ B
A set A is a (proper) subset of another set B, denoted by , if all its elements are
present in B.
Mutually exclusive sets have no element in common. That is, the intersection of two
mutually exclusive sets is the empty set ϕ.
As a quick example to illustrate set theory terms, consider the following set of ice cream
flavors S ={Chocolate, FroYo, Honey, Mango, Matcha, Mint, Rocky Road, Strawberry,
Vanilla, Vegan}, and its subsets:
A = {Chocolate, Mango, Matcha, Mint, Strawberry, Vanilla}
B = {Chocolate, Honey, Rocky road, Strawberry, Vanilla}
C = {FroYo, Honey}
D = {Rocky road}
E = {Vegan}.

(B,E). For example, the intersection of the two sets has no elements and is empty, A ∩ D = ϕ
= {}, where ϕ is the Greek symbol traditionally used to represent an empty set.

5.9.2 Conditional probability distribution of
Computing marginal probabilities from joint point probabilities: The individual
probabilities of two random variables X and Y can be computed from the joint probabilities
of X and Y. For example, the individual probabilities of flavor choices for your friend Y can
be computed from Section 5.3.3 in the following manner:
P(Y = OF) = P(X = CC & Y = OF)+P(X = S & Y = OF)
= 0.5+0.05
= 0.55
P(Y = S) = P(X = S & Y = S)
= 0.1
P(Y = CC) = P(X = CC & Y = CC)
= 0.1
P(Y = Mint) = P(X = CC & Y = Mint)+P(X = Mocha & Y = Mint)
= 0.2+0.05
= 0.25
Sanity check: All probabilities lie in the range [0,1] and sum to 1.
Computing marginal probabilities from conditional probabilities: Suppose we want to
know how often your friend chooses Mint. We simply sum his choice of Mint given all
your possible flavor choices. From Figure 5.8, we know that he chooses Mint 0.2
proportion of the times you choose CC, which is 0.8 times in total; therefore, he chose
Mint 0.2/0.8=0.25 of the times you chose CC. Similarly, he chooses Mint 0.05 proportion
of the times you choose Mocha, which is 0.05 of the times in total; therefore 0.05/0.05=1,
i.e., he chose Mint every time you chose Mocha. These are the only two occasions when he
chooses Mint. Therefore,
P(Y = Mint) = P(Y = Mint|X = CC)P(X = CC)
+P(Y = Mint|X = Mocha)P(X = Mocha)
= 0.25∗0.8+1∗0.05
= 0.25
given your flavor choices. This logic can be stated formally for any two random variables
X and Y as:
P(Y) = P(Y|X)P(X)
(5.19)

friend also chooses it 10% of the time. Similarly, you both choose Strawberry
simultaneously 10% of the time out of the 15% of the time you choose Strawberry. This
can be stated as:

6
Data-Driven Hypotheses Testing
Is it better to exercise in the morning or at night? Which is more
popular, coffee or tea? Does an AI algorithm favor one group over
another? In order to provide objective and data-driven answers to such
questions, we collect samples for gathering data, formulate and test
hypotheses to draw conclusions within an acceptable probability of
error. This process is called statistical hypothesis testing.
Social media companies extensively use hypothesis testing in the
form of A/B testing to increase user engagement by comparing ease-of-
use and attractiveness of icon designs and placement. NASA relies on
hypothesis testing to distinguish between flight trajectories of
suspicious drones and those of birds. Self-driving cars recognize
moving objects like bicycles, humans and cars by testing them against
statistical profiles based on known features. AI models and algorithms
are audited for fairness and bias using hypothesis testing. In this
chapter, we will learn about statistical hypothesis testing and its
applications in AI.
6.1 Automation and Hypothesis Testing
Companies increasingly rely on AI to screen job applicants, determine
parole eligibility for the incarcerated, and to establish compatibility on
dating apps. They use statistical hypothesis testing to perform sanity checks
called AI system audits, e.g., to ensure that female applicants are not
excluded while AI algorithms screen resumès for hiring, or compliance with
regulatory requirements. Social media companies use hypothesis testing to
compare different designs and products in order to find the best way to keep
users engaged on their platforms. Statistical hypothesis testing provides an
objective way to answer questions, compare groups, and derive conclusions
and make inferences based on sampled data. The framework provides
guarantees on the validity of results through margins of error and
probability that the conclusions are correct. AI applications use testing-
based reasoning extensively, e.g., to decide whether feature A or feature B
has better user engagement. In this chapter, we will provide an overview of
statistical hypothesis testing and its applications, and highlight its relevance
to AI. Our objective is to cover the necessary concepts and provide Gemini
prompts, additional material in the Appendix, and further references to help
those interested in deeper study. A comprehensive and rigorous discussion
of this topic can be found in Mathematical Statistics: Basic Ideas and
Selected Topics, Volumes I-II by Bickel and Doksum [2], and Statistical
Inference by Casella and Berger [7].
FIGURE 6.1: Left: AI is often used to screen resumès during the hiring
process. Does it favor one group over another? Right: Are puppies adopted
at higher rates than other animals at rescue centers? Statistical hypothesis
testing provides data-driven answers to such questions.
6.1.1 Process of hypothesis testing: Key steps
Suppose an animal rescue organization wants to check if there is a
preference towards adopting puppies over other animals at their rescue
centers across the country. Since it is expensive and time-consuming to
collect data from every rescue center, they would choose a few
representative centers across the country and gather data over a period of
time regarding animals available for adoption, and check if the proportion
of puppies that were adopted during that period was “similar” to the
proportion of all animals adopted.
However, what constitutes “similar” or “different”? Are adoption rates of
0.4 (puppies) and 0.39999 (all animals) different? How about 0.4 and
0.389? Where would one draw the line? The two proportions will never be
identical due to sampling variation and other chance happenings. If an
influencer announces “Adopt a Peacock” day at the time the samples are
collected, there could potentially be a one-time spike in peacock adoptions
that could produce misleading results. The process of statistical hypothesis
testing is an objective, data-driven way to determine whether the two
proportions are significantly different (or not) after accounting for the noise
of such chance variability.
Figure 6.2 shows a simple process diagram of hypothesis testing in the
context of the puppy adoption question. ReferenceBox 6.1.1 lists the key
steps, and ReferenceBox 6.1.2 provides definitions of key terms and
concepts. Section 6.2.3 shows a step-by-step worked-out illustrative
example of hypothesis testing. Please refer to these as you read through the
chapter.
Extended Description
FIGURE 6.2: Are puppies favored for adoption over other animals at
animal rescue centers? The process of statistical hypothesis testing is
designed to answer such questions in an objective, data-driven manner. ⏎
ReferenceBox 6.1.1: Key Steps in Hypothesis Testing ⏎
1. Formulate null hypothesis based on question to be answered. State
the alternative hypothesis.
2. Collect samples and gather relevant data.
3. Compute a suitable descriptive statistic from the sample.
4. Choose an appropriate sampling distribution of the descriptive
statistic and specify level of significance.
5. Test if the sample statistic is consistent with the sampling
distribution dictated by the null hypothesis, i.e., is it a typical value
or an unlikely, low-probability value?
6. State conclusion and answer question.
We describe below the key steps mentioned in ReferenceBox 6.1.1 and
illustrated in Figure 6.2. The terminology and definitions are explained in
ReferenceBox 6.1.2 and should be thoroughly studied. The key steps in
hypothesis testing are:
1. Formulate null and alternative hypotheses: Based on the question
that needs to be answered, compose a hypothesis that you wish to
confirm or contradict, e.g., “Puppies are adopted at the same rate as
other animals at the animal rescue center.” By default, in the absence of
any prior evidence, we assume the status quo, or null hypothesis, that
there is no difference, and we use the sampled data to either confirm or
contradict this hypothesis.
An alternative hypothesis is a specific statement that holds if the null
hypothesis is not true. “Puppies are adopted at a different rate than other
animals at rescue centers” is an example of an alternative hypothesis.
The bubble next to Step 1 in Figure 6.2 shows two other examples of
alternate hypotheses, of higher and lower rates of puppy adoptions. It is
important to state the alternative hypothesis before collecting data so
that it is not influenced by what we observe in the data.
2. Select a sample and gather data: Collect data relevant to the problem.
For example, sample animal rescue centers across the country, and at
each center count: the number of animals available for adoption during
the past month, number of puppies available for adoption during the past
month, number of animals adopted in the past month, and number of
puppies adopted in the past month. It can be any duration, but applied
consistently across all data that is collected. Choose the most suitable
sampling technique to get reliable results.
Make sure that the data collected is relevant to the problem. There
is no point in collecting data about tigers in jungles or migrating
wildebeest in the Serengeti, Figure 6.3, however tempting.
In addition, ensure that the sampling process provides
representative data of animal rescue centers across the country, and
on different days to mitigate the effect of abnormal data such as
“Adopt a Peacock” day sponsored by an influencer. What would
happen if you were to collect data only on “Adopt a Peacock” day?
FIGURE 6.3: Data relevance: To answer questions about adoption rates
of puppies at rescue centers, would it make sense to collect data about:
Tigers in jungles? Wildebeest in the Serengeti? What would happen if
you were to gather samples at rescue centers only on “Adopt a Peacock”
day? Photo credits: Tiger, Wildebeest and Peacock, courtesy Unsplash.
⏎
Data Debate 6.1.1 What type of sampling method should we use to
collect samples to test if puppy adoption rates are the same as those of
all animals at rescue centers?
3. Compute a descriptive statistic from the sample: Determine the
T(X)
appropriate descriptive statistic to be computed from the sample,
e.g., sample mean, standard deviation, or proportion. In the instance of
pet adoptions, we would use the proportion of all animals that are
T(X)
adopted, and the proportion of puppies that are adopted. is a
T(X)
random variable since it varies from sample to sample. is called a
test statistic because it is used to test the validity of the null hypothesis.
4. Choose an appropriate test and level of significance α: Use the
T(X)
sampling distribution of the test statistic based on the null
hypothesis to identify regions of high likelihood for typical values. For
T(X) P (X)
simplicity, we will drop and use the notation N to represent
the probabilities of the sampling distribution of the test statistic under
P (X)
the null hypothesis, and A under the alternate hypothesis.
In the pet adoption example, the sampling distribution of the proportion
of adopted animals is a Normal distribution since a sample proportion is
a special kind of an average. Explore GeminiBox 6.1.1 to find out how a
sample proportion is an average.
GeminiBox 6.1.1: Sample proportion is an average ⏎
“Is a sample proportion a kind of an average?” (cid:0)
We will use the sample mean as the test statistic going forward, but the
hypothesis testing process is similar for any test statistic such as the
median.
Since the probability tables for the Standard Normal distribution are
widely available, it is customary to convert the test statistic to a Z-score,
by subtracting the average of the sampling distribution of the sample
mean and dividing by the standard deviation of the sampling distribution
of the sample mean. From Chapter 5 we know that the average of the
sampling distribution of the sample mean is the population mean μ, and
σ/√n
the standard deviation is the standard error, given by , where μ
and σ are the population mean and standard deviation of the population.
Then, the Z-score:
T(X) − μ
Z = ,
σ/√n
has a Standard Normal distribution.
Using the special properties of the Standard Normal distribution
discussed in Chapter 5, and the probability tables of the Standard
Normal distribution for an accurate probability interval, we can state that
P(−1.96 ≤ Z ≤ 1.96) = 0.95.
Therefore we expect that 95% of samples will have a Z-score that falls
in this region, as shown in Figure 6.4.
The bounds -1.96 and 1.96 are called critical values that correspond to a
confidence level of 95%, the probability that a Z-score will fall in that
interval marked typical region in Figure 6.4.
α = 1 − 0.95
The probability is called the level of significance, the low
probability region of the distribution where there is only a 5% chance of
finding a sample from the null distribution through sheer chance. They
are shaded red in Figure 6.4. The region outside the typical region,
beyond the critical values, is called the critical region.
5. Check the test condition: Is the Z-score computed from the sample in
the typical region of the sampling distribution under the null hypothesis,
where the probability of finding such a value is high?
T(X)
6. Draw a conclusion: If the answer is yes, then the test statistic
computed from the sample is typical and the null hypothesis is valid. If
the answer is no, it is quite unlikely (less than α probability) that the null
hypothesis is valid based on the sample. For example, if the test statistic,
the proportion of puppies, is within the typical region, then the adoption
rates for puppies is the same as other animals. If the answer is no, then
there is a significant difference in the adoption rate of puppies compared
to other animals at the rescue center.

FIGURE 6.4: Left: A two-tailed test, showing critical values, critical and
typical regions where the level of significance α is split between the two
extremes of the distribution. Right: Single-tailed test. ⏎
6.1.2 What is a p-value?
A p-value is a probability related to the test statistic computed from the
sampling distribution and provides an alternate way of testing the null
hypothesis based on probabilities instead of critical values. We will discuss
using a one-tailed test for simplicity, but the explanation applies in general.
The area to the right of the value of a statistic under its sampling
distribution is called its p-value, as shown in Figure 6.5. The larger the p-
value, the more typical the sample.

Key concepts in hypothesis testing are summarized in ReferenceBox

example in Section 6.2.3.
ReferenceBox 6.1.2: Hypothesis Testing Terminology ⏎
Hypothesis refers to a statement formulated to answer a particular
question. The statement will be tested, i.e., confirmed or
contradicted, using data sampled from the population.
Null or baseline hypothesis refers to the default state, which is a
hypothesis of no difference, until we can prove otherwise. An
example null hypothesis is: “there is no difference in the adoption
rate of puppies at rescue centers compared to other animals.”
Alternate hypothesis is a specific alternative to the null hypothesis.
For example, “puppies are adopted at a different rate”, or “puppies
are adopted at a higher rate”, or “puppies are adopted at a lower
rate.” The first statement includes both the second and third
statements.
T(X)
Test statistic, , is a descriptive statistic computed from the
sampled data. It is a random variable since it varies from sample to
T(X) = t
sample. The value of based on the particular sample X is
used to make a determination regarding the hypothesis. Sample
mean, sample proportion and sample median are commonly used test
statistics. For simplicity, we will use the Normal distribution as an
T(X)
example of the sampling distribution of under the null
hypothesis, but the discussion holds for other types of sampling
distributions as well.
The test statistic is converted to a Z-score by subtracting the mean μ
of the sampling distribution of the sample mean and dividing by the
standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the sample mean,
σ/√n
i.e., its standard error ). The Z-score follows the Standard
Normal distribution whose probability tables are available.

Confidence level is the probability associated with the typical region
of the Standard Normal distribution. If the Z-score computed from
the sample falls in the typical region, then it is likely that the null
hypothesis is true based on the sample. The typical region is marked
and shown with a blue arrow in Figure 6.4 and corresponds to the
T(X)
interval where a majority of values of are expected to fall if
the null hypothesis is true.
Critical region is a low probability region of the Z-score distribution.
If the Z-score falls in the critical region then it is unlikely that the
null hypothesis is true based on the sample. The critical region is
shaded in red and shown with red arrows in Figure 6.4.
Critical values are the cut-off values that mark the boundary of the
critical region. They are the thresholds used for determining whether
a test statistic is consistent with the null hypothesis. Examples of

correspond to two-tailed and one-tailed tests discussed in Section
6.2.
Level of significance, α, is the probability corresponding to the
critical region. It is the probability of finding an unusual sample and
incorrectly rejecting the null hypothesis. In Figure 6.4,

Statistical hypothesis testing involves variations of this fundamental
process depending on the question, sample sizes, number of samples, and
the nature of the sampling distributions. We will discuss each scenario in
detail. See Section 6.2.3 for a step-by-step example of how to use sample
data to test a hypothesis.
6.2 Tests Based on Type of Hypothesis
In the discussion above, we discussed the null or baseline hypothesis of no
difference. For example, in the animal adoption example, we assumed that
the puppy adoption rates are the same as all animals. We did not specify
whether the adoption rate is higher or lower, just different. This is an
important point that results in two different types of tests and the way the
level of significance is interpreted.
6.2.1 Two-tailed test
For the null hypothesis “there is no difference in adoption rates for puppies
compared to all rescue animals”, there are two alternate possibilities, i.e.,
higher or lower adoption rates. Therefore, an alternate hypothesis that has
no prior knowledge or belief includes both unusually high rates of puppy
adoptions as well unusually low rates. The critical region will be split in
two, as shown in Figure 6.4. The red shaded areas indicate whether the
adoption rate for puppies is more (right side) or less (left side) compared to
the overall adoption rates for all animals. This is called a two-tailed test
since there are two possible alternatives to the null hypothesis of “no
difference in proportion of adoptions.” Such a test would flag puppy
adoption rates that are too low or too high.
6.2.2 One-tailed test
Suppose that the rescue center manager tells us that puppies are adopted at a
higher rate than the average adoption rate for rescue animals. This implies
that when we look for unusual values, we should focus only on higher
values since we have prior information. Such a test with only one possible
alternative is called a single-tailed or one-tailed test, as shown in Figure 6.4.
(A similar argument applies for “lower adoption rate” had the manager told
us that puppies are adopted at a lower rate than the rate for all animals. We
would use a test where the critical region lies entirely on the lower valued
side of the distribution.)
Many common test statistics like sample mean and sample proportion
follow a Normal distribution. The critical values for a given α can be
determined using Standard Normal tables or using software tools. Work
through Example 6.2.3 to fully understand the process of hypothesis testing.

6.3 Tests Based on Sample Size
One of the assumptions of the Central Limit Theorem is that the sample size
is reasonably large. What happens if the sample size is small? There are
many situations where it is either expensive, damaging or difficult to collect
data, e.g., collecting dirt samples from Mars (Figure 6.7, left) or biopsies
for diagnosing rare diseases.
FIGURE 6.7: Left: NASA's Perseverance rover took this selfie on May 10,
2025, marking its 1,500th Martian day, or sol, exploring the Red Planet.
The small dark hole in the rock in front of the rover is the borehole made
when Perseverance collected a sample dubbed “Bell Island.” Collecting
samples on Mars is expensive, so the sample size tends to be small (Photo:
JPL). Right: Large samples are collected and analyzed by AI tool BirdCast,
a real-time bird movement forecasting tool powered by the crowdsourced
data collection resource, eBird. The picture on the right shows forecasts for
bird migrations on June 9, 2025 in Morris County, NJ. ⏎
6.3.1 Mars rover Perseverance: Small samples and Student's
t-test
Consider a hypothetical situation where a Mars rover can only take 10
samples. It reports that each of the 10 samples of equal volume has 10, 12,
13, 13, 12, 10, 14, 15, 7 and 13 units of silicates and iron oxide, the
minerals that make Martian soil seem red.
n = 10
Since we only have samples, we cannot use the Z-score test for
sample means. Student's t-test, which was designed by William Gosset to
test the quality of ale, is suitable for small samples. It is appropriate when
the underlying distribution of minerals is Normal, and the standard
deviation of the population is unknown. Suppose these assumptions hold,
and we want to test whether the level of minerals is the same as that on
earth, which is, say 10 in this hypothetical example. The null hypothesis is
“there is no difference in the soil composition of Earth and Mars in terms of
these minerals”, and we expect the mean of the 10 samples to be the same
μ = 10
as Earth, namely .
The t-test statistic is given by:
x¯ − μ
t =
σ(x)/√n
(6.1)
= (11.9 − 10)/(0.814506 ∗ √10)
(6.2)
= 2.58
(6.3)
x¯
where μ is the Earth's mean, and is the sample mean. We use the Earth's
mean composition since the null hypothesis assumes that Mars has the same
composition.
σ(x)
The population standard deviation is unknown and is estimated
using the sample. When we estimate the standard deviation from the
sample, we impose a constraint on the data that it must have that specific
standard deviation, so effectively our sample size is only 9. This is called
the degrees of freedom denoted by df. Using the t-distribution table with 9
df, we find that the critical values are 1.833 and 2.262 for the one- and two-
α = 0.05
tailed tests, at level of significance. The value of the test statistic
2.58 is greater than both the critical values; therefore, we conclude that the
average silicate and iron oxide content in Martial soil is different (two-
tailed test), and higher (one-tailed test) than that on Earth in this
hypothetical example.
A special type of t-test is used when comparing pairs of related values.
For example, measurements on the left and right eyes of a person, or
symptoms of a patient before and after treatment. Such a test is called a
paired t-test and involves applying a t-test to the difference in the paired
values of measurements.

6.3.2 BirdCast AI: Large samples and the Z-score test
BirdCast is an AI tool for bird migration developed by researchers at
Cornell, Colorado State and University of Massachusetts, Amherst, in
partnership with EPA, NASA, NSF, Audubon Society and many others. It
enables real-time tracking and forecasting of bird movements powered by a
birding and reporting tool, eBird. Thanks to the automation and
crowdsourcing powered by eBird, data is plentiful and sample sizes are
large. The Z-score test illustrated in Example 6.2.3 applies.
Suppose, hypothetically, that the average number of birds migrating
through Morris County on June 9 is expected to be 300 per hour based on
historical observation. We might wish to see if June 9, 2025, follows this
pattern.
1. Null hypothesis: The number of migrating birds spotted each hour in
Morris County, NJ, on June 9, 2025 is the same as historical data, 300
per hour.
α = 0.05
2. The level of significance is specified to be .

different hours of the day.

The previous example shows that it is important to state the alternate
hypothesis before conducting the test, rather than adjust conclusions after
observing the data, which would be tantamount to data-dredging described
in Section 6.6. If our alternate hypothesis had stated that “the hourly
migratory rates in Morris County in 2025 are lower than previous years”,
we would have used a one-tailed test and found evidence that the rates were
indeed lower. However, if we have no a priori reason to believe that, we
should use a more conservative alternate hypothesis “the hourly migration
rates are different,” and our test would find no reason to indicate that.

GeminiBox 6.3.3: Applications of Hypothesis Testing
How is hypothesis testing applied in real life? (cid:0)
6.3.3 A/B testing: Tests based on more than one sample
Websites embed subtle behavioral cues that we respond to without even
being aware. These cues are chosen carefully through A/B testing. Social
media companies use A/B testing extensively and continuously to decide
between design elements and their placement to maximize user
engagement. E-commerce companies tweak wording, font size to display
price, and placement of images to optimize sales. AI tools enable collecting
large amounts of data in real-time, and testing hundreds of thousands of
hypotheses to compare pairs of scenarios to choose the one that provides
the best desired outcome. This process is called A/B testing. The image
below presents a hypothetical example of two identical scenarios, except for
the “Learn more” button. Just by changing the color, and including an arrow
icon, the click rate went from 52% to 72%. Companies constantly assess
customers' behavioral psychology and preferences through such tests.
Statistics for Experimenters by Box et al. [6] is a classic reference for
practitioners of hypothesis testing.
Extended Description
FIGURE 6.8: Social media companies and e-commerce websites assess
behavioral psychology and user preferences through A/B testing. Image:
Maxime Lorant, CC BY-SA 4.0. (cid:0)
To illustrate A/B testing, consider the example of our animal rescue
center, which now wants to raise funds through soliciting monetary
donations. It tries two different icons, a puppy and a cat, on different
webpages and compares the proportion of users who actually click and
donate money via the two types of webpages. This is called a two-sample
test, since we compare two different samples: monetary donations from
webpages with a cat icon and monetary donations from webpages with a
puppy icon. There are many variations of this two-sample test. We describe
a simple version below, where the two samples are independent yet
collected under comparable conditions.

6.4 Could We Have Made a Mistake? Type 1 and Type 2
errors
We have discussed that the level of significance α is the probability of
finding an unusual sample value by chance from the null distribution, a
worst case scenario of error. A mistake implies that we came to the wrong
conclusion of rejecting the null hypothesis based on an abnormal sample.
For example, consider Figure 6.10. It is possible that the particular sample
we chose might have been an exception with abnormal values even though
P (X)
its sampling distribution is genuinely the null distribution (blue), N .
Such an abnormal sample leads us to the false conclusion that puppies are
adopted at a higher rate. Such a mistaken conclusion is called a Type 1 error
in statistics and a false positive in machine learning. The probability of a
Type 1 error is represented by the red shaded region to the right, and is
α.
often prespecified to be no more than the level of significance It
represents the probability of an abnormal sample if the null hypothesis is
true. It is given by:
P (X > C ) = α.
N α
Extended Description
FIGURE 6.10: Type 1 error or false positive: Reject the null hypothesis due
to an abnormal sample value from the null distribution. Type 2 error or false
negative: Mistakenly accept the null hypothesis (i.e., reject the alternate
hypothesis) due an abnormal sample from the alternate distribution. ⏎
On the other hand, suppose the alternate hypothesis that puppy adoption
P (X)
rate is genuinely higher as denoted by the sampling distribution A ,
the red dashed curve, is true. Then a particular sample might have unusually
low adoption rates just by chance, and would lead us to conclude that there
is no difference in the adoption rates of puppies and overall adoption rates
of all animals. This is called a Type 2 error in statistics and a false negative
in machine learning, where a genuinely different sample from the alternate
P (X)
distribution of higher puppy adoptions, A distribution, is mistakenly
declared not to be different. The probability is given by a sample from the
alternate distribution being unusually small, i.e., less than the boundary of
the critical region, C :
α
P (X < C ) = β.
A α
Ideally, we would like both types of errors to be small. But Figure 6.10
shows that it is a trade-off. Sliding the decision boundary of the critical
region (dotted red line) to reduce the Type 1 error implies an increase in
Type 2 error and vice versa, in this particular type of scenario of symmetric,
unimodal distributions. Section 6.4.1 has a nice example of this trade-off.
You can learn more about different types of errors by using the prompts in
GeminiBox 6.4.1.

6.4.1 Power of a test
Suppose you are given the weights of two groups of animals. One group is
horses. The second group is either elephants or zebras. Which one is easier
to tell apart from horses based on weights?
It is intuitively clear that elephants have much higher weights, so it
should be easier to tell whether the mean of an observed sample of weights
(represented by squares on the X-axis in Figure 6.11) is that of elephants
(purple) or horses (gray). On the other hand, a sample of weights of zebras
(purple) might be harder to tell apart from that of horses.
Extended Description
FIGURE 6.11: The sample mean has a high power of distinguishing
P (X) P (X)
between well-separated null ( N ) and alternate hypotheses ( A )
(e.g., distribution of weights of horses and elephants) but low power when
the two distributions have a high overlap (e.g., distribution of weights of
horses and zebras). ⏎
When the null distribution (weights of horses) and alternate distribution
(weights of elephants) are far apart as shown in Figure 6.11, left, then the
overlap is very little and the chances of classifying elephants as horses
based on a sample of weights, i.e., Type 2 error, is small. On the other hand,
when the distributions have considerable overlap, like the weights of horses
and zebras shown in Figure 6.11, right, then there is a greater chance of
mistaking the sample of weights of zebras for those of horses, i.e., the Type
2 error is high.
The ability of a test statistic to discriminate between the null and
alternative hypotheses is quantified by its power. It is the area to the right of
the critical value C under the distribution of the alternative hypothesis,
α
P (X)
A .
Power = P (X > C ),
A α
where X is the random variable that represents the sample mean, and the
P (X)
probability is computed using the alternate distribution, A . The
greater this area, the greater the power.
6.4.2 Relationship to Type 2 error
Recall that Type 2 errors are false negatives, i.e., the probability that we
accept the null hypothesis when the alternate hypothesis is true.
Type 2 Error = β = P (X < C ).
A α
This also implies that:
Power = P (X > C ) = 1 − P (X < C ) = 1 − β.
A α A α
In hypothesis testing language, the weight distributions of horses and
elephants are well-separated, and a sample mean will have a high power to
distinguish between the two, and Type 2 error will be low. On the other
hand, the weight distributions of horses and zebras are quite similar, and it
will be harder to distinguish between the two distributions based on the
sample mean of weights. That implies that a test based on the sample mean
in this situation will have a high Type 2 error, reflected in the big chunk of
the purple distribution in Figure 6.11, right, and as a consequence, low
power.
ReferenceBox 6.4.1: Types of Errors and Power of a Test
Type 1 error (False Positive) happens when a sample from the null
distribution is mistakenly declared to be from the alternate
distribution since it exceeds the critical value, C . This happens if
α
the sample is abnormal just by chance. For example, a set of
unusually heavy horses is declared to be from a population of
elephants based on weight. The probability of Type 1 Error is α, the
level of significance:
P (X > C ) = α.
N α
Type 2 error (False Negative) is when a sample from the alternative
hypothesis distribution is declared to be from the null hypothesis
distribution. This happens when the sample from the alternate
distribution has unusually small values, less than the critical value
C , but values that are typical for the null distribution. Type 2 error,
α
β, is given by:
P (X < C ) = β
A α
The power of a test is the ability of the test statistic to discriminate
P P
between samples from the null N and alternate A distributions.
The power of a test based on the sample mean is typically high when
the distributions are well separated (weights of horses and elephants)
and low when there is considerable overlap between the two
(weights of horses and zebras). Power is given by:
P (X > C ) = 1 − β.
A α
6.5 Nonparametric Tests for Empirical Distributions
The tests we have discussed thus far are based on the Normal distribution
and Student's t-distribution. These are called parametric tests because we
assume a prespecified distribution, e.g., the Normal, and test hypotheses
about its parameters such as the mean μ and standard deviation σ. While the
Normal distribution works for the sampling distribution of the sample mean
because of the Central Limit Theorem, it does not work in many scenarios
where the underlying distribution is not Normal. In such instances, it is
possible to use tests that are purely data-driven and do not make
assumptions about the underlying distribution. Such tests are called
nonparametric tests.
There are many nonparametric tests. Some are based on the ranks of
ordered data (Mann-Whitney or the Wilcoxon rank sum test), and some use
the empirical distributions (Kolmogorov-Smirnov) to test whether two
groups of data have the same probability distribution. Robust
nonparametric statistical methods by Hettmansperger and McKean [20]
provides a strong grounding in nonparametric tests. We will discuss a
simple, single sample test for testing the median of population using a
sample.
6.5.1 StreetStyle, AI tool for fashion: Sign test for the median
The fashion industry has been a big adopter of AI, using it to generate
design ideas, to forecast trends and to understand customer preferences. AI
has also been used to detect global fashion trends as explained in The
Cornell Chronicle article that's available in Section 6.7. The AI tool
StreetStyle [28] uses computer vision and pattern recognition along with
expert knowledge to identify concentrations of a particular type of clothing,
and analyze trends, e.g., trucker hats creeping onto fashion runways.
A sign test is a popular tool in fashion. Suppose we wish to test the
hypothesis that the median weight of a particular type of fashion accessory,
e.g., a headpiece shown in Figure 7.3, is m. We do not know whether the
distribution of the weight of the headpiece is bell-shaped or not. However,
if m is indeed the median, then if we select a sample of n headpieces
independently of each other, we would expect half of the data to be less
than m in value and half to be greater, because the median divides the rank-
ordered data into two halves. Therefore, if we subtract the median m from
each value of the sample, we would expect half the values to be negative
and half to be positive. If we ignore the numeric data values and focus just
on the sign of the difference, we would expect that roughly half the
differences would have a positive sign and half of them a negative sign.
Extended Description
FIGURE 6.12: AI StreetStyle can identify global fashion trends in
headpieces. Left: A headpiece from the Kathakali art form in India. (Photo:
Maneesha Shahani (cid:0) ). Right: Testing whether the median weight of a
Kathakali headpiece is 1.75 pounds using a sign test. Gemini generated
p = 0.5
Binomial distribution with n = 50 and (cid:0).
This is exactly like a coin toss where the probability of heads or tails is
0.5. Therefore, the number of samples with positive signs (number of
sample values that exceed the median value), out of a total sample of n
headpieces, will be governed by the Binomial distribution discussed in
Chapter 5. Formally:
W ∼ B(n,p), p = 0.5
B(n,p)
where W is the total number of positive signs in n samples, and is a
p = 0.5
Binomial distribution of n samples with probability of an
independently sampled point being greater than the median m. Note that
counting the number of positive (or negative) signs is equivalent to
counting the number of headpieces whose weight exceeds (or less than) the
median value, m. Let us work through an example.

6.6 Pitfalls of Hypothesis Testing: Data Dredging
In this chapter, we have discussed hypothesis tests that involve numeric
variables, analyzed one at a time. In the next chapter that focuses on
relationship between multiple variables, we will study two additional
hypothesis testing topics, Chi-square tests of independence and Analysis of
Variance (ANOVA), that focus on categorical variables and their interaction
with other variables.
We conclude this chapter with a brief caution about the pitfalls of
hypothesis testing, particularly in the age of AI where data and computing
power have exploded, enabling the testing of hundreds and thousands of
hypotheses each second.
Research studies often focus on multiple attributes or multiple groups
and are interested in answering multiple questions simultaneously, called a
family of tests. It is possible that a single sample is used for testing all the
hypotheses, or multiple samples are used. Regardless, the focus is on
multiple hypotheses being tested simultaneously. Matt Sosna's article in
Section 6.7 explains the dangers of multiple testing. We describe it briefly
here.
Suppose we are interested in testing the connection between the color of
jelly beans and acne. Suppose that the jelly beans come in 20 colors. We
expect ( null hypothesis) that there is no association between any given
color of jelly beans and acne, at the level of significance α=0.05. That is,
the chances of getting a false positive, by sheer chance and variation in
samples, are 5% or 0.05 for any of the 20 colors. That is, the probability of
accepting the null hypothesis of no association between a given color of
jelly beans and acne when it is actually true is 95%.
If the 20 tests, one for each color, are independent of each other, then the
probability of correctly accepting the null hypothesis in ALL the 20 tests,
using the probability rule of multiplication probabilities of independent
events is:
(0.95)20 = 0.36
The probability of the complementary event of getting at least one false
positive in 20 tests is:
1 − (0.95)20 = 1 − 0.36 = 0.64.
Therefore, the random chance of getting a false conclusion for at least one
of the 20 colors is 64%. The more tests you run, the more likely the chances
of a false positive. This is how spurious associations show up!
The process of deliberately running multiple tests and choosing to report
the few false positives that occur by chance as a genuine rejection of the
null hypothesis (“green jelly beans cause acne!”) is called data dredging or
p-hacking. By continuously running tests, we are guaranteed that one false
positive with a small p-value shows up by chance. Explore GeminiBox
6.6.1 to learn more.
GeminiBox 6.6.1: Data dredging ⏎
“What is data dredging?” (cid:0)
The journalist John Bohannon, pranked the media by running a study
with a small sample (15) and testing 18 hypotheses with 18 variables until
one of them showed up as negating the null hypothesis just by chance. His
study on how chocolates help weight loss was reported and amplified by the
news media until it reached far corners of the world. Read his article I
fooled millions into thinking chocolate helps weight loss in Section 6.7, and
a related article from NPR titled, Why A Journalist Scammed The Media
Into Spreading Bad Chocolate Science.

chart). Sketch the distribution, and label the critical values and
critical region for the hypothesis.
vi. What is the conclusion? Write the conclusion statement in terms
of the given context.
(b) We want to test the statement: “The mean bill length for the Gentoo
species is different than 50 mm,” using a random sample of 10
penguins from the Penguin data.
i. Find the mean bill length for the Gentoo population.
ii. Select a random sample of 10 Gentoo penguins.
iii. We will use a Student t-test to test the significance of the
statement. Will this be a one-tailed or two-tailed test? Why?
iv. Find the test statistic and, using a significance level 0.05, find
the critical values.
v. What is the conclusion? Explain.
2. The population of Gentoo species
A group of research students went to the Palmer Islands to collect data
about penguins. They sampled 60 penguins and found that of the three
different species in the sample, 24 penguins were of the Gentoo species.
They hypothesized that in the population of penguins in the entire
Palmer Islands, 40% of penguins belong to the Gentoo species.
(a) Assuming the Palmer Penguin data set is the population, should we
accept this hypothesis?
7
Variable Relationships: the Full Picture
Most real-world problems involve multiple factors and variables. How would sleep and hunger affect
an athlete's performance in a soccer game? How does the cost of a concert ticket change with the
venue, and fame and popularity of the artist? How do climate and environmental conditions affect
crops and harvests? In all these examples, there are multiple variables that interact with each other in
ways that affect the outcome. AI has made it possible to analyze hundreds of variables simultaneously,
across time and space, which has led to the discovery of new drugs and gene therapies, techniques for
real-time hurricane alerts, wild fire management, and optimizing fashion supply chains, among
others. In this chapter, we will study the statistics of multiple variables and their interactions, and
examples of AI tools and applications that enable studying them.
Studying multiple attributes builds a more complete and richer description of an object, increasing the
accuracy of analytics and AI models. For example, the more variables we study, such as bill length, bill
depth, flipper length, the better we understand the differences between each of the three species of the
Palmer penguins. This chapter provides an introduction to the study of multiple variables. The objective is
to provide a brief overview of each type of analysis, to motivate its need and to connect traditional statistical
concepts to AI applications.
The χ-square test of independence, ANOVA and linear regression are discussed in some detail. However,
the topics of time series, spatial analysis and spatio-temporal analyses are introduced very briefly at a high
level, with a greater focus on their application to AI, highlighted through examples. These topics are
complex and require extensive foundations, but being familiar with them is essential since they are
fundamental to many real-world applications as we will learn in the illustrative examples.
7.1 Illustrative Example: Penguins, the more variables the merrier
Consider Figure 7.1 that depicts three variables from the Palmer penguin data: flipper length, bill length and
species. The first two variables are continuous quantitative variables, while the last one is a qualitative,
nominal variable. There are several interesting observations:
In Figure 7.1, the two boxes on the left show the distribution of the three penguin species, Adelie,
Chinstrap and Gentoo (different colors), across the values of bill length and flipper length. The figures
are called “stripcharts” in R, and show how the data values are stacked on the X-axis, with a little bit of
jitter to avoid overplotting. The three species have overlap in terms of bill length, particularly Chinstrap
and Gentoo which are almost indistinguishable based on bill length alone. Similarly, Adelie and
Chinstrap penguins are indistinguishable based on flipper length alone as indicated by their overlap in
the second box.
Now, look at Figure 7.1, right. This plot is taken from Horst et al.’s [21] analysis of the Palmer Penguin
data, the scatterplot of flipper length versus bill length. Here the three species occupy their own distinct
regions in the two-dimensional space of flipper length vs. bill length, with very little overlap.
While we could not distinguish between the three species based on just the bill length or just the flipper
length, when we consider both variables together, the three species are quite distinguishable in the two-
dimensional space of flipper length and bill length.
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.1: Plots of three variables from the Palmer Penguin data taken from Horst et al.'s [21] manual.
Bill length and flipper length values for the three species are stacked on the X-axis. Some species are very
similar and have considerable overlap in range of values, e.g., Chinstrap and Gentoo with respect to bill
length, and Adelie and Chinstrap with respect to flipper length. Right, a scatterplot of flipper length vs. bill
length from Horst et al.'s [21] manual. What happens to the overlap between species when you consider the
two variables flipper length and bill length together? ⏎
There are many instances where considering two or more variables, and their interaction provides more
insight than merely examining each variable by itself. In machine learning and AI, variable interactions are
the key to good predictive models.
It is not always possible to visualize like we did in Figure 7.1, or use all the attributes or dimensions of
the data in analysis. More variables increase the computational burden as well as cause the notion of
distances in higher-dimensional space to collapse. This is called the curse of dimensionality. Explore
GeminiBox 7.1.1 to learn more.
GeminiBox 7.1.1: Curse of dimensionality ⏎
“What is the curse of dimensionality?” (cid:0)
In such instances, it is useful to consider projections onto lower dimensions, which is a way of reducing
the number of dimensions. Use the prompt in GeminiBox 7.1.2 to learn more. The stripcharts are one-
dimensional projections since only one variable is represented. The scatterplot is a two-dimensional
projection, since it represents two variables. There are other dimensions, such as body mass, that are not
represented in the plots.
GeminiBox 7.1.2: Lower-dimensional projections ⏎
“What is meant by projection onto a lower dimension?” (cid:0)
AI has enabled the processing of hundreds of variables resulting in applications that impact almost all
aspects of life, from real estate sales to building generative AI models that can write romance novels and
create cute puppy images. It has made it possible to develop increasingly precise models, for example, to
power autonomous cars. Companies like Zillow predict the sale price of a home based on numerous
variables such as square footage, number of bedrooms, location, prices of neighboring homes, condition of
the house, and supply in the neighborhood. The variables and predictions are updated in real-time using AI
tools. Explore how AI uses multiple variables with GeminiBox 7.1.3.
Data Debate 7.1.1 Do you think it is important to use multiple variables to determine an athlete's
performance at a particular event? If so, what variables? Do you think these would change with time? What
if the athlete were participating in an event near the Alps versus near the Sahara Desert? Would that make a
difference? Why?
GeminiBox 7.1.3: AI models and multiple variables ⏎
Does AI use multiple variables? What is an example? (cid:0)
7.1.1 Illustrative example: AI tracks climate change
Changes in climate and pollution have created many challenges: icebergs melt and lead to flooding,
uncharacteristic heat causes droughts and food insecurity, and wildlife gets ensnared in plastic waste as
shown in Figure 7.2, left. Monitoring and providing early warning to communities challenged by climate
changes requires studying a complex array of variables over time, such as temperature trends, ocean flows,
changes in vegetation and migration patterns.
FIGURE 7.2: A turtle caught in a plastic bag. AI helps monitor ocean waste by tracking a complex array of
constantly changing variables. (Photo credit: Coordinator copy.) A drone deployed in agriculture to monitor
crop diseases like citrus canker uses image recognition based on multiple variables. ⏎
Providing advance warning requires speedy and timely processing of all these variables. AI is being used
extensively and has been shown to be an effective tool in monitoring constantly changing variables. Read
the World Economic Forum story in Section 7.8 on how AI is being used: (i) to pinpoint icebergs that are
melting, (ii) to power drones to aid reforestation in Brazil, and (iii) to monitor and minimize waste and
emissions.
7.1.2 Illustrative example: AI deployed in drones helps farmers
The United States Department of Agriculture's National Institute of Food and Agriculture conducts research
and educates farmers on how to manage land, livestock and environmental resources to make better
decisions. The deployment of drones, shown in Figure 7.2, right, for detecting crop diseases through image
recognition and examining soil conditions, and other AI-driven tools make it possible to collect and analyze
a constant stream of data of different types of variables across space and time to help farmers optimize land
use. Read more in the article AI powered hydroponics: Revolutionizing sustainable agriculture in Section
7.8.
The preceding illustrative examples have demonstrated that studying the relationship between multiple
variables is crucial for tackling complex problems and can be addressed effectively with AI. Classical
statistical methods lay the foundation for such analyses. We will discuss three examples of such multivariate
analysis: the χ2 test for independence of categorical variables, Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) for the effect
of categorical variables on continuous variables, and linear relationships between continuous variables.
We will also familiarize ourselves with special types of variables that are measured continuously in time
(temporal), across space (spatial), and across both time and space (spatio-temporal). These data types need
specialized data structures and computing approaches and have benefited greatly from AI methods.
7.2 Independence of Categorical Variables
In Chapter 2, we studied several types of qualitative categorical variables. Species of penguins, ice cream
flavors, different types of sports, and fashion brands are all good examples of qualitative variables. Studying
the effect of categorical variables on each other is critical in many applications, e.g., what is the most
attractive combination of color and shape of a “like” icon on a website in order to attract most user
engagement? Let us start with an example.
7.2.1 AI, fake handbags and the Chi-squared (χ2) test of independence
The luxe fashion resale company, The RealReal, uses AI to generate product descriptions as well as to
screen fakes. In 2019, the company was hit with a class action lawsuit that stated that it was selling knock-
off luxury goods. It was settled in 2021. In response, The RealReal deployed AI to pre-screen hundreds of
thousands of products including luxury handbags, to filter out a few for human scrutiny. It would have been
impossible to manually screen all such products given the volume, without the speed of AI. Read more
about this story in the article The RealReal uses AI to spot fakes in Section 7.8.
One interesting hypothetical test for such a fashion goods company would be to see if there was a
dependence between the region where the fake goods originate and the brands. In order to test if there is
indeed such a dependence between fake brands and regions where they were uploaded, the company would
use the Chi-squared
(χ2
) test for independence.

7.2.2 Step-by-step example of χ2 test of independence
Consider the example of two categorical variables Brand (with three levels Celine, Prada and Birkin) and
Region (with five levels NY, NJ, CT, CA, IL). The null hypothesis H would be: “There is no dependence
N
between the region and the brands”. The alternate hypotheses H would be: “There is a dependence between
A
fake brands and regions where they are uploaded.” Suppose the company collected the data shown in Figure

regions (categories of X) is n X =4, and the number of brands (categories of Y) n Y = 3. Consider the
following:
1. The marginal probabilities for the brands are computed by taking the column totals and dividing them by
the grand total of 1000. Therefore:
P(Celine) = 180/1000 = 0.18
P(Prada) = 180/1000 = 0.18
P(Birkin) = 640/1000 = 0.64

P(CT) = 140/1000 = 0.14
P(CA) = 100/1000 = 0.1
P(IL) = 100/1000 = 0.1

The test statistic's sampling distribution is a well-known continuous distribution called the χ2 with

much less than a level of significance of α = 0.05.
6. We conclude that the null hypothesis is rejected and that there is a significant dependence between
region and the brand of fakes that originate there.

“What are the underlying assumptions of the χ2 test of independence?” (cid:0)
Explore GeminiBox 7.2.1 to learn more about the χ2 test such as its assumptions: independent and randomly
chosen data, tabulated to reflect counts or frequencies of categorical data, not proportions or percentages.
Dependence must not be mistaken for causation; it just means that there is a propensity of certain values
of the two categorical variables to co-occur. Furthermore, if the cell counts are too small, say smaller than 5,
then other tests, such as Fisher's exact test should be considered. As always, human supervision and vetting
is crucial, which is why The RealReal has a set of experts to validate the goods screened by AI.
7.3 Analysis of Variance for Continuous and Categorical Variables
Suppose that in addition to counts or frequencies of categorical variables, we have multiple measurements
of a continuous variable within each combination of the levels of the categorical variables. Do diseases
respond to different drug treatments? Are they more effective for men or women? Do different fruit respond
differently to natural and artificial light?
Horticulturists at the USDA study hydroponic produce, Figure 7.4, left, to identify the best varieties that
respond to hydroponic agriculture. Agritech uses AI to monitor light conditions, humidity, mineral levels,
plant growth indicators and other variables and analyze data from hundreds of sensors. According to the
article AI powered hydroponics: Revolutionizing sustainable agriculture in Section 7.8, AI makes
sustainable agriculture possible. With increasing pressure on earth's soil resources, there is great interest in
the use of AI for alternative farming methods, e.g., sustainable soil-less agriculture and vertical farming.
FIGURE 7.4: Left: A USDA horticulturist examines hydroponic strawberries. What type of fruit would
thrive under hydroponic agriculture? Right: Hypothetical data table to test the variation in weight
(continuous variable measured in pounds) of fruit due to the interaction between two categorical variables
(Light treatment and Fruit type). ⏎
7.3.1 Hydroponic agritech AI and ANOVA
Consider the second hypothetical example in Figure 7.4. It contains five measurements of weight of fruit,
called replications or repeated measurements for each combination of Light Treatment type (artificial,
natural) and Fruit type (oranges, strawberries, tomatoes). The number of measurements in each cell
corresponding to row i and column j is n ij. They need not be equal, but very often they are, in a planned
balanced experiment. Designing experiments to yield accurate and unbiased results is an important area of
study in statistics. The Theory of Design of Experiments by Cox and Reid [10] is a classic that covers the
proper design of experiments to ensure accurate results. Check GeminiBox 7.3.1 for a quick overview of
experimental design.
GeminiBox 7.3.1: Balanced experimental design ⏎
“What are balanced experiments in statistics?” (cid:0)
In Figure 7.4, n ij = 5 for all cells. We are interested in testing whether the weights of the fruit are
different across different categories of Light treatment (rows) and Fruit type (columns), and across different
combinations of light treatment and fruit type (cells).
GeminiBox 7.3.2: AI and vertical farming
“How is AI used in hydroponic cultivation?” (cid:0).
ANOVA stands for Analysis of Variance and refers to testing whether typical values of a continuous
variable (e.g., the mean) are the same across different categories of a qualitative variable, or combinations
of categories of multiple qualitative variables. It is a generalization of the t-test from two groups to multiple
groups. In the example of hydroponic cultivation, the two categorical variables are light treatment with two
possible values, natural and artificial, and fruit type, with three possible values, oranges, strawberries and
tomatoes. ANOVA helps test whether the typical values of the continuous variable, average weight of the
fruit, are the same across all the categories. We describe an example with two categorical variables, but it is
applicable to combinations of more variables as well. Agresti's Categorical Data Analysis [1] provides a
comprehensive and rigorous coverage of ANOVA, logistic regression and models of other binary responses.
One-way ANOVA is a method of testing the typical values of a continuous variable with respect to a
single qualitative variable, e.g., fruit type. Two-way ANOVA is a way of testing whether the average value
of the continuous variable is the same across combinations of two qualitative variables, e.g., fruit type and
light treatment. We present the test statistic for one-way ANOVA here, while detailed explanations and two-
way ANOVA test statistic are provided in Section 7.5.
ReferenceBox 7.3.1: F-statistic for ANOVA ⏎
Let:

x ijk be the kth observation in row i and column j of the data in Figure 7.4, right. For example,
x 213 = 1.9. In this example, there are n k = 5 observations in each cell.

7.4 ANOVA with Excel
The manual computation of ANOVA test statistics is quite tedious and time-consuming. However, we have
provided a fully worked out step-by-step manual computation of a two-way ANOVA for the Fruit type vs.
Light treatment data in Figure 7.4, right, in Section 7.5. Luckily, programming languages such as R and
Python, and software tools including Excel and Google Sheets provide easy ways of automatically
computing ANOVA statistics. We provide examples below.
7.4.1 One-way ANOVA with Excel: Palmer penguins
One-way ANOVA test is used for testing whether the typical values of a continuous variable are the same
for different values of a single categorical variable. It is a generalization of the t-test in Chapter 6 to more
than two groups. Consider the example of Palmer penguins: suppose that we want to examine if there is a
difference in the mean flipper length (continuous variable) across the three species (qualitative variable) of
penguins: Adelie, Chinstrap and Gentoo.
For this example, we select a random sample of 10 penguins of each species from the data set and
organize the flipper length measurements data in a table as shown in Figure 7.5. The following assumptions
are required for the one-way ANOVA test to be valid:
Level of measurement: Independent variable should be qualitative nominal (e.g., penguin species); the
dependent variable should be quantitative (e.g., flipper-length in mm).
Independence of groups: Measurements of each group should be independent, i.e., the measured values
of one group must not be influenced by the measures of the other groups.
Normality: Continuous variable within each group is normally distributed especially for small sample
sizes.
Homogeneity: The variance within each group must be roughly the same.
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.5: One-way ANOVA with Excel: Input data. Dialog box selections. Output of one-way ANOVA.
⏎
Formulate the null and alternate hypotheses and specify the level of significance α:
1. Null hypothesis: “There is no difference in the mean flipper length across the three species.”
2. Alternate hypothesis: The mean flipper lengths of the three species are statistically different.
3. Level of significance: α=0.05.
Follow the following steps to use Excel Data Analysis Toolkit to perform the one-way ANOVA test:
First, organize the data in three columns with species names as headings, as shown in Figure 7.5, left.
Next, open the Excel Data Analysis Toolkit and choose One-factor ANOVA. A dialog screen appears as
shown in Figure 7.5, middle. Select the cells and input the data exactly as shown in Figure 7.5, left.
Select the output range in the dialog box, a single cell where you want the output tables to be displayed.
Any empty cell in the sheet will do.
The output is ready in Figure 7.5, right! Examine and interpret the output and declare a conclusion.
Interpretation of test output: The output tables from Excel are displayed in Figure 7.5, right.
The row Between groups contains the value of the test statistic F R = 50.2208 corresponding to the F R -
statistic in ReferenceBox 7.3.1, the ratio of sums of squares.
The critical value of the F-distribution with n r −1 = 3−1 d.f., (since there are n r = 3 species) is given by
F = 3.3541. This value is much smaller than the critical value F R = 50.2208.
The p-value =0.00000, which is less than the specified α = 0.05.
Therefore, the null hypothesis is not valid, and we conclude that there is a significant difference in the mean
flipper length of the three species.
7.4.2 Two-way ANOVA with Excel: Hydroponic fruit
Let us discuss two-way ANOVA using the example of hydroponic fruit. The two qualitative variables or
factors are light treatment (rows) and type of fruit (columns). There are three null hypotheses:
H1: The mean weights of fruit grouped by light exposure (rows) are the same.
H2: The mean weights of the type of fruits (columns) are the same.
H3: The interaction between the two factors (light treatment and fruit type) has an effect on the mean
weight of fruit.
The level of significance is set to α = 0.05. Open the Excel Data Analysis Toolkit. From the list, select the
test ANOVA: two-factor with replication. Select the entire table as input range, set the number of rows =5,
and enter the significance level alpha as 0.05, as shown in Figure 7.6, left. The output range can be any cell
on the sheet. The output is shown in Figure 7.6, right. The intermediate tables containing the results are
shown in Section 7.5.
FIGURE 7.6: Left: Excel Data Analysis Toolkit entries for two-way ANOVA. Right: Output table with F
statistics for null hypotheses H1, H2 and H3. ⏎
Interpretation of results in Figure 7.6, right, row-by-row are as follows:
Sample (Rows): Upon examining the F-test statistic and the appropriate critical value, F −crit, we see
that F = 2.4 < F −crit = 4.26. In addition, p-value = 0.1336 > 0.05, the level of significance α. We
accept the null hypothesis H1- the mean weights of the fruits under the two light treatments are the same.
Columns: Upon examination of the F,F −crit and p-values, we see that F = 9.16 > F −crit = 3.4
and p-value 0.00110 < 0.05. We reject the null hypothesis H2. The mean weights of the three varieties
of fruits are not the same.
Interaction: Upon examining the F,F −crit and p-values in this row, we see that
F = 37.1987 > F −crit = 3.4028 and p-value 0.0000 < 0.05. We reject the null hypothesis H3.
There is interaction between the two variables, light treatment and fruit type.
We conclude that the variety of fruit and the interaction between light treatment and fruit type influence the
weight of fruit.
7.5 Manual Computation of a Two-Way ANOVA
In this section we provide a step-by-step computation of two-way ANOVA for the data in Figure 7.4, right.
The notation is a little complex. Please work through this example to truly understand the concepts and
logic behind ANOVA.
7.5.1 ANOVA: Expected values

7.5.2 ANOVA: Sum of squares of deviations
Next, we test how different these means are from the overall mean by computing the sum of squares of
deviations from expected values. The larger the deviation from the overall expected value, the greater the
variability and difference between the groups in terms of the continuous variable of interest.
Row sum of squares captures the deviations of the row means from the overall mean.
SSR = ∑n (R −X)2,i = 1,…,n .
i i r
i
It reflects how different each level of categorical variable 1 is from the overall mean. In Figure 7.4, right,
n i = 15 for all rows, corresponding to each of the two light treatments. Furthermore,
SSR = 15∗(1.573333−1.636667)2+15∗(1.7−1.636667)2 = 0.120333.
Column sum of squares captures the deviations of the row means from the overall mean.
SSC = ∑n (C −X),j = 1,…,n .
j j c

It reflects how different each level of categorical variable 2 is from the overall mean. In our example,

SSC = 10∗(1.77−1.636667)2+10∗(1.77−1.636667)2+10∗(1.37−1.636667)2 = 1.066667.
Interaction sum of squares captures the deviations of the individual cell means from the overall means,
after subtracting the effect of the categorical variable 1 alone (corresponding row mean), and categorical
variable 2 alone (corresponding column mean.)

It reflects the sum of squares of deviation of individual measurements from the overall mean. In our case,

The error sum of squares is
SSE = SST −SSR−SSC−SSI,
what remains after all the factors that affect the mean have been accounted for. In our example,
SSE = 1.352. This is also called residual sum of squares.
7.5.3 ANOVA: Sampling distribution
Each row, column and interaction effect can be tested for significance using statistics based on the sum of
squares and their sampling distributions. The sampling statistics T(X) and their sampling distributions, the
F distributions with appropriate degrees of freedom (d.f.), are given by:
T(X) = (SSR/n −1)/(SSE/(n −1)(n −1));F = F ,d.f.

While the light treatment (F , row effect) by itself has no effect on the weight of the fruit, as reflected by
r
small F and large p-value, the fruit type (F , column effect) and the interaction between the fruit type and

light treatment (F rc, interaction effect) have a significant effect on the weight of the fruit as demonstrated
by large F values and correspondingly small p values. Intermediate output tables corresponding to effect on
