7.6 Relationships Between Continuous Variables
Suppose you bought a new car. You drive it around, sparingly at first, but then your new job requires a
commute, and you drive it more and more. What happens to the mileage on the car as you drive more? What
happens to the fuel consumption? What happens to the resale value of the car? You know instinctively that
mileage increases with distance, as does fuel consumption, but the resale price of the car decreases with
more miles.
The relationship between the distance you drive and mileage is deterministic, an exact reflection of the
number of miles you drive. However, fuel consumption depends on whether you are driving on highways,
on country roads, or in heavy traffic, and whether it is an EV, hybrid or conventional car. In general, the
more you drive, the more fuel you need. Similarly, the resale value of the car decreases with the number of
miles driven but also depends on the make of the car, the level of maintenance, and whether the car has been
in accidents.
In this section, we will study relationships between continuous variables like resale price and miles
driven. Some variable relationships are simple and intuitive, e.g., a change in one variable causes a
proportionate but constant amount of change in the other variable. This is a linear relationship that can be
represented using a straight line. The relationship can also be more complex, e.g., every increment in time
spent studying can cause an exponential improvement in grades.
In this chapter, we will focus only on linear relationships. More complex relationships like quadratic and
exponential, or those that cannot be captured by compact mathematical expressions, require advanced
statistical concepts, machine learning and artificial intelligence methods like deep neural nets.
7.6.1 Linear relationship: Correlation
Consider Figure 7.8. The data is courtesy of UCI Machine Learning Data Repository and pertains to
characteristics of wheat seeds. It can be found at (cid:0). We have created three scatterplots (using R) of the
variable Perimeter with respect to three other characteristics: Compactness, Length of Kernel and
Asymmetry Coefficient. The plot also includes a straight line that approximately “fits” the data, more on
that in a moment. The first panel shows the scatterplot of Perimeters vs. Compactness. The data points are
scattered farther away from the line, indicating that a straight line might not be a good fit. A curve (shown in
pink) might be a better representation. The second panel shows the relationship between Perimeter and
Length of Kernel. The data points are clustered closely around the line indicating that it might be a good
approximation of the data. The third panel shows the scatterplot of Asymmetry Coefficient vs. Perimeter.
The data points are so scattered that the line does a poor job representing the data points. There is probably
no structure to the data.
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.8: Scatterplots and lines of best fit for wheat kernels based on the open source UCI Machine
Learning Repository data. The figures were created using R. ρ=0.5292436, 0.9724223, -0.2173404,
respectively, for the data in the three panels. Is that consistent with your understanding of what ρ means? ⏎
A correlation coefficient quantifies the strength of linear relationship between two continuous variables.
Suppose that the mileage on a car is represented by X miles and its resale value by Y dollars. X and Y are
random variables since they vary from car to car, and if measured at different points in time. If we measure
the ordered pair values (x i ,y i ) for i = 1,…,n, cars independently of each other, then the correlation
coefficient between miles and resale values is given by:
–
∑ (x −x)(y −y)
ρ = i i i ,
–
√∑ (x −x)2√∑ (y −y)2
i i i i
–
where x,y are the sample means of mileage (x) and resale price (y), respectively.
The value of a correlation coefficient can proved to be between -1 and 1; see [35] for proof. A value of +1
means perfect positive linear relationship, that is, for every increase of one unit in X, Y increases by β where
β is a constant. Similarly, a value of -1 implies a perfect negative linear relationship. A value of ρ close to 0
implies the lack of a linear relationship, i.e., when X increases, Y may increase, decrease or stay the same.
The absence of a linear relationship does not exclude other types of relationships, for example quadratic or
exponential.

The correlation coefficient ρ between Perimeter and Compactness, Length of Kernel and Asymmetry
Coefficient is respectively, 0.5292436, 0.9724223, -0.2173404. Are the correlation values consistent with
the data shown in the three scatterplots in Figure 7.8?

7.6.2 Line of best fit: Ordinary Least Square regression
We are often interested in predicting the value of Y for a given value of X. For example, the resale value of a
car based on its mileage, or length of a wheat seed kernel based on its perimeter. Y is called the dependent
variable, and X is called the independent variable. Compact mathematical functions, e.g., that of a straight
line, are often used to express the relationship between the two variables. Consider the line:
y = α+β∗x,
(7.5)
where α represents the intercept on the Y-axis, and β represents the slope of the line.
There could potentially be many lines that pass through the data, corresponding to different values of α
and β. If you think of a straight line twirling through the data with any given point as a fulcrum, then the
line that is closest to most points is considered the line of best fit.
Note that the points in the second panel of Figure 7.8, center, are close to the line but not exactly on the
line. That's because variables such as resale value of a car, dimensions of a wheat kernel are random
variables that vary from car to car, or seed to seed, and are subject to variability caused by chance including
measurement errors. The variability due to chance is incorporated into the line of best fit by adding an error
component, ϵ:
y = α+β∗x+ϵ
(7.6)
The regression line represents the line of best fit. If you draw a line from x value on the X-axis parallel to
i
the Y-axis, the point
y^
i where it intersects the regression line is the predicted value of the dependent variable
Y for the given x value. That is:
i
y^ = α+β∗x
i i
The difference between the predicted values and the observed data value is called a residual denoted by:
r = y −y^.
i i i
If the line of best fit approximates the relationship between Y and X well, then the residuals are expected to
be small. The line of best fit minimizes
∑r2.
i
i
This method of finding the line of best fit is called Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) because it minimizes the
sum of squares of the residuals,
r = y −y^.
i i i
The line in Equation 7.6 is called the regression line. The book Introduction to Linear Regression Analysis
by Montgomery et al. [31] provides a comprehensive discussion of the topic.
Figure 7.9, left, depicts a regression line for the relationship between acceleration (time in seconds from 0
to 100 km/h) and the top speed of Electric Vehicles (EV). It was created with Excel using the publicly
available EV data set on kaggle (cid:0). Each individual observation (x i ,y i ) represents the ordered pair of
acceleration and top speed measurements, and it is depicted as a dot on the plot. We observe that:
α = 280.95
β = −13.97.

Figure 7.9, left, shows the residual for two points. For point A whose coordinates are (x = 3,y = 210),
the predicted value is:
y^= α+β∗x = 280.95+13.97∗(3) = 239.04.
Therefore, the residual for point A is:
y−y^= 210−239.04 = −29.4.
Similarly, the residual for point B is 22.23. The greater the residual, the farther a point is from the regression
line. Points that have abnormally large residuals are outliers with respect to the regression line. Anomaly
detection involves finding outliers with respect to models that fit the data well.
Data Debate 7.6.3 Are the signs of the residuals reasonable? Why?
7.6.3 Relationship between correlation (ρ) and slope (β)
The slope of the linear regression line is related to the correlation coefficient in the following way.
s
y
β = ρ ,
s
x
where s y ,s x are the sample standard deviations of x and y. The greater the correlation coefficient, the
stronger the relationship between y and x, and the steeper the line of best fit. For every unit change in x, y
will change by β units.
The purpose of using a mathematical model like the regression line in Equation 7.6 is to express the
relationship between x and y in a compact manner. We could represent hundreds of thousands of wheat
kernel measurements with the regression line, and predict the kernel length based on the perimeter using
that compact equation and be assured that the margin of error will be within the scope of a Standard Normal
distribution.
There are other ways of determining the line of best fit, e.g., orthogonal regression where the sum of the
squares of the perpendicular distances to the regression line is minimized, taking into account errors in both
x and y values. In contrast, simple linear regression takes into account just the errors in y values. However,
simple linear regression as described above remains the most popular and intuitive.
Data Debate 7.6.4 In Figure 7.9, what would it mean for a data point to be an “outlier”? Can you identify
two of them?
Different types of mathematical functional relationships are used to represent the dependence of Y on X,
not just linear relationships. Quadratic, logarithmic and logit functions are common ways of capturing
complex relationships between variables, both categorical and continuous. A rigorous introduction to
generalized linear models is provided in McCullogh and Nelder's Generalized Linear Models [29]. Other
regression models use different functions in different regions of the attribute space called piecewise or
segmented or spline regression models. Deep learning networks create dense multi-layered functions that
can capture more complex relationships in a empirical manner from the data.
7.6.4 Beware: Correlation is not causation
A word of caution about correlation. It is not causation, just a co-occurrence of pairs of values of two
variables. Sometimes it could be just chance that two variables have a similar trend, e.g., according to
Figure 7.10, the number of Google searches for Taylor Swift and the use of fossil fuel in British Virgin
Islands, have a correlation of 0.902! Tyler Vigen's Spurious Correlations contains hundreds of such
examples of nonsensical correlations and explains why these are spurious. The fallacy here is the coupling
of the two variables into ordered pairs based on time. Any two variables that trend similarly over time, e.g.,
first name Tyler and the amount of gasoline pumped in Italy, will exhibit high correlation.
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.10: Tyler Vigen's website Spurious Correlations contains hundreds of examples of spurious
correlations, and explanations of why they are suspect. Left: Time series of fossil fuel use in the British
Virgin Islands and the number of Google searches for “Taylor Swift” during 2006 - 2021. Right: Scatterplot
of the same data. ⏎
While some spurious correlations are due to chance, others might be driven by a hidden variable, called a
confounding factor that influences the two variables separately. A common example of this is the correlation
between ice cream sales and drownings. They are positively correlated, but this does not that mean that an
increase in ice cream sales causes more people to drown. There is another factor (confounding variable) that
has to be taken into account: high temperature. When it is hot, more people go swimming, and this can lead
to drownings; and when it is hot, more people buy ice cream.
Learn more about correlation and causation by exploring Tyler Vigen's Spurious Correlations website and
GeminiBox 7.6.2.
GeminiBox 7.6.2: Correlation and Causation ⏎
“What is the difference between correlation and causation?” (cid:0)
7.7 Time and Space Are Special
Time and space are special types of continuous variables because proximity in time and/or space has a
strong influence on outcomes. For example, areas close to a wildfire are at a higher risk than those far away.
Similarly, the closer the date of an important event like a wedding or a critical examination, the greater the
levels of stress. Air traffic controllers monitor aircraft both in time and space to avoid collisions caused by
two planes that are close together in both time and space.
Data that are tracked over time are called temporal, longitudinal or time-dependent. Data that are
measured across space are called spatial data. Spatial data can be geospatial data that refers specifically to
the earth's surface, e.g., latitudes and longitudes, or any type of location or positional information, e.g., on
the human body, on a mechanical system, or on an image or video. Spatio-temporal data, like the ones
monitored by air traffic controllers, are measured across both space and time.
Temporal and spatial data are complex types of data objects that require specific formats and data
structures for efficient analytics. However, they are an essential part of statistics and AI, for studying real-
world phenomena like hurricanes, stock markets and self-driving cars. They entail extensive study. In this
chapter, we will introduce the concepts of temporal, spatial and spatio-temporal data, and study motivating
examples connected to real-world AI applications that use these three types of data. The Analysis of Time
Series: An Introduction [8] by Chris Chatfield provides a thorough introduction to time series. Statistics for
Spatial Data [11] by Noel Cressie covers the topic of spatial data exhaustively.
7.7.1 Time-dependent data
Many characteristics and variables change with time. Our vital signs like blood pressure and heart rate, the
position of the sun in the sky, and stock prices all vary continuously. Being able to monitor and potentially
predict them is a critical task and spans multiple applications in medicine, business, ocean science,
astronomy, sports and entertainment. Time as a variable requires special considerations because:
Proximity in time: Observations taken close together in time tend to resemble each other.
Time is cyclical in the sense that certain patterns repeat: the clock shows 3 AM daily, Mondays occur
every seven days, the winter solstice happens once a year, and so on, resulting in certain events to repeat
systematically, e.g., ebb and flow of tides, commuters on trains, and holiday-related shopping volumes.
Many events have a temporal signature that helps in forecasting extreme events. For example, hurricanes
are preceded by a build-up of wind and pressure, and they are followed by a big drop-off.
For these reasons, variables that change with time need a special framework.
A time-dependent variable is denoted by y, where the subscript t represents time at which the observation
t
y was made. For example, the following measurements represent daily values of Apple stock, from kaggle's
DJIA 30 stock time series data, (cid:0) shown in Table 7.2. The first column represents time, which is measured in
days. There are five variables that change with time: opening price for that day, high, low, closing price and
number of shares traded that day.
TABLE 7.2: Daily stock price data for Apple's stock ticker AAPL from January 3,
2006 to January 26, 2006. ⏎

Data Debate 7.7.1 Is the stock ticker name AAPL time-dependent? Do values of Open change gradually
over time? How about Volume? Are there gaps in the time variable, Date? Why?
There are many classes of time-dependent variables that do not change systematically or predictably with
time. Some, called stochastic processes, change in a probabilistic way. For example, the way a helium
balloon drifts once released into the air outdoors. A First Course in Stochastic Processes by Karlin and
Taylor [25] provides a great introduction for students with a math background. Yet another class of time-
dependent variables is data streams, e.g., the number of clicks per second on a popular app. Their behavior
changes with time but in ways that cannot be captured by compact statistical models. Data-driven
approaches like histograms are used to capture such instances. Time-dependent data, also known as
longitudinal data, is a vast topic, and we will present only a brief overview of traditional time series.
Analysis of Longitudinal Data by Peter Diggle et al. [14] provides a rigorous introduction for students with
a math background.
7.7.1.1 Illustrative example: Spacecraft telemetry and AI
NASA launches spacecraft regularly to understand the universe, e.g., to analyze the composition of planets
and other space objects. Figure 7.11, left, shows the solar probe Parker, formerly known as Probe Plus. It
was launched in 2018 and entered the sun's atmosphere in 2021, sending back a treasure of previously
unseen data. Such spacecrafts collect enormous amounts of data called telemetry to track their own
functioning, e.g., variables such as temperature of various components, pressure, performance indicators of
computing systems, fuel levels, loads on various systems and location coordinates. Continuously tracking
such variables enables engineers to flag any anomalies, e.g., high temperatures or stress on systems, and
take preventive action before the occurrence of catastrophic failures. According to an article, Detecting
anomalies in spacecraft telemetry (cid:0), published by Aerospace organization:
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.11: Left: Artist's rendering of the Parker Solar Probe (formerly Solar Probe Plus) launched in
2018 that plunged into the solar atmosphere in 2021 and sent back data that had never been collected before.
The progress of such probes is tracked through telemetry data that continuously monitors health and
functioning of the probes to detect anomalies early and ensure mission success. AI methods are increasingly
used to handle the staggering amounts of time series data generated and collected by spacecraft. (Source:
NASA Goddard Space Flight Center from Greenbelt, MD, USA, CC BY 2.0.) Right: The number of objects
launched into space has increased exponentially. (Source: Our World in Data.) ⏎
The volume of data returned by spacecrafts continues to increase with missions such as the NASA-Indian
Space Research Organization Synthetic Aperture Radar that will generate about 85 terabytes of data per
day. Spacecraft telemetry is inherently time-series data, and many anomaly detection approaches which
exist today lend themselves well to this type of data.
As the number of space objects grows as shown in Figure 7.11, right, AI becomes critical in managing the
safety and operational integrity of these spacecrafts by monitoring telemetry time series data.
7.7.1.2 Illustrative example: NOAA's weather forecasting
Average temperatures are classic examples of time series, as seen in Figure 7.12, which represent average
monthly temperatures in Florida. Notice:
1. The values of the average temperature rise and fall in a cyclical pattern, hitting 90's in summer and
falling to 70's in the winter. This is called seasonality.
2. The blue dots are the actual values of the temperatures each month which vary depending on factors
unique to that year and month, e.g., wild fires, unusual ocean currents due to glaciers melting, and other
unexpected phenomena. Years 2017 and 2023 had unusually warm temperatures compared to other
years, and years 2018 and 2025 had unusually cool temperatures.
3. The red lines are smoothed values that represent expected values based on historical data. They tend to
be less variable from year to year since they are averages over long time windows of multiple years.
4. The black dashed line is the trend line intended to show whether there is a drift in the values over a
period of time.
5. The differences between predicted (red) and actual (blue) values are called residuals and represent how
good the prediction is. Notice that years 2016, 2018 and 2025 have big residuals for the winter month
predictions.
FIGURE 7.12: Average monthly temperatures in Florida are provided by NOAA National Centers for
Environmental Information, Climate at a Glance: Statewide Time Series, published July 2025, retrieved on
July 25, 2025 from NOAA's Climate at a glance. The graphs and forecast were created using R. ⏎
Seasonality is helpful in anticipating sales of seasonal items such as swimsuits and skis, in predicting
temperatures and rainfall, and by airline industry for preparing for a holiday travel surge. Astronomers and
space scientists rely on repetitive orbits of space objects to plan their experiments and studies.
Many time series exhibit a drift over time. Global ocean temperatures are rising, recreational travel is
increasing and the incidence of many diseases are on the decline thanks to vaccines. This drift in one
direction over time is called trend. Time series like those in Figure 7.13, left, do not have a seasonal
component but show a very strong trend. Some financial indicators have declined over time (“Non-
performing loans as % total gross loans”), while others have increased (“Regulatory Tier 1 capital to risk-
weighted assets”) or stayed steady (“Regulatory capital to assets”).
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.13: Time series with trend. Left: Financial soundness indicators. Right: Expansion in cropland
by region. ⏎
Figure 7.13, right, shows that cropland expanded as agriculture became the engine of growth initially, and
then reached a steady state with industrialization or when there was no more room left for expansion. Africa
shows a rapid gain while countries like India have reached a steady state as most of the land has been used
up. Highly industrialized nations like the USA see a shrinkage of cropland over time. This kind of layered
visualization of time series enables comparison of different groups over time while also keeping track of the
total.
It is clear from these examples that time series applications routinely address numerous real-world
situations where change is frequent, and monitoring it is essential to provide accurate responses e.g., in
hurricane warning systems or in monitoring the economic productivity of nations.
7.7.2 Time series
A time series y in its simplest form can be represented mathematically as a combination of three
t
components: seasonality, trend and residuals which are what remains after the seasonality and trend have
been subtracted from the observed data. For example,
y = S +T +ϵ,
t t t
(7.7)
where the subscript t indicates the time instant at which the measurement of the time series variable y is
t
taken, S represents the seasonal component at time t, T represents the trend at time t, and ϵ is what remains,
t t
usually random noise if the model is a good fit for the data. Such a representation of a time series as
components that are added (or combined in other ways) is called a decomposition. There are many software
tools available for this purpose, including R. Time Series Analysis and Its Applications: With R Examples by
Robert H. Shumway and David S. Stoffer [39] provides an excellent hands-on introduction to time series.
To understand Equation 7.7, consider Figure 7.14, a time series corresponding to monthly average sea
levels. We present a very high-level explanation of time series decomposition.
The peaks and troughs of the dark blue line (3-month running mean) represent changes in sea levels
during the course of a year as they rise and fall with the seasons. A peak and the corresponding trough
constitute a one-year cycle. This is the seasonal component S in Equation 7.7 that repeats in a broadly
t
similar way year after year.
However, while the seasonal pattern is present, there has been an increase in the sea levels year to year
evidenced by the fact that each year's peak is roughly higher than the previous year's with a few
exceptions here and there. This upward drift of the time series is the trend portion T of the time series,
t
represented by the red trend line.
Finally, the actual monthly average is the cyan line. The gap between the cyan and the blue line at any
time t constitutes the error ϵ of the time series.
t
Extended Description
FIGURE 7.14: The development of global sea level from 1992 to 2020 with seasonal variations.
Measurements have been conducted via satellites TOPEX/Poseidon, Jason-1 and Jason-2. The trend line
clearly shows the constant increase in global sea level of 3.5 ± 0.4 mm/year since the 1990s. Image credit:
Geek3. ⏎
These three together constitute the time series, and represent the individual components of the
decomposition.
The objective of constructing a time series model like the one in Equation 7.7 is to be able to describe the
time series in a compact form without listing all the individual values. If the model is a “good fit”, then the
residuals will be very small and exhibit no pattern, just random noise that follows a Standard Normal
distribution.
Equation 7.7 is useful for forecasting future values of a time series. Figure 7.12 shows the forecasted
values of average temperatures in Florida based on historical data from 2023 to 2025. The forecasted values
were created using R. The forecast is shown as a solid line, with the 80% and 99% confidence regions
highlighted in shades of gray.
Data Debate 7.7.2 In Figure 7.12, what is the proper way to interpret the shaded confidence regions? Of
the two nested gray regions, which one corresponds to 80% confidence and which one to 99%?
Forecasting plays an important role in NOAA's hurricane predictions. NOAA focuses its research on new
models and algorithms, data gathering techniques, and innovative instrumentation to predict wind speeds
and precipitation amounts. Such forecasts help create timely alerts and evacuations to better protect life and
property. Read about NOAA's hurricane forecast research in Section 7.8.
7.7.3 Spatial data
Geographic data, also called geospatial data, are specified through coordinates such as latitudes and
longitudes, e.g., the public schools location data sample shown Table 7.3, where the last two columns
contain the latitude (LAT) and longitude (LON), respectively. We rely on geospatial data daily when we use
our car's GPS to drive from point A to point B. Geospatial data, which refers particularly to the surface of
the earth, is used extensively to study phenomena like wildfires, earthquakes, deforestation and animal
migration. Spatial data in general can refer to any data that identifies a position or location, e.g., inside an
organ like the brain which can be viewed via medical imaging.
TABLE 7.3: Example of spatial data: locations of public schools are specified by latitude and longitude, the
last two columns. Published by National Center for Education Statistics. ⏎
NAME STATE CITY ZIP NMCNTY LAT LON
Albertville Middle School AL Albertville 35950 Marshall County 34.2602 -86.2062
Albertville High School AL Albertville 35950 Marshall County 34.2622 -86.2049
Albertville Intermediate School AL Albertville 35950 Marshall County 34.2733 -86.2201
Albertville Elementary School AL Albertville 35950 Marshall County 34.2527 -86.2218
Spatial data, as well as spatio-temporal data in Section 7.7.4, require highly complex analytics which are
beyond the scope of this book. For our purpose, it is enough to understand where such data arise and where
important AI applications are used.
Data Debate 7.7.3 What makes spatial data special?
Demographic studies use geospatial data extensively. Consider Figure 7.15, right, created by the Federal
Reserve Bank of New York. It is an interactive map that shows the change in home prices in counties across
the USA. Check out this link (cid:0) to explore counties across the country and identify how real estate prices
changed across different regions in the USA over time. Unusual events like the COVID-19 pandemic create
abnormal patterns as people flee densely packed urban areas for homes in the open countryside.
FIGURE 7.15: Spatial data is used in AI applications ranging from detecting wildfires to studying real
estate markets. Left: The Advanced Land Imager (ALI) on NASA's Earth Observing-1 (EO-1) satellite
captured this image of the Bastrop County Complex Fire in southern Texas on September 12, 2011. Right:
Changes in house prices in June 2025 by county in Northeastern USA. Source: (cid:0), Mapping home prices:
Federal Reserve Bank of New York. ⏎
Like time, proximity in space influences neighboring regions. Areas close to a wildfire are at a much
higher risk than areas far away. Similarly, when epidemics spread, areas close to the center of the epidemic
are at much higher risk of infection. For this reason, spatial data requires a special type of models. AI has
accelerated the use of this complex data form, leading to applications in wildfire detection and management,
dynamic real-time real estate applications like Zillow, automated medical image screening, and astronomy.
7.7.3.1 Illustrative example: Prithvi GeoAI models for earth sciences
GeoAI refers to specialized AI models built for geospatial data. Such data can be voluminous and complex.
NASA uses its Prithvi AI model, built in collaboration with IBM, to fill in gaps in satellite imagery caused
by cloud cover, to distinguish between flood water and permanent water on the earth's surface to enable
flood water management, to monitor crops on agricultural land, and to map scars caused by wildfires in
order to direct recovery efforts. Figure 7.15, left, shows the burn scar of a wild fire in Texas. GeoAI studies
such images to train AI algorithms to detect burn scars to monitor habitability and recovery.
Data Debate 7.7.4 What type of data would you need to collect to create a figure such as the one in Figure
7.15, right?
7.7.4 Spatio-temporal data
Monitoring events across space and time is essential for many tasks, e.g., spread of wildfires and
floodwaters, movement of birds and animals, propagation of misinformation across a social network, and
flow of vehicular traffic. Such data have both a spatial and temporal component and are called spatio-
temporal data. Analyses of rocket trajectories, paths of autonomous cars, flight paths, and migration of
herds across the Serengeti in monsoon are all examples of spatio-temporal data and entail studying the
effect of proximity in space and time. Spatio-temporal data tend be extremely complex and need special
types of models and data structures that are beyond the scope of this book. We focus on the applications of
such data in real-world examples.
7.7.4.1 Illustrative example: NOAA tracks hurricanes with AI
Consider Figure 7.16 which shows the tracks of category 1, 2 and 3 hurricanes in Florida. The left panel
shows the tracks observed over the twenty-year period between 2004 and 2023. The panel on the right
shows the tracks of the year 2024. These tracks are classic examples of spatio-temporal data that represent
movement in time over a spatial region. They are called trajectories. The figures were created using the
Historical Hurricane Tracks Viewer provided by climate.gov to explore historical hurricanes and their
trajectories.
FIGURE 7.16: Tracks of Category 1, 2, and 3 hurricanes in Florida over the 20-year period 2004-2023.
Right: Tracks of Category 1, 2, and 3 hurricanes in Florida, 2024. From Historical Hurricane Tracks Viewer,
climate.gov. (cid:0) ⏎
What do you notice about the tracks of 2024? Do you see their reach into the Gulf of America, formerly
known as Gulf of Mexico? Was that observed in the past 20 years? Would you have expected that track
based on the preceding 20 years? Is that trajectory an anomaly?
NOAA conducts extensive research in the use of AI for monitoring the climate and environment. The
organization has developed an innovative system called Warn-on-Forecast (WoFS) shown in Figure 7.17,
left, to forecast severe weather. WoFS is designed to make probabilistic predictions using the latest radar
and satellite data. Read the article in Section 7.8 and watch this YouTube video (cid:0) from NOAA about WoFS
advanced technology that helps save lives and property.

7.7.4.2 Illustrative example: AI and path planning for autonomous cars
For a driverless vehicle to function properly, its AI-driven software has to accurately recognize different
types of data such as images, movement, traffic signals, weather conditions, pedestrians and other vehicles
as shown in Figure 7.17, right, and generate a path for it. This requires generating many possible paths and
finding the most efficient route with the least risk. There are special AI tools called path finding algorithms
that create these automatically. The paths are examples of spatio-temporal data which specify a series of
locations and times.
Spatio-temporal data tend to be very challenging and voluminous with many types of dependencies. They
require advanced AI tools. Learn more about them by exploring the prompt in GeminiBox 7.7.1.

8
Data Quality and AI
AI is built on data. It learns how to distinguish between cats and dogs
by poring over hundreds of thousands of images labeled as cats or dogs.
GenAI learns how to write novels by studying millions of sentences and
articles. Customer service chatbots are trained on thousands of
transcripts of conversations between customers and customer support
representatives. What if cat photos were mislabeled as lions? What if
the novel-writing bot was trained on stories written by babies picking
out words at random from a box? What if the customer service
transcripts were full of hateful words? What if AI was trained on
images shown in Figure 8.1? AI would simply learn and repeat these
errors. While algorithms and tools have advanced, the data used by AI
is only now getting the attention it deserves. In this chapter, we will
learn about different types of data quality issues, how they arise, how
they are measured, consequences of fixing them, and AI-based
solutions.
FIGURE 8.1: Data quality issues can derail AI. Left: Bad data can be
incomprehensible and detrimental to automation. (Photo: US Government
National Archives catalog.) Right: Data can be misleading and incorrect,
and potentially harmful if used to train AI. (Photo: Wikipedia,
Andrewj0131.) ⏎
Consider the following hypothetical situation: you need to finish an
assignment in a hurry, but you cannot find the relevant file because you had
labeled it as “job search” instead of “homework.” Your friend sends a copy
over but only the first page of the scan shows up. Annoyed, you send out a
text demanding he resend it, only to find that your friend's answer is utter
gibberish, as if his smartphone could not decipher what he was saying.
When you finally receive the entire assignment from him after some back
and forth, you discover that the assignment had 20 questions, not the usual
5. You panic, since 20 questions would take you the whole day, but you
have only 2 hours before the deadline. You check the file carefully and
realize that each question had been repeated four times for some odd
reason, but because there were no question numbers, you could not tell that
they were duplicates until after reading through them.
A further evaluation of the situation shows that it was caused by the
confluence of several types of data quality issues. The first data quality
issue was incorrect labels, i.e., mislabeled files that made the data hard to
find, i.e., inaccessible. The second data glitch was incomplete data, when
only the first page of the assignment showed up. The third data issue was
the incomprehensible text message from your friend, a result of bad
transcription, but human and software errors can result in such mangled
messages as well. The fourth problem was data duplication, which could
have happened if your friend resent the assignment multiple times. Finally,
the lack of metadata (question numbers) masked the duplication. Each data
quality problem came about in a different way, requiring a different fix.
This is what makes data quality challenging, because it is highly context-
dependent making it difficult to find general solutions that can be
automated and applied widely.
However, given how critical data is to AI, it is important to develop
intuition for data quality issues, to spot problems, and to evaluate solutions.
This chapter focuses on different types of data quality issues and how they
arise, methods to detect them and quantify their effect, and to clean data and
prevent the occurrence of data glitches in downstream data pipelines used
by AI applications.
AI pioneers such as Andrew Ng believe that for AI to achieve its true
potential, it is necessary to focus on the data that drives AI, in a systematic
manner. Read his article Why it's time for data-centric artificial intelligence
in Section 8.5.
While AI, machine learning algorithms, and statistical models consume
data, they can also be used to ensure the quality of data. AI can monitor data
pipelines, flag data that does not conform to rules and expectations, identify
instances of non-compliance to standards, and apply models to clean and
validate the data.
8.1 Data Quality: Has your data gone bad?
Between the time it is created and the time it is used by AI applications,
data passes through many processing stages. It originates in multiple places,
e.g., gathered by sensors on different ocean buoys, is transmitted to data
centers, integrated with other data such as weather measurements and
topological features, processed to precise specifications, and finally made
available to AI applications for use. Each stage might use different
hardware and software environments, different procedures and processes
with different error tolerances, resulting in a heterogeneous set of rules
applied to the data. In addition to the errors that could impact data quality
within each stage, the hand-offs between processing stages also contribute
to data quality problems, e.g., when data are dropped due to incompatibility
between two stages. It is important to understand how data quality issues
can arise at different stages of the data pipeline. We discuss some common
examples of data quality issues. However, the list is not exhaustive since
data issues continue to arise in novel ways, as AI enables creating,
collecting and storing new forms of data daily, some of them
comprehensible only to machines, e.g., encrypted data.
8.1.1 Data pipeline and data quality
Consider the schematic representation of a data pipeline shown in Figure
8.2. In the box labeled “Original Data”, each individual data point is
represented by a dot, where black dots are typical data points and an orange
dot is a genuine anomaly, e.g., unusually high wind speed.
During data collection, some measurements might be overlooked
resulting in missing data (white dot with dashed lines). Missing data can
have severe consequences, e.g., those related to healthcare and diagnoses
(Figure 8.3, left). Some might be misrecorded leading to artificial
anomalies (orange dots) which cannot be distinguished from authentic
data anomalies.
When data is transmitted, it can get mangled (black oval), for example,
“Doe” could be misspelled as “Doh”. Sometimes, data is retransmitted if
there is no acknowledgment that it has been received, causing
duplication of data (joined dots).
When data is ingested into a data repository for storage, some records
might be rejected (red dots) by the database depending on what
constraints have been built in, e.g., “negative values of distance are not
allowed,” or heuristics such as “fish cannot live outside water” or “riding
a bicycle needs legs.” The latter two constraints would result in rejection
of the image in Figure 8.3, right. Some legitimate data might be dropped
(white dot with dashed lines) if the storage limit is exceeded, resulting in
additional missing data.
While retrieving data, the database query might add some additional
constraints, e.g., “remove all occurrences of Doh”, inadvertently
resulting in legitimate “Dohs” in the data being dropped.
During the data-cleaning stage, missing values might get imputed (gray
dots), i.e., estimated because some AI applications cannot process
missing data. Similarly, some anomalies might be brought in-line with
typical values (gray dot with dashed orange line) so as not to distort the
results.
FIGURE 8.2: Data quality issues can arise at almost every point in the data
pipeline. Data measurements (black dots) might be missed during the
collection stage (white dot with dashed lines). Some might be misrecorded
leading to anomalies (orange dots). When the data is transmitted, it can get
mangled (black oval). Data might get duplicated if it is retransmitted.
During storage, data might be rejected (red dots) by the database. Some
data might be dropped (white dot with dashed lines) if there are storage
limits. While retrieving data, a query might inadvertently result in
additional data being dropped. During the cleaning stage, missing values
might get imputed (gray dots), and anomalies might be brought in-line with
typical values (gray dots with dashed orange line). By the time the
particular data is available for use by an AI application, it might have very
different statistical properties from the original data. Since AI and data
science is based on statistical and probabilistic principles, such statistical
distortion during the cleaning process needs to be closely monitored. This
figure was created using Microsoft PowerPoint. ⏎
FIGURE 8.3: Missing values can severely impact healthcare, e.g.,
incomplete medical history and its impact on clinical diagnosis. Left: NGOs
like India's Ayushman Bharat use AI to manage data and deliver quality
healthcare to remote regions and underserved communities. (Photo: Ganesh
Dhamodkar.) Right: Inconsistencies in data can throw off the best AI
algorithms. What if images like this one from the Guinness Storehouse in
Dublin were used to train autonomous cars? (Photo: Nga Ahorangi.) ⏎
By the time the processed data is available for use by an AI application, it
might have very different statistical properties than the original data. The
question then becomes: “Does the processed data available to an AI
application have similar statistical characteristics as the original data
collected from the real world?” Since AI and data science are based on
statistical and probabilistic principles, such statistical distortion that
happens during the cleaning process needs to be closely monitored. This
topic is discussed in Section 8.4 and [13].
Data Debate 8.1.1 Data cleaning changes data. Given this fact, should you
clean data, and if so, how much?
Data Debate 8.1.2 Can you think of an example of data quality issues in
sports that could mislead AI?
Data Debate 8.1.3 What would happen if GPS navigation software were
trained on outdated maps?
8.2 Monsters in Your Data: Types of data glitches
Data can be of poor quality for many reasons. For example, a surveyor
might collect data from nearby regions since there might not be enough
time to travel too far. A clinical study might drop data deemed negative,
e.g., adverse side effects of a new drug. Software might fail, resulting in
large chunks of sensor data not being recorded. All these situations result in
different types of data quality issues, with different consequences, and
necessitating different remedies. It is this variety that makes data quality
challenging since data quality problems, their consequences and subsequent
remedies are very closely tied to a particular application and do not
generalize.
Classifying, quantifying and fixing data quality errors is an important
part of ensuring that AI applications are trained on high-quality data. We
start by describing some common types of glitches.
8.2.1 Missing and mangled data
Missing values are some of the most common causes of poor data quality.
Imagine if half your test grades went missing and were automatically set to
0 by a grading software. How would that impact your final grade?
Misspellings like the ones shown in Figure 8.1, left, are an example of
mangled data. They not only make data incomprehensible, but also they can
have serious consequences. In a medical database, if “Helen” is misspelled
as “Ellen”, it could make it hard to locate medical records in an emergency
where the delay of every second could mean the difference between life and
death.
FIGURE 8.4: The United States Postal Service department sends back
letters and packages if addresses are incomplete or wrong.
Data Debate 8.2.1 AI tools can correct spellings and offer suggestions for
completing a sentence. What would happen if the training data were full of
typos and misspellings?
8.2.1.1 Illustrative example: Healthcare, missing data and AI
In healthcare, missing values arise in different ways. Sometimes, lab reports
go missing, either due to software problems, or actual hard copy records
being misplaced. On occasion, the patient is a no-show. In such cases, there
are gaps in the patient's medical history that could affect clinical decisions.
For example, an incomplete medical history or an incomplete list of
patient's medications could have serious consequences if the doctor is not
aware of the missing conditions or medicines. The treatment the doctor
would prescribe based on incomplete data could potentially conflict with
those missing conditions and could have serious health consequences for
the patient. AI-powered tools offer solutions by using advanced techniques
to impute or fill in missing data, or even predicting no-shows so that
patients could be reminded of upcoming appointments.
GeminiBox 8.2.1: Missing values
“How do missing values impact healthcare? How can AI help?” (cid:0)
India has instituted data interoperability projects that use AI to ensure the
flow of health data across systems, while preserving patient privacy for its
1.4 billion citizens. Read about how the world's most populous country uses
AI to advance healthcare by preserving and digitizing data, and ensuring
data integrity, in Section 8.5.
Several non-profit organizations leverage open-source AI platforms to
deliver healthcare to remote areas and underserved populations. AI is
particularly good at focused repetitive tasks. Healthcare companies use AI
agents to tackle and expedite routine tasks like medical transcription, to
summarize case histories to shortlist diagnoses for the doctor to check, and
refill medications, ensuring that data is recorded in a timely manner and
updated constantly, thus ensuring data quality.
As another example of missing data, consider the following paragraph
that is missing some words, and other words are jumbled and garbled. This
happens in the real world quite often, e.g., when speech-to-text AI on
smartphones encounters unfamiliar accents that it has not been trained to
recognize.
Example 8.2.1.
Geetha likes to go to the brksfh and wefr a
⏎
bxyk.
Unless it rains, she pekudqs to stay in
her and drwqk a cup of
Data Debate 8.2.2 Is this paragraph usable as is? Can you guess what the
original version is? Does your best guess for a correct version of the
paragraph match with those of your classmates? What's the implication of
different versions of the reconstructed data?
The correct answer is: “Geetha likes to go to the beach and read a book.
Unless it rains, in which case she prefers to stay in her room and drink a cup
of tea.”
AI models are used to detect less obvious types of missing values too.
For example, values used as a default, such as “January 1, 1970” for dates,
occur with a much greater frequency than others. There are several reasons
for this. For example, some software tools auto-populate defaults, e.g.,
“000-000-0000” for phone numbers, since some applications fail when they
encounter blanks in the data. Such defaults are called hidden missing values
because it is not always obvious that the values are just stand-ins. Unusual
spikes in frequencies of values are often a hint that the value could be a
default. It is surprising how many people were born on January 1, 1970!
Check out GeminiBox 8.2.2 to see what makes this date so popular.
Sometimes, it is not possible to tell whether values are missing. Consider
Figure 8.5 with its three panels. It is obvious that parts of the image are
missing in the middle panel. However, if the original image on the left was
unavailable, how would we know that the image in the right panel was
incomplete?
FIGURE 8.5: Incomplete data is a subtle form of missing data, where it is
not obvious that data might be missing. ⏎
Other forms of hidden missing values include data imputed during
cleaning by filling in blanks with typical values like the mean. The data
looks complete, with legitimate-seeming data. While this is helpful to some
applications, others that rely on probability distributions might produce
biased results if there is a prevalence of imputed values. We discuss below.
GeminiBox 8.2.2: January 1, 1970 ⏎
“What is special about the date January 1, 1970?” (cid:0)
Missing values need to be addressed so that they are not propagated
through AI data pipelines corrupting downstream AI models. Missing data
could cause software to crash while performing certain mathematical
operations. A worse scenario is that it could produce misleading or even
disastrous results. For example, what if all the data from a particular set of
sensors, like cameras on a robot, was missing?
8.2.1.2 Cleaning missing data
There are several ways of handling missing data. The simplest approach is
to drop records where data values are not populated. Is that a good solution?
Would we know about Geetha's preferences if that entire paragraph in
Example 8.2.1 were to be dropped from a customer preference survey?
What if large chunks of the data values are missing?
Data Debate 8.2.3 When could excluding records with missing values be a
problem?
Another option for treating missing data is to fill in missing data with
usable values. This process is called imputation. We have already discussed
the use of default values for qualitative variables, e.g., “000-000-0000” for
phone numbers, or “John Doe” for a person whose name is not known.
To get an intuition for missing value imputation, consider the
hypothetical example in Figure 8.6. The table on the left shows three
attributes for six individuals: Name, Gender and Age. The three blanks
denote missing values. The table in the middle shows the data after the
values have been imputed.
The third record is missing gender. We use our knowledge of the real
world, that “Nina” is usually associated with the female gender. So we
fill in the blank for “Gender” attribute for this record with the value “F”.
This is a heuristic approach to imputing values, since we use common
sense to make a quick guess.
We fill in the name in the fourth record using a rule or convention for
missing names: “Jane Doe”, for a female whose name is unknown.
We fill in the weight in the third record based on the mean of all
available values:
(144 + 135 + 125 + 132 + 116)
= 130.4.
5
Note that the denominator is 5, and not 6, since dividing by 6 would
imply including the missing value in the computation by setting its value
to 0.
The table on the right imputes a different value to Tom's weight by using
only those weights that correspond to men.
Extended Description
FIGURE 8.6: Missing values in a data table; age imputed based on
everyone; age imputed based only on men. ⏎
(144 + 135)
= 139.5.
2
That is, additional information from another attribute “Gender” is used
to refine the imputation of Tom's weight.
We summarize our three methods of imputing values for missing data:
Rule-based: For qualitative variables, use rules based on heuristics,
convention or empirical frequency distributions. For example, a
frequency distribution of gender for the name “Nina” would presumably
be 99% female.
Typical values: Missing values of quantitative values are substituted
with typical values like the mean or the median based on the available
non-missing values.
Based on attribute relationships and simulated distributions: Impute
a value to a missing attribute based on the known value of another
attribute. For example, impute a value to Tom's missing weight based on
Tom's gender by using the mean weight of men. More sophisticated
models based on larger data sets could use non-missing values to create
an empirical probability distribution, and impute missing values by
drawing values at random from the conditional distribution of weight
given that the gender is “M”.
Model- and AI-based: Values can be imputed by creating a predictive
model, e.g., linear regression or even neural nets, for the variable with
missing values using the remaining variables and their non-missing
values.
AI uses language models to fill in missing text. Predicting future missing
values, e.g., probability of a patient missing an appointment, is another way
AI helps in anticipating missing values, and to take preventive action by
nudging patients with reminders. Use the prompt in GeminiBox 8.2.3 to do
a deep dive into missing value imputation.
GeminiBox 8.2.3: Missing value imputation ⏎
“How are missing values imputed?” (cid:0)
8.2.2 Incomplete data
Incomplete data is a special form of missing data. It is often more insidious
since it is often not obvious that data is incomplete, unlike overtly missing
values which are blanks such as those in Example 8.2.1 and Figure 8.6, left.
Figure 8.5 is an example of the difference between missing and incomplete
data. The original image contains three plumeria flowers. Due to a software
malfunction, parts of the image got obscured resulting in missing pixels as
shown in the center image. AI techniques could be used to fill in the
missing parts and reconstruct an approximate image.
On the other hand, the image on the right shows just the top-left corner of
the original image. Without prior knowledge, there is no reason to suspect
that the image is incomplete. It is possible that the photographer chose to
focus just on the single petal. Furthermore, it is not possible to reconstruct
the original image from just the fragment. However, the original might exist
on the photographer's camera, or on any public social media site the
photographer might have uploaded it to. AI tools, e.g., Google Lens, could
potentially comb through publicly available images and find the original
image to reconstruct the fragment.
Incomplete data could lead to serious problems. For example, what if
your grade was based on half the questions you answered on a test? Or,
doctors diagnosed and treated diseases based only on half of the symptoms
or medical history? There are numerous examples of incomplete data that
lead to failure. The article How poor data quality impacts AI and machine
learning in Section 8.5 has details about how poor data quality affected AI
in the following instances:
Autonomous cars were unable to recognize vehicles from unfamiliar
angles because the image data used for training was incomplete, and did
not have pictures of vehicles from every possible angle, e.g., from the
side, or turning a corner.
AI used to screen resumés at a large corporation was trained on the data
of existing employees and past applicants, predominantly men. As more
women majored in computer science, the number of qualified women
applicants exploded, but the training data remained incomplete and was
not updated. The AI resumé screener discarded resumés that had female-
sounding names or contained words that were “typically” associated
with women.
In another instance, automatic cleaning robots malfunctioned when their
training data failed to include a complete set of images of all potential
obstacles.
We discuss below the very real consequences of decisions based on
incomplete data.
8.2.2.1 Illustrative example: Incomplete data and hardship in the EU
Decisions based on incomplete data can seriously impact millions of lives
as happened in the European Union. In 2010, Harvard professors Reinhart
and Rogoff published a paper that stated that if the debt held by a country is
90% or more of its GDP, then economic growth will slow drastically. This
motivated countries, e.g., the UK, to impose severe austerity measures such
as cutting public welfare programs and raising taxes, inflicting significant
hardship on millions of people. However, it turns out that the results were
incorrect because the professors had inadvertently included only 15 rows of
data in their Excel spreadsheet instead of all 20 rows. Analysis based on the
entire data in the spreadsheet would have changed the conclusions
significantly. The mistake was spotted by a graduate student at University
of Massachusetts at Amherst, Thomas Herndon. You can read more by
clicking here (cid:0).
Greece, which had one of the highest debt to GDP ratio as shown in
Figure 8.7, was particularly devastated by the austerity measures which
resulted in scarcity of healthcare. Unemployment went up among young
people, leading to their mass exodus, leaving behind an aging population.
Some of these effects are thought to be irreversible, and are described here
(cid:0).
FIGURE 8.7: Incomplete data used by Harvard economists resulted in
incorrect conclusions about debt to GDP ratios that led to austerity
measures on countries like Greece that had high debt to GDP ratio. The
economic hardships caused irreversible damage to the Greek economy.
Subsequently, the findings of the study were reversed. ⏎
8.2.2.2 Cleaning incomplete data
Some important ways of avoiding and addressing incomplete data include:
Clearly define the data to be collected and maintained. For example,
suppose you need to create a data repository of students enrolled in
higher educational institutions in the world. For each enrollee, mandate
the collection of student name, year of enrollment, year of graduation,
college name, and college address. Other fields such as “Major” could be
specified as needed.
Collect and cross-validate from multiple independent data sources, e.g.,
gather information from county, state, national and international
organizations, e.g., International Association of Universities,
Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development and the World
Bank to create as complete a list of educational institutions and enrollees
as possible. Gathering data from multiple independent sources provides a
more complete picture since one source might have data the other one
missed or failed to collect.
Audit the data for validity and ensure that data are updated. For example,
repeat this activity regularly, say every week, and add new students that
enroll and new colleges that come into being. Failure to update and keep
data current is one of the biggest reasons data are incomplete.
AI is an effective tool for addressing incomplete data. AI agents can
continuously poll multiple sources of data for validation, as well as acquire
additional contextual information. For example, AI tools can search
multiple educational and government databases for enrollees and gather
other publicly available information about the college, e.g., the region
where the college is located, its financial information, and educational
specializations it is known for. Additional attributes and variables generally
make the data more informative, as well as provide opportunities for cross-
validation, e.g., storing both zip code and state will permit sanity checks
such as “if zip = 07976, then state = NJ”. Such checks could potentially
unearth data quality issues in other attributes as well.
Data Debate 8.2.4 How can you determine if a data set is incomplete?
8.2.3 Inconsistent data
What if you were to see a fish on a bicycle such as the one in Figure 8.3,
right? Or, you check the trajectory of GPS coordinates of your car, and find
that it is in Seattle at one moment, and in New York five minutes later? The
two preceding examples violate our understanding of the physical world
around us. Fish cannot survive outside water, nor do they have the
physiology needed to ride a bike. Similarly, given the current state of
transportation, it is impossible to travel around 3000 miles between Seattle
and New York in under 5minutes.
Inconsistent data could potentially have disastrous consequences if left
unchecked. Consider the following real-world example.
8.2.4 Illustrative example: Orbiter lost due to inconsistent
data
In 1999, the Mars Climate Orbiter launched by NASA was lost due to
improper data processing that resulted in internally inconsistent data. One
of the project teams used imperial units, while another team assumed that
the data was in metric units. When the data was passed from one team to
another, the conversion was overlooked and the orbiter's thrust was
miscalculated causing it to burn up in the atmospheres at a cost of hundreds
of millions of taxpayer dollars. This is an example of how an improper
understanding and interpretation of data could lead to internally inconsistent
data, with catastrophic results. For further details, click this link (cid:0).
8.2.4.1 Detecting inconsistent data
Inconsistent data issues are often detected by filtering the data through
predefined rules and format specifications, e.g., “social security numbers
must have 9 digits,” and “measurements of distance and duration should be
positive.” These rules are called constraints. Data that violates these
constraints is considered inconsistent. Constraints can be complex, designed
to capture the dependence between variables. For example, “if
zipcode=07976, then state=NJ.”
Data Debate 8.2.5 What would be a good constraint to check for
improbable cars that can travel 3000 miles in 5 minutes?
8.2.4.2 Cleaning inconsistent data
Data pipelines that feed AI applications require rigorous monitoring for
inconsistent data. Here are a few common-sense checks:
Ensure that all constraint checks are performed during data processing to
detect inconsistencies. Automated constraint checks during data
processing are often turned off because they could be computationally
expensive and time-consuming.
When data is updated or new sources are added, make sure that they are
consistent with existing data, e.g., attributes, formats, units of
measurement, and layout.
Continuously update time-dependent data. For example, using outdated
maps could trip up navigation systems.
AI can help with auditing data systems. While some rules are predefined by
experts, many can be discovered using AI tools which excel at detecting
inconsistent data. They not only flag data that violate rules and constraints,
but they can also learn constraints from the data itself by using probabilistic
reasoning. As a very simple example, if 99% of values of a variable have 9
digits, it will create a rule that says that the format of this variable must
have 9 digits, and flag the remaining 1% of values as inconsistent. This
reduces the amount of data that potentially needs to be screened by a
human.
8.2.5 Anomalies
Anomalies are a particular type of inconsistent data. They are data values
that are not aligned with the distribution of the rest of the data. Consider the
following example. Suppose that the grades of a student on their first four
tests are: A, A, A, D. The student insists that they did well on test No. 4, and
that “D” is an unexpected grade that needs to be investigated. The grade
“D” in this example is an anomaly because it is inconsistent with historical
values. A common explanation could be that the teacher mixed up the rows
in the spreadsheet while recording grades, and entered the next student's
grade. This is an example of “human error” which occurred while the
teacher was trying to combine data from the student roster with the grade
sheet. Computers and programmers make such errors too, while combining
data from different sources, during data integration.
AI is particularly good at detecting data inconsistencies and anomalies in
massive data. However, not all anomalies in data are bad. It is important to
explore and explain anomalies detected by AI. Sometimes, anomalies can
lead to amazing discoveries as demonstrated in the example below.
8.2.5.1 Illustrative example: AI finds a planet far, far away
AI has discovered new planets and stars by identifying anomalies in data
collected by NASA's Kepler Space Telescope. In 2017, NASA used AI to
discover a new planet, Kepler-90i, using AI-based anomaly detection by
identifying subtle changes in light caused by a planet crossing the star
Kepler-90. NASA and Google's AI research team deployed machine
learning algorithms to scour massive amounts of data in search of
anomalous signals missed by previous studies. You can read more about this
exciting discovery in Section 8.5, and by exploring the prompt in
GeminiBox 8.2.4.
FIGURE 8.8: Kepler-90i, an exoplanet orbiting the star Kepler-90 was
discovered by searching for data anomalies in light reading. NASA Ames
Research Center, Wendy Stenzel.
GeminiBox 8.2.4: AI discovers new planet by finding anomalies in
data ⏎
“How was anomaly detection used to discover Kepler-90i?” (cid:0)
Surprisingly, AI can be a source of anomalies and inconsistent data due to
its tendency to return nonsensical information dubbed hallucinations. It has
been known to fabricate legal cases, quote non-existent books, and offer
dangerous advice. It is critical to vet AI output through cross-validation
with authoritative sources of information.
GeminiBox 8.2.5: AI Hallucinations ⏎
“What are AI hallucinations?” (cid:0)
Data Debate 8.2.6 After reading the output generated by the prompts in
GeminiBox 8.2.5, debate the causes of hallucinations and how to fix them.
8.2.5.2 Cleaning anomalies
Anomalies and outliers are tricky to remediate. Sometimes, they are
genuine errors, e.g., speech-to-text conversions while using hands-free
devices. The website “Last Word or Nothing” (cid:0) lists some bloopers.
THEY SAID, AI HEARD:
He's a research scientist postdoc. = He's a research
scientist narwhal.
I'm a data scientist turned entrepreneur. = I'm a dinosaur
sharpener.
These are genuine anomalies and should be fixed. However, anomalies can
lead to important discoveries as described in Illustrative Example 8.2.5.1.
Here are some ways of addressing anomalies:
Choose a cleaning method based on how the data is to be used. Anomaly
treatment is often contextual. Some applications rely on anomalies, e.g.,
network intrusion uses anomalies to identify suspicious network usage
patterns. Others that involve calculating average values, e.g., average test
scores of a student, are distorted by anomalies such as one bad test. It
might be worth dropping the worst score from the computation of a
student's average.
In some scenarios, one root cause, e.g., bad software, can produce
unusual data patterns across multiple systems, e.g., spikes in default date
values such as “January 1, 1970.” Finding the root cause can help treat
these related anomalies simultaneously rather than individually.
Analyze anomalies and create potential explanations for future tagging.
Consider the hypothetical example where a new employee's badge might
be flagged as an anomaly until it is approved, which might take a few
minutes to hours. This is typically caused when data systems are briefly
out of sync. An anomaly explanation might be: “If the employee's name
is in the HR database, and the on-boarding timestamp is less than one
hour old, ignore the anomaly.”
AI is an amazing anomaly detection tool because of its ability to rapidly
scan massive amounts of data. It is used extensively to monitor complex
systems such as telecommunications networks, flight patterns, galaxies and
gene sequences.
Data Debate 8.2.7 Find a specific example of the use of AI in healthcare to
detect anomalies. Discuss whether this could have been discovered
manually by humans looking through the data.
8.2.6 Duplicates and near-duplicates
Suppose that you go to the grocery store and notice at the check-out that
your bill seems larger than expected. You check the receipt and find that
you've been charged for 2 soaps and 2 diapers while you only bought one of
each, Table 8.1, left. Duplicate scans of the soap and diapers inflated your
bill. Had you not checked, you would have been poorer by $20.
TABLE 8.1: Duplicates can have serious
consequences. Left: Grocery bill with
duplicate entries. Right: Duplicate photos can
clog up devices and cloud storage. Photo:
Lynn Greyling. ⏎
Item Name Quantity Price
1 Gallon Milk 1 $6.00
2 Apples 3 lbs $4.00
3 Soap 1 $2.00
4 Soap 1 $2.00
5 Diapers 1 $18.00
6 Diapers 1 $18.00
7 Water-2liter 1 $3.00
In another example of duplication, consider the case where you receive
the same photo on your smartphone from multiple family members. The
multiple copies take over your phone camera roll, as in Figure 8.1, right.
Most smartphones will offer to “merge” such duplicates.
Duplicates have serious consequences for AI. They increase the
representation of the duplicates giving them greater weight in the models
that are trained. For example, if the phrase “All work and no play makes
Jack a dull boy” was repeated a sufficient number of times in the training
text of an AI model, that is all it would learn and ignore all the other text.
Duplicates in the census data could lead to misleading population counts
and misallocation of school funding.
Near-duplicates are a subtle form of duplicates. For example, the same
individual, e.g., John Doe, might be referred to in different databases as
Jack Doe, Jacky Doe, Jack Doh, John Doh. In order to realize that they are
the same entity, additional information such as address and social security
number might be needed.
8.2.6.1 Cleaning duplicates
The process of removing duplicates is called de-duping. There are many
approaches to de-duping.
A simple approach is to sort the data so that identical records appear
together. Multiples can be removed leaving one unique record behind. In
Table 8.1, left, we would remove the extra “Soap” and “Diapers”
transactions leaving just one of each.
New data can be matched against existing data before being added to the
data repository. Any data records that match existing records would be
discarded.
Near-duplicates could be detected by checking additional sources of data
to clarify information such as social security numbers. Yet another way
would be to profile the data and look for spikes in frequencies of certain
values that could point to near- duplicates.
Duplicate detection is a vast field of study. Use the prompt in GeminiBox
8.2.6 to learn more.
GeminiBox 8.2.6: De-duplication ⏎
“What is de-duplication? What is an example? How is it done?” (cid:0)
8.2.7 Other data quality issues
Data quality issues can be of many other types as well. Here are some
additional ways data can be of poor quality. There are so many
opportunities for data to be corrupted during data processing and
preparation stages that it is not possible to list all the ways.
8.2.7.1 Delayed and out-of-order data
Certain applications use data streams where the data arrives in real-time
continuously. If the data gets delayed or arrives out of order, the application
will have insufficient data to work with. For example, if you are watching a
video stream and the images are delayed, the video will seem choppy and
freeze frequently affecting the viewing experience. During elections, the
results from certain regions arrive sooner than others, making the election
results see-saw in favor of one party to the other. At such times, it is
important not to declare results prematurely until a reliable portion of the
data has been obtained.
8.2.7.2 Irrelevant and outdated data
What if your grade in English was based on your Mathematics test
performance? Or your senior-year grade was based on your middle-school
GPA? There are many instances when data is hard to come by or too
expensive to collect. In such situations, applications are built using
whatever data is easily available. It is possible that the data might not be
relevant to the problem, or quite outdated. Imagine routing autonomous cars
using maps from 20 or 30 years ago. New developments, cul de sacs, new
traffic patterns and traffic signs would not be reflected resulting in
potentially disastrous outcomes.
8.2.7.3 Data accessibility and interpretability
Suppose there is a database with the best quality data, collected and curated
at great expense. But it is on a hard drive and hidden in a remote cave
somewhere like in an Indiana Jones movie. And, even when you manage to
find the data, all you see are a set of numbers. Without a description of the
data, i.e., metadata, would you be able to use it? While the data itself might
be of good quality, if it cannot be accessed and understood, then it is not
usable.
8.3 Measuring Data Quality
Measuring data quality is an essential step in ensuring a supply of high
quality data to AI pipelines. A data quality index (DQI) provides an
objective and universal way to determine the usability of data. It also helps
quantify how effective data cleaning techniques are improving the quality
of data by moving the index in the appropriate direction. Such an index
helps make objective choices between multiple cleaning strategies.
A simple DQI is to compute the proportion of data that has data quality
issues. For example, consider the data in Figure 8.9 where each row
represents the data pertaining to a phone call. This data is simulated but
mimics the format of a real call detail record, a standard format used by
phone companies. Each record has 9 columns, each corresponding to an
attribute. The attributes are described in the header of the table, the first
row.
There are 45 cells in the data sheet. Of these, 2 cells are missing data
One cell has an anomalous value
One cell has an out-of-order timestamp
Three cells have inconsistent values where the data is delivered before
the event (the call) is completed
The last record with 9 data elements is an exact duplicate of the previous
one, except for the delivery time
FIGURE 8.9: Example data (not real): There are 45 cells in this table that
contain data. Of these, at least 14 cells have data quality issues. ⏎
There are 14 cells with bad data. A simple data quality index, DQI, would
be:
no. of cells with bad data
DQI =
total no. of cells
14
=
45
= 0.31.
Note that there are different ways of counting bad data cells. We counted
only the delivery timestamp cell as bad data in the second record which was
out of order, but we counted all the cells in the last record which was a
duplicate. As long as we are consistent in the way these rules are applied,
the DQI is robust.
Data quality is very application-dependent which makes the problem
hard to generalize and automate. It is best to define what constitutes bad
data and how to count it at the beginning of any project, and ensure that
those guidelines are adhered to across the entire length and breadth of the
project.
8.4 Statistical Distortion and Data Cleaning
In this chapter, we have discussed several data cleaning methods such as
missing value imputation and de-duplication. It is clear, e.g., from Figure
8.2 and Figure 8.6, that data gets altered both by data quality issues as well
as data cleaning efforts. It is important not to clean the data to such an
extent that it becomes too different from the original data that is supposed
to represent some information about the real world. Here are considerations
to bear in mind while cleaning data:
Quantify the change in the data caused by cleaning: One way to
quantify the change in the data is through a measure called statistical
distortion, proposed by Dasu and Loh [13]. Statistical distortion
measures the change in the distributional properties of data before and
after cleaning, using histogram distances, e.g., Kullback-Leibler
distance. The greater the distance, the less faithful the cleaned data is to
the original. It is important to choose a stopping strategy for cleaning at
the beginning of the AI project based on the amount of distortion that
can be tolerated by the application before the results become unreliable.
This is akin to specifying the level of significance, α, the probability of
Type 1 error, ahead of testing a hypothesis.
Keep track of the cost of cleaning: It is possible sometimes to clean the
data perfectly and recover the original data. For example, one could
travel to the home of every person named “Doe” or “Doh” to verify the
correct spelling of their last name. However, this would entail high costs
in both time and money. Resource constraints can often define the scope
of cleaning.
Data Debate 8.4.1 Do you think checking in on every Doe and Doh on
earth is feasible? How long would it take to get the final version of the
clean data? How much would it cost to travel from home to home? Is it
possible to reach every Doe and Doh on the planet? Would you even
know of the existence of every Doe and Doh on the planet? Would it be
worth it?
Ask if cleaning data helps the goal of the project: Not all bad data
needs to be cleaned. For example, if the project requires calculating
typical values such as averages and medians, then missing values that are
scattered randomly across the data and within limits, might not distort
the results too much. On the other hand, anomalies could potentially
distort typical values and might need to be addressed.
Data quality and data cleaning are an important part of data literacy, and
they are crucial for building effective and accurate AI tools and models. The
book Exploratory Data Analysis and Data Cleaning by Dasu and Johnson
[12] is a rigorous introduction to data quality and data cleaning.

1. The theater club in a university wants to collect data about students'
interests in music and arts using an online survey. What kind of biases
and data quality issues can creep in during this data collection process?
What actions can be taken to address these issues?
2. Can you think of different ways that data can get contaminated? Write
down three examples.
3. Write down three ways of cleaning data, and the associated costs in
terms of time, money, labor and computing resources.
4. Could you help the detective with the GPS travel trajectory that took a
suspect from Seattle to New York City in under 5 minutes? What are the
possible explanations? Why?
5. Give three examples of inconsistent data.
6. Examine the output of the prompt in GeminiBox 8.2.3 and list at least
one way in which AI can fix missing values in numeric data and in text.
7. Mr. and Mrs. Murthy are planning a 7-day vacation to Dubai. Mr.
Murthy asks AI chatbot Gemini to make an itinerary. Is this a good idea?
List some pros and cons.
8. A pharmaceutical company has developed a new drug for migraine and
is now in the process of testing for effectiveness and safety of the drug.
The researchers want to enroll up to 100 people for pre-clinical research
who have migraines to try the new drug and sends out a survey to
several clinics in the state to gather information to see if the participant
will qualify and want to participate. What types of biases and data
quality issues should researchers watch out for and how can they
minimize data quality issues?
9. How does data quality impact small businesses? Baked goods gift
baskets: Jay and Kumar have an online business where they create gift
baskets with freshly baked goods for the holidays for shipping across the
country. The holidays are coming up and they want to ensure that they
are well prepared. They decide to reorganize their customer information,
and vendors from whom they order supplies and baked goods. They are
asking you, a data quality specialist, to help them with this task. What
would you tell them? Do you think AI can help them with this task?
10. Comparison of data collection survey methods and how data quality
