9
What's that AI?
AI is rapidly becoming an indispensable tool and transforming all
aspects of our life. It has attracted trillions of dollars of investments
from governments, technology companies, and start-ups all over the
world. We must learn to speak AI in order to use it effectively and call
out its misuse.
In this chapter, we provide a high-level overview of AI, its use cases
and limitations, and connect the concepts in prior chapters and
illustrative examples to specific AI applications. This chapter acts as a
segue from the fundamentals of statistics and data science, to
introductory concepts in machine learning and AI.
9.1 What's AI, anyway?
AI is simply the use of data and knowledge to train software to learn and
perform specific tasks, and more ambitiously, mimic human thinking to
automate decisions and actions. Recognizing objects and telling them apart,
e.g., cats vs. dogs, planning optimal routes to go from one place to another,
screening job and mortgage applications, powering healthcare chatbots,
answering consumer queries, and even writing in Shakespeare's style or
painting with Michelangelo's flourish, are all within the scope of AI.
Different ways in which these objectives are achieved result in different
types of AI.
AI has been around for more than 125 years. It started to coalesce when
English mathematician Alan Turing (1912-1954) wondered if machines
could think and exhibit “artificial intelligence.” The field was formally
defined at a Dartmouth conference organized by Professor John McCarthy
in 1956. Since then, the fortunes of AI have gone through cycles of
skepticism and hype, until the present, when AI finally seems to be
delivering on its promise. Figure 9.2, courtesy Tarjomyar, presents a
timeline of the evolution of AI up until 2025. Milestones include the
development of special algorithms (back-propagation) in 1986 that allowed
neural network algorithms to go back to previous layers and update
computations such as weights, and transformers in 2017 that enabled
parallelization on a massive scale and allowed language models to
understand context and generate human-like language. In this chapter, we
discuss a simple ontology of AI and how the concepts learned thus far relate
to AI. A detailed discussion of AI algorithms is not the intent of this chapter.
The classic text Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach by Russell and
Norvig [37] is considered the authoritative reference on AI.
Figure 9.1 shows two excellent examples of AI. The photo on the left
depicts a bionic arm that can be used as a prosthetic for wounded soldiers or
others who have lost their limbs to birth defects, disease or accidents. This
field of AI is Robotics, which integrates several types of AI. Some key
developments in robotics are shown in Figure 9.2, e.g., the musical robot
WABOT-2 (1980) that played instruments and the social robot Kismet
(2000) that could interact with humans.
FIGURE 9.1: Left: A bionic arm relies on different types of AI. The Food
and Drug Administration (FDA) and Defense Advanced Research Projects
Agency (DARPA) funded this brain-controlled, prosthetic arm. (Photo
credit: The Johns Hopkins University Applied Physics Laboratory
(JHU/APL). Right: AI plays chess and even beats grandmasters at Go
(Image: Flickr). ⏎
FIGURE 9.2: A timeline of AI evolution. Adapted from the image:
Tarjomyar, CC BY-SA 4.0. (cid:0) ⏎
Figure 9.1, right, depicts a robot playing chess. AI has beaten human
experts in many games of strategy including chess, Go and Starcraft II,
using probabilistic reasoning, optimizing rewards and efficiently searching
possible strategies by sampling different paths. In doing so, it developed
several unorthodox but highly effective strategies that might be
counterintuitive to humans. Figure 9.2 shows IBM's Deep Blue that caused
a sensation in 1997 when it defeated the world champion Garry Kasparov at
chess. In 2016, Google's DeepMind beat Lee Sedol, the world's best Go
player. Watch the documentary Alpha Go.
AI already powers most aspects of our life, from healthcare to fashion,
from commerce to sports, as we have seen in the illustrative examples in the
previous chapters. However, AI's capabilities have been both exaggerated
and demonized. It is neither capable of being some sort of superhuman, nor
is it going to take over the world. Given that AI is an important tool in our
daily lives, it is critical to understand the basics of AI, in order to use it, to
build on it, and to recognize when it makes errors.
9.1.1 AI and what we have learned so far
In previous chapters we learned about data collection and management,
exploratory data analysis, probability theory, sampling, hypothesis testing
and relationships between multiple variables. These concepts form the
foundation of AI and are used extensively in the development and
advancement of AI. Here are some examples.
NASA uses adaptive sampling to efficiently sample and autonomously
explore the surface of Mars for traces of minerals that could have supported
life, as shown in Figure 9.3, left. The article Here's How AI Is Changing
NASA's Mars Rover Science in Section 9.6 has details. Music and speech
are sampled to generate new music and help create synthetic voice
assistants.
FIGURE 9.3: Left: NASA and JPL use sampling to power AI for exploring
Mars. Right: AI uses anomaly detection to identify abnormalities in fetal
brain scans. (Photo: Robo Universe.) ⏎
Empirical distributions and probabilities are extensively used by AI
applications to solve complex problems. For example, the fashion industry
uses empirical distributions to manage supply chains and inventory, and for
predictive analytics. Empirical distributions are used in fashion to power AI
to spot design hits and misses in Section 9.6 provides details. Anomaly
detection is used to diagnose abnormalities like tumors, fractures and other
unexpected changes, as shown in Figure 9.3.
Professional sports teams such the Philadelphia 49ers use AI to generate
hypotheses about match-ups, and test hypotheses in real-time to choose the
best substitutions that increase the likelihood of winning. In addition, as
explained in Generative AI: 10 Game-Changing Benefits for Sports
Companies, Section 9.6, their medical staff on the sidelines can diagnose
and treat injured athletes faster and with greater accuracy with the help of
AI.
Data quality discussed in Chapter 8 is now at the forefront of AI since
without data there is no AI. In Chapter 10, we will focus on the responsible
and ethical use of data and AI.
During the course of this chapter, it is important to establish the
connections between AI and concepts learned in earlier chapters, just like
the examples above (see Figure 9.4). Doing so is good training for courses
in machine learning and AI. Therefore, as you read through the applications
and types of AI, pause and think of what concepts from the earlier chapters
could have been used. For those interested in further study, Russell and
Norvig's Artifical Intelligence: A Modern Approach [37] is a comprehensive
classic.

chapters form the foundation of today's AI which in turn improves the

9.2 Illustrative Example: Build an AI classifier with
probabilities
To develop an intuition for how statistics and probability are used in AI, let
us work through a very simple Naive Bayes Classifier using Bayes' Rule
and the multiplicative rule of probabilities of independent events from
Chapter 5.
Suppose that we are given the following data labeled with preference of
ice cream flavors. The attributes consist of “Creamy” (Yes/No), “Dairy”
(Yes/No), “Sweetness” (High(H)/Medium(M)/Low(L)), and flavor “Type”
(Exotic/Standard). We are given the following training data where each
record has a “Preference” label (Like/Dislike). Our objective is to predict
the preference (Like/Dislike) for a new flavor given some or all of the
attributes: Creamy, Dairy, Sweetness and Type.
The Naive Bayes Classifier (at a very high level) involves the following
steps:

upon the class label. Therefore, the joint attribute probabilities
conditional upon the class label can be multiplied using the
multiplicative rule of independence:

The probability that the flavor will be liked is 1.875 times the probability
that it will not be liked. Therefore, we “predict” that the new flavor will be

And, that, is a Naive Bayes Classifier for predicting whether a new flavor
will be liked or disliked based on its Type! This is just a very simple
example of the use of statistics and probability in AI. Next, let us consider
how probabilities are used in language modeling.
9.3 Illustrative Example: Large Language Models and
Probabilities
Large Language Models (LLMs) generate text. They write emails,
summarize legal documents and create specialized itineraries, among other
things. The output to the prompts in GeminiBoxes scattered throughout the
textbook was generated by an LLM, Gemini. LLMs are one of the major
factors that have contributed to the current AI boom, as they are
increasingly incorporated into workplace productivity tools. Autocomplete
in email is another example of the application of LLMs.
LLMs use probabilities extensively, to reason and generate human-like
language. Consider a highly simplified example. The sentence “I want to go
to the gym and” could be completed in multiple ways:
(1) “pay membership fees.”
(2) “work out.”
(3) “eat ice cream.”
An LLM trained on vast corpora of text could attach probabilities to each of
these, for example, 0.01, 0.987 and 0.003. The phrase with the highest
probability would be chosen to predict what comes next. LLMs can predict
not just the next word or phrase, but entire paragraphs. The blog
Introduction to Large Language Models (cid:0) contains a quick overview of
LLMs.
LLMs use the concept of tokenization which breaks up language into
units such as words and punctuation. Tokens make it easier for algorithms
to process text and associate probabilities in a systematic and structured
manner rather than when presented with free-form chunks of text. Tokens
such as punctuation also provide contextual understanding leading to better
prediction. To quote an old grammar standby, “Let's eat Grandma” has an
entirely different meaning than “Let's eat, Grandma.”
An innovation in 2017 (shown in Figure 9.2), the transformers
architecture enabled parallelization in contrast to the extant sequential
approach to natural language processing. Self-attention where computations
are limited to those tokens that are immediately relevant to a particular
token enabled this parallelization, making language models much faster and
feasible for practical use. Transformers were introduced in a ground-
breaking paper Attention is all you need by Vaswani et al. [43].
LLMs are not perfect. They are prone to hallucinations, and do not have a
notion of “understanding” or “truth” or “ethics.” They inherit all the biases
and imperfections embedded in the training data, and cannot address
scenarios outside the corpora they are trained on. They consume large
amounts of energy and might not be kind to the environment. However,
they have been adopted rapidly and successfully and are sure to have an
outsized impact going forward.
9.4 Types of AI
In the beginning, AI was designed to perform a single task. Given an image,
classify it as a cat or dog, for instance. It would be trained on hundreds of
thousands of labeled images of cats and dogs, and when shown a new
image, identify it as a cat or a dog. Its performance was never at 100% due
to the fact that images can be fuzzy, some cats and dogs look similar in
photos, and images in training data could be mislabeled due to human error
or otherwise.
As the field advanced, and the ability to collect large amounts of data
grew exponentially, AI was trained on more fine-grained data collected
every split second. Data from the internet, from atmospheric sensors, from
drones, from street cameras, from the stock market, from medical probes,
and from every digital trace left by smartphones like cell tower pings,
almost everything under the sun could be represented as some type of data.
All this data in turn helped AI programs create a more complete picture of
the physical world, allowing them to interact with it and perform tasks such
as drive a car through crowded streets, or, land a rover on the moon. Quite
amazing, right?
The other development that helped advance AI was the increase in
computing power. Computer chips, hardware, processors and software
evolved to enable companies like Google and Meta to keep pace with the
constant torrent of fine-grained data of different types that was available for
collecting. Specialized AI chips optimized for AI operations have
contributed to a great extent to make the integration of AI applications into
practical daily use. Read AI Chips: What They Are and Why They Matter in
Section 9.6.
AI falls into broad categories based on the conceptual approach to a task.
We found Crash Course AI, created by PBS, to be a useful way to think
about AI and its organization. We have adapted it in this chapter to meet our
specific needs. The categories are only for guidance since they are all
interrelated, with fluid boundaries, and are used in conjunction in most
sophisticated AI tasks. In this chapter, we provide an overview to prepare
the reader for further courses in AI and Machine Learning. The foundational
concepts of statistics and data intuition described in the preceding chapters
play a critical role in the understanding and development of AI.
9.4.1 Symbolic AI
Human beings have extensive knowledge about the world around them. For
example, “if it's raining, carry an umbrella,” or “fish will die if taken out of
water.” Such knowledge needs to be communicated to computers and AI, in
order for them to perform human tasks and mimic human thinking.
However, in the past, computers could not understand a human language, so
computer scientists developed specialized languages for that purpose.
Human knowledge of the physical world was communicated to computers
in the form of rules, represented as a combination of symbols and relations.
Symbols are typically nouns and objects, while relationships are typically
verbs and adjectives that connect the symbols. For example,
IsFriend(CharlieBrown,Linus),
is an example of a symbolic relationship where Charlie Brown and Linus,
characters from the Peanuts comics, are objects and “IsFriend” is their
relationship. Logic is used to deduce the validity of the statement and return
an answer, True or False. In this case the answer is “True,” since Charlie
Brown and Linus are friends.
Data Debate 9.4.1 Consider the following:
IsFriend(CharlieBrown,Lucy).
Is this true or false?
To be fair, this is a trick question since it is not clear whether Charlie Brown
and Lucy are friends or foes, perhaps both.
Symbolic AI uses such knowledge and rules rather than traditional data.
It has evolved over time to incorporate other forms of AI to accomplish
complex tasks, e.g., enable chatbots like Siri which can answer open-ended
questions rather than just “True or False” type of questions. Siri came into
being in 2010, as shown in Figure 9.2, and changed the way humans
interact with devices, making them accessible through human conversation
rather than a specialized computer programming language. Learn more
about how symbolic AI is used by exploring the prompt in GeminiBox
9.4.1.
GeminiBox 9.4.1: Applications of symbolic AI ⏎
“What are the most popular applications of symbolic AI?” (cid:0)
9.4.2 Machine learning
Machine learning is a type of AI that uses data to learn. More data lead to
better performance, in general. Data can be of any kind: images, video,
webpages, audio, numbers, text.
Data Debate 9.4.2 What concepts from Chapter 2 could be useful here?
Machine learning comes in different flavors, some of which are listed
below. The book Elements of Statistical Learning by Hastie et al. [19] is a
comprehensive reference for foundational statistical learning algorithms.
Another classic textbook on the topic of machine learning is Pattern
Recognition and Machine Learning by Christopher Bishop [3].
Unsupervised learning consists of grouping or clustering objects based
on similarity of specified attributes. Clustering helps identify subsets of
homogeneous objects that are either close together or densely populated,
e.g., galaxies in space, migratory paths of birds, or consumers with
similar tastes in movies. In Chapter 7, in the scatterplot of the attributes
bill length versus flipper length in the Palmer Penguin data, there were
three distinct clusters of points. Each cluster corresponds to a different
species, Adelie, Chinstrap or Gentoo, shown in Figure 9.5 indicating that
the two attributes together could be used to group penguins into species,
and identify or predict the species of a penguin based on those two
attributes.
FIGURE 9.5: Unsupervised learning identifies clusters of objects that
are similar to each other, such as penguins that belong to the same
species. (Horst AM, Hill AP, Gorman KB (2020). palmerpenguins:
Palmer Archipelago (Antarctica) penguin data. R package version 0.1.0.
https://allisonhorst.github.io/palmerpenguins/. doi:
10.5281/zenodo.3960218.) ⏎
Supervised learning is a predictive task, rather than merely grouping
based on similarity. It uses additional information about the objects,
namely their “class” labels. Apple or grape? Dog or cat? Horse or zebra?
It “trains” on data that already has class labels associated with each
object, and learns patterns of attributes for each class. As an
oversimplified example, consider Figure 9.6 where the labeled data
yields the rule “if color=purple, then label=grape.” What do you think
the label of the new object is? What do you think will happen if a new
object is a banana? We have already discussed one of the most popular
examples of supervised learning, the Naive Bayes Classifier, in Section
9.2.
FIGURE 9.6: Highly simplified example of supervised learning based
on labeled training data. ⏎
Neural networks are complex learning algorithms that mimic biological
neurons, shown in Figure 9.7. Humans explore and interact with the
word through electric signals collected by neurons and processed by the
brain. Neurons combine different types of inputs like sight, hearing,
touch, taste and smell (our five senses) collected through dendrites to
make a decision based on a threshold of excitation, and pass on the
signal to other neurons through synapses. For example, our eyes might
perceive fire, our skin might sense heat, and our nose could smell smoke.
Depending on the strength of the combined signals, the brain might
signal “campfire, get ready to eat marshmallows!” or “wildfire, run for
your life!”
Neural nets, as they are known in short, gather data in the shape of
different attributes analogous to the brain gathering information from
different senses, combine the inputs into a single signal much like
dendrites, and check to see if the signal exceeds a particular threshold, to
make a yes-no decision. The simplest unit of a neural network is called a
perceptron. Simple neural networks typically have a single layer that
combines individual inputs and outputs a decision.
FIGURE 9.7: Left: A neuron accepts inputs through its dendrites,
processes them and passes on the signal as an electric impulse to other
neurons via synapses. Image courtesy of the City University of New
York (cid:0). Right: Neural networks mimic neurons to gather input, combine
them into a signal and make a binary decision depending on whether the
signal exceeds a predefined cut-off threshold. Image: Chrislb, CC BY-
SA 3.0, via Wikimedia Commons. ⏎
GeminiBox 9.4.2: Perceptron
“What is a perceptron?” (cid:0)
Deep learning consists of complex combinations of multiple neural
networks through layering. Each layer results in more and more complex
mathematical combinations of inputs. Deep learning networks need
massive amounts of data and computing power. The models tend to be
opaque and not easily explainable or interpretable by humans. However,
they are the key to automation, and the reason that AI can perform
complex tasks such as drive cars and clean warehouses. Deep Learning:
Foundations and Concepts by Bishop and Bishop [4] is a reference text
for upperclassmen and graduate students interested in delving further
into neural networks and deep learning.
Other types of learning include reinforcement learning and recommender
systems which are specialized types of machine learning algorithms.
Reinforcement learning rewards correct answers and penalizes wrong
answers, leading to the reinforcement of strongly predictive patterns over
multiple iterations. Recommender systems use data from other similar
objects, whether our own historical preferences or those of others like us,
to recommend shows, clothes, books and music. Recommender systems
use relationships between multiple objects, that is across multiple
records, rather than just an object and its attributes contained in a single
record. Such an approach has computational implications since it might
require scanning the data multiple times.
9.4.3 Natural language processing
Voice-enabled AI assistants like Siri or Alexa need to understand human
language, called natural language understanding and reply in human
language using natural language generation. Together, these two aspects
constitute natural language processing, or NLP for short, which enables
humans and computers to communicate with each other like humans. NLP
ingests large amounts of text as well as rules that describe syntax and
grammar to learn how to process words and word sequences to form
sentences, paragraphs and hold conversations in a human language. Speech
and Language Processing by Jurafsky and Martin [24] is considered to be
an authoritative text on this topic.
Large language models, or LLMs, are specialized and advanced versions
of NLP models that use deep learning. They are being increasingly
deployed in AI assistants to generate and communicate in human-like text.
Learn more by using the prompt in GeminiBox 9.4.3.
GeminiBox 9.4.3: Applications of LLMs ⏎
“What are three real-world applications of LLMs?” (cid:0)
Data Debate 9.4.3 What type of data would NLP systems use?
9.4.4 Robotics
Robotics involves serious hardware, electronics and engineering, but in
order for the robots to accomplish tasks like moving and interacting with
the environment, they need to process data from sensors that capture audio,
video, and other data about the surroundings, and process the data to make
decisions. For example, cleaning robots at Heathrow airport discussed in
Chapter 5 would need to process visual cues from their cameras, recognize
the object, and figure out the best action to take, e.g., “move right by 1
meter.” Such robotic functioning is governed by many different types of AI
including Object Recognition, Symbolic AI and Deep Learning.
The agricultural robot shown in Figure 9.8, left, helps with pest control
by targeted spraying and even performs mechanical weed removal, helping
farmers reduce cost and increase efficiency.
FIGURE 9.8: Left: Autonomous pest control in agriculture using robots.
Right: Artificial General Intelligence (AGI), romanticized in fiction and
movies like Ex Machina, is a future aspirational state of AI that can perform
all the complex and varied tasks that a human can do, rather than specific
tasks, however complex, like the current AIs are able to accomplish. ⏎
9.4.5 Generative AI
The GeminiBoxes in this textbook are examples of Generative AI. They
generate human-readable answers when prompted in a human language.
NLP and large language models mentioned in Section 9.4.3 help Gemini
convert human language into machine code, search the web for answers,
collate and summarize the information, and return an answer in human
language, with a list of sources that it drew the information from. Imagine
how long it would take to find all that information manually!
Any AI that can generate content is called generative AI. It is used
increasingly by corporations, artists, scientists and many others to create:
travel itineraries, emails and executive summaries, pictures, music and
videos. Examples include DALL-E shown in Figure 9.2, the picture-
generating AI, and of course, Gemini.
GeminiBox 9.4.4: Generative AI
“What's genAI?” (cid:0)
Another interesting example of genAI are VLMs, or Vision Language
Models, that can interface between visuals and text. Subtitles, visual
descriptions of images and video, are generated using VLMs. Many
websites mandate such a description of content in the form of Alt-text,
which is often done manually. VLMs could potentially be used to generate
Alt-text to help increase the accessibility of online content to those with
functional impairments. Learn more about VLMs by clicking on the link in
Section 9.6.
9.4.6 Agentic AI
Most AIs discussed thus far follow very specific instructions, e.g., “write a
play in the style of Shakespeare”, or “pick up a tennis ball.” However, we
have also discussed AIs that can measure the environment around and make
decisions in response through reasoning, without relying on human
intervention, and learn from the feedback. AI with such capabilities to
function autonomously is called agentic AI.
Self-driving cars are examples of agentic AI. Agentic AI is also used for
monitoring cybersecurity threats, for managing systems and processes, and
in healthcare to monitor symptoms of patients and calibrate dosage. Since
agentic AI can make autonomous decisions, it can respond rapidly in each
of these situations where a timely intervention could make a big difference
in outcomes.
9.4.7 AGI
Humans have the ability to perform a mind-boggling variety of tasks. They
can see, talk, think, imagine and feel emotions. They can ride a bike, cook,
paint, weed the garden, go dancing with friends, calm a crying baby and go
swimming. AI, as of now, is capable of doing specific tasks, however
complex, extremely well. But in its current state, it cannot accomplish all
the general tasks humans undertake as a part of daily routine: brush teeth,
take a shower, drive a car, feel happy at the sight of a smiling baby, sing on
the way to work, doodle at a boring meeting, create a new genre of music,
and cheer for a favorite team. The holy grail of the field of AI is a human-
like intelligence that can think, act and create, called Artificial General
Intelligence or AGI.
While such an AGI does not exist at the moment, movies and shows such
as ExMachina (Figure 9.8, right) and Black Mirror have imagined AGIs
that are human-like. There is much controversy about the current
capabilities of AI and its ability to benefit or destroy humanity. It is
important to filter out all the misinformation and hype, and rely on experts,
not quacks or influencers, to be educated and informed by facts, not
opinion. Chapter 10 presents an article on the debate between AI experts
Yann LeCun and Yoshua Bengio who, along with Geoffrey Hinton, won the
prestigious ACM Turing award in 2018 for their contributions to AI.
Data Debate 9.4.4 Find three examples of AGI in movies and shows.
9.4.8 Web search
AI now plays a big role now in searching, curating and summarizing results
in response to search queries on the web. For example, in response to the
query “ancient architecture in india”, the search engine uses AI, to find the
relevant webpages in a split second, using a process called indexing. The
index allows searches to look up information quickly in huge databases
where the webpages are stored. The result of this particular search is shown
in Figure 9.9. Given that AI has to search through trillions of documents,
this is quite a feat. AI also provides a summary of the page, titled AI
Overview, with links ranked in the order of relevance, and lists the sources
that contributed to the summary. It would be impossible for humans to
manually search for such information on a regular basis, let alone at this
speed. To understand how search engines use AI to find relevant documents
and summarize them, use the prompt in GeminiBox 9.4.5.
FIGURE 9.9: A screenshot of search results from a Google query. Web
search uses AI to locate relevant information in a split second, from trillions
of documents online. In this case, when asked for “ancient architecture in
india”, the search returns documents as well as generates a summary, “AI
overview”, and lists the sources from which it compiled the summary. ⏎

“Do search engines use AI?” (cid:0)
Data Debate 9.4.5 Examine Figure 9.9 and identify the sources of
information for this query. What is the significance of the highlighted text in
the AI Overview?
9.5 AI: Power and Responsibility
The famous quote rooted in Spider-man lore “with great power comes great
responsibility” is particularly true of AI. As demonstrated in this textbook,
AI impacts every aspect of life. From keeping homes at desired
temperatures, to plotting best traffic routes, monitoring stock markets,
summarizing documents, suggesting shows, playing chess, AI is
everywhere. Not only is AI present in every aspect of daily life, it can also
have serious consequences for many. AI is used for medical diagnoses,
parole decisions, to screen mortgage applications, and to decide whom to
hire. It is our responsibility to ensure that AI algorithms are audited so as
not to perpetuate unfair practices in society. We will learn more about this
in Chapter 10.
The societal impact of AI is still being evaluated as AI tools become
increasingly commonplace. The article on Google's AI blog Applying AI to
help solve society's biggest challenges and improve lives (cid:0) provides a look
at the diversity of ways that AI can affect society, from managing wildfires
and floods, protecting coral reefs to helping farm-fresh food reach young
children in school cafeterias. The following section presents several specific
ways in which AI impacts society, a few cautionary tales about the mistakes
AI can make, and the need to prepare communities for AI.
9.5.1 AI and education
AI has the potential to change traditional teaching and learning. AI is an
outstanding tool for gathering, collating and summarizing information. It
enriches knowledge and information by presenting numerous aspects of a
topic. AI has democratized access to knowledge and education by digitizing
content, lectures and exercises, and making them accessible to remote,
underserved corners of the world. According to the article Bridging the
Education Divide: How AI Can Support Underserved Communities, “in
Sub-Saharan Africa, about 90% of children cannot read a sentence by age
10, a crisis termed ‘learning poverty’ by the World Bank.” AI can
personalize learning at scale and provide tutoring through voice assistants
without need for smartphones. Read the entire article in Section 9.6.
Teachers have started to integrate AI as a teaching tool into their classes.
AI makes administrative tasks such as lesson plans and evaluation easier. AI
has also made it possible to customize lessons and learning to different skill
levels. Just as digitization has made it possible to embed links, images and
multimedia learning modes, AI opens up new possibilities in terms of
drawing knowledge from different disciplines and applications. The
potential of AI to provide individual instruction is being tested in schools.
However, students must learn the fundamentals of a subject in order to spot
inconsistencies, errors and biases in AI's output. AI has its imperfections,
like hallucinations, but it is also being willfully misused to spread
misinformation, and undermine established science. In a report by the
National Education Association (NEA), different applications of AI in
education are discussed. The report states that “the surge in AI requires that
educators become prepared to assess when it is appropriate to use AI, help
their students become AI literate, and advocate for the development of
policies about this technology.” This NEA report is referenced in Section
9.6.
Finally, it is important to preserve the privacy of both students and
teachers by making sure that education and evaluation data remain private
and protected. Read more about the pros and cons of AI in the classroom in
Section 9.6.
9.5.2 AI and employment
According to the World Economic Forum, “half of employers plan to re-
orient their business in response to AI, two-thirds plan to hire talent with
specific AI skills, while 40% anticipate reducing their workforce where AI
can automate tasks.” AI is expected to result both in an explosion of jobs in
the tech sector, as well as a significant decline in jobs that will be replaced
by automation. Understandably, there is much public angst about AI's threat
to jobs, as indicated in the results of a Pew Research Center poll shown in
Figure 9.10.
Extended Description
FIGURE 9.10: Pew Research Center graphics. Left: How Americans feel
about AI use in the workplace. Right: Adoption of AI, specifically
ChatGPT, in different age groups. ⏎
FIGURE 9.11: Left: Human imagination and creativity resulted in
structures like the Seattle Museum of Pop Culture designed by Frank
Gehry. (Image: Dietmar Rabich / Wikimedia Commons / “Seattle (WA,
USA), Museum of Pop Culture, Fassade – 2022 – 1593” / CC BY-SA 4.0).
Right: Classroom at the Hawa Abdi Center in Somalia, a country where
thousands of children have been displaced. AI offers personalized learning
at scale to help reach remote and underserved communities. (cid:0)
Like any revolutionary technology that changes the course of history,
e.g., invention of the steam engine and motor car, it brings with it inevitable
disruption of jobs that no longer can compete with the new technology such
as horse-drawn buggies that disappeared after the advent of the motor car.
At the same time, widespread deployment of AI could lead to the creation
of new job types, such as AI Auditor and AI Ethicist, speculates New York
Times opinion and technology writer Robert Capps whose article is
referenced in Section 9.6, along with other articles about the effect of AI on
the work force.
Data Debate 9.5.1 Can you think of three new jobs that large-scale
adoption of AI could create?
9.5.3 AI and architecture
AI has been readily adopted by artists and people in other creative
professions at the same time raising concerns about authenticity of artwork.
Architecture known for its creative geniuses like Frank Gehry and Zaha
Hadid, has also benefited from the use of AI. While still in the early stages
of adoption, AI can be used to generate architectural designs by providing
detailed descriptions. It can also analyze building models for compliance
with codes, do a resource assessment, e.g., what type of construction crew
would be needed and for how long, scope out the materials, and provide an
approximate timeline. According to Professor Phillip Bernstein of Yale
School of Architecture, while AI has helped in small ways, it has a long
way to go, in terms of taking responsibility for public safety and health, the
way architects are licensed to do. Read his interview in the article, How
might AI affect architects? A Yale expert weighs in, in Section 9.6.
9.5.4 AI and healthcare
AI has many applications in healthcare. It excels at examining images such
as CT scans and detecting abnormalities that can indicate fractures or
tumors, as in Figure 9.3, right. It can help doctors by screening symptoms
and shortlisting possible diagnoses. AI can be used for drug discovery, for
generating transcriptions of doctors' dictated notes, for predicting
recurrence of disease or the likelihood of missing appointments. AI can
manage emergency vehicles, perform routine administrative tasks like
refilling prescriptions or filling forms, and streamline administrative
processes. In areas with a shortage of healthcare providers, AI can increase
the reach of specialists. Robot assistants might be able to help seniors with
mobility challenges. Given that healthcare can potentially make the
difference between life and death, it is important to make sure that AI is
used ethically, responsibly and democratically. Section 9.6 contains several
articles, from the National Center for Supercomputing Applications, World
Economic Forum and the National Institutes of Health, about AI and the
field of healthcare.
9.5.5 Community and AI
While AI is ubiquitous, it is still a mysterious and fuzzy concept for many.
Figure 9.10, right, shows a Pew Research survey about the use of AI tools
like ChatGPT. There is a significant disparity based on age. Almost 60% of
young people use ChatGPT, but only 10% of senior citizens do. Each age
group is vulnerable in its own way to the spread of misinformation
generated either by AI or bad actors willfully misusing it. Uninformed
communities fall prey to such fake content and forward it, causing real-
world damage.
Young people have access to social settings like school and the
workplace where they might be exposed to AI tools, but senior citizens are
often isolated. It is essential to engage every segment of the community in
AI education, by holding free classes and seminars at public institutions like
schools, libraries and community centers. AI can be a valuable tool when
used properly.
Technology companies such as Google are focused on making AI
accessible to all communities in the United States of America by setting up
non-profit entities e.g., Google's AI Opportunity Fund. (cid:0)
Community organizations such as libraries also provide the public with
information and learning sessions to understand AI. For example, in New
York City the organization Data and Society has collaborated with the NYC
Public Library to present a series Understanding AI to the public (cid:0). It is our
collective responsibility to make sure that AI will benefit the communities
we live in.
Data Debate 9.5.2 Can you think of three ways to help your local
community learn about and engage with AI?
9.5.6 AI is not always right
AI is famously prone to making up facts, euphemistically called
hallucinations, which we have discussed in earlier chapters. It has no notion
of real-world truth, only the data it is trained on. In their hurry to be at the
forefront of AI innovation, companies have often rushed to create and adopt
AI tools.
Aaron Drapkin's article on AI disasters recounts some well-known errors
(cid:0) that occurred during 2023-2025. AI created reading lists with fake books
that fooled well-known newspapers, sent rude messages to its creators at
Anthropic claiming it had a physical body and wore a blue blazer and red
tie, and cited fictional precedents in legal briefs. Such problems could occur
when training data includes satire, such as articles from The Onion.
Sometimes, AI tools are deliberately subverted by users as was the case
with Microsoft's Tay. In other instances, malevolent parties deliberately
spread lies and misinformation that are amplified by AI bots that crawl the
web and generate news summaries.
Given the power of AI, it is our responsibility to understand and educate
ourselves and our community, to use its power for good, and to spot errors,
bias and prejudice. The next and concluding chapter of this book, Chapter
10, provides guidance on such matters.

9.8 Summative Projects
1. Art and AI: Research, investigate and explore how AI is impacting
various art mediums- such as the music industry, movies, fine art, drama
and theater.
(a) Choose any one art medium you are interested in (such as movies,
music…) and find several articles from reputable sources to read or
listen to podcasts and understand what impact AI is having on
artists and art in that medium. Write a summary of the influence of
AI on this medium.
(b) Interview an artist or a person involved in creating or curating art in
the medium you have chosen. Ask them questions about how they
are incorporating AI into their art/music/etc. Make a list of at least
5 questions you will use for your interview and note down their
responses, or you can make a recording of the interview.
2. Community and AI: Artificial intelligence is transforming all facets of
everyday life. Whether you are aware or not, it is impacting all our lives
and is being used by medical professionals, businesses, scientists, and
the government to make all kinds of decisions, and policy. AI is a
thinking system and has the potential to help and make life better for
humans, but at the same time it can also be exploited to create scams,
take advantage of people and spread false information and perpetuate
biases. The most vulnerable groups in our society like seniors, or minors
have to be educated on how to take precautions, and learn to effectively
use AI.
(a) Investigate if your local community organizations such as library or
senior center are providing classes or information sessions on how
to use AI for local community members. Interview the instructor/
or attend a session to find out how they are helping the public to
understand and make best use of AI. Write a one-page report.
(b) Interview members of your family such as your grandparents,
parents or your teenage cousin to find out what they think of AI and
how they use it. Write a one-page report.
(c) Find out if schools in your community provide AI literacy to
students in grades K-12. How are they doing this?
(d) What are some AI bots and chatbots that are available for free to
use? What are the specific functions or tasks that they can perform?
(e) Explain how AI is used for scams and identity thefts, as well as to
spread false information and perpetuate biases.
(f) AI systems sometimes hallucinate, that is, the output they generate
is false and incorrect, and it is presented as factual information.
How can the general public protect themselves from such
hallucinations or misinformation?

10
Responsible Use of Data and AI
Data is the foundation of AI and the many AI-powered tools that
permeate our lives. Such data has far-reaching consequences, beyond
cool apps and sci-fi movies. When used in the context of medical care
and judicial decisions, AI could have life-changing consequences.
Therefore, it is imperative that data that powers AI applications be
created and used responsibly and ethically. In order to accomplish this
goal, it is important to understand ways in which data can adversely
affect AI applications, and how to detect and remediate improper and
unethical uses of data and AI.
FIGURE 10.1: Left: Personal data is stolen and used without consent,
violating individuals' data privacy. (Image: Wikimedia Commons.) Right:
Deliberate spread of fake news and misinformation could have real-life
consequences and harm society. (Image: Nijwam Swargiar.)
AI elicits strong responses. Some fear it might be so powerful that it
might turn the world into a dystopian wasteland straight out of movies such
as The Matrix or the Terminator series. Others cite its flaws, e.g.,
hallucinations and error-ridden documents and images, full of typos and
inconsistencies, discounting AI as a hyped-up fad of the moment. Two
pioneering AI experts, Yann LeCun and Yoshua Bengio, winners of the
prestigious Turing Award, have had public debates, where LeCun does not
see AI as an existential threat, while Bengio likens it to nuclear weapons.
Their back-and-forth on this subject is detailed in the YouTube video Munk
Debate on Artificial Intelligence — Bengio & Tegmark vs. Mitchell &
LeCun and the article Artificial intelligence could lead to extinction, experts
warn in Section 10.8. The truth, as always, is somewhere in between. AI is
indeed a powerful technology, and like any such revolutionary technology,
brings large-scale upheaval to society in terms of new ways of living and
working. Therefore, it is important for us to learn how to harness the data,
science and technology behind AI to speed up progress for the betterment of
society, just as the advent of vaccines and antibiotics, and of electricity, cars
and computers did.
Given the potential of AI, bias and ethics have come increasingly into
focus with the widespread use of AI algorithms for making life-impacting
decisions such as approving mortgage applications, recruiting for high-
paying jobs, and even criminal convictions. History is replete with
examples of bias and prejudice, and these are embedded in documents and
data that are used to train AI. All of us have biases, conscious or
unconscious. In addition, when data is collected or selected for use, there
might be no consideration for the consequences, or, in the case of biased
parties, it might be collected to reinforce a point of view. The book The
Ethical Algorithm: The Science of Socially Aware Algorithm Design by
Kearns and Roth [26] focuses on the intentional design of technology to
serve the betterment of humans and society. In order to understand this, let
us consider the following simple examples.
Stereotypes (bias): What's the image that pops into your mind when you
think of “skateboarder”? What about “housekeeper”? Studies have
shown that in image databases, there is a strong gender bias such as
“cooking” being associated with women and “skateboarding” being
associated with men. Search engines and other social media tend to
amplify the lopsided distribution of data and reinforce such biases.
Cherry-picking (selective) data: Corporations cherry-pick data from
experimental studies to present their products in the best possible light,
e.g., tobacco companies, pharmaceuticals, and even toy makers.
Governments, policy groups and other lobbyists take similar approaches
to controversial subjects in order to advance their agenda. Many social
media platforms selectively show users stories that will appeal to their
point of view. This has had an adverse effect on societies by polarizing
communities and eliminating common ground, but it increases the
engagement of users and ad revenues for social media companies.
Misinformation: Information spreads with great speed on the internet,
and gets amplified and entrenched by AI algorithms that “upweight” it
based on “likes”. In addition, software platforms have made it easy to
forward such items en masse before they can be verified or contradicted.
Such features make it easy to game AI algorithms for both legitimate as
well as malicious reasons, to have an outsized impact on the real-world.
Figure 10.2, right, shows an AI-generated fake image of Pope Francis in
a white puffer jacket. Such fake videos or photos of celebrities endorsing
products, or in compromising situations have become all too common.
Data privacy: Suppose your roommate saw your laptop open and
looked through your course grades? Or, hacked your phone and read
your private text messages? Or checked your medical records? Many
companies do not just snoop and collect data, they also sell it to other
vendors. The Federal Trade Commission (FTC), a US government
agency set up to protect consumer rights, has issued a report detailing the
lax privacy controls that put children and teenagers at risk, of
surveillance and online abuse. The graphic in Figure 10.2 provides a
high-level overview of how this process works.
Data security: The lack of proper protection and security measures for
data repositories that contain private citizens' data have led to major
security breaches. From schools to hospitals to banks to the government
of the United States, data breaches have exposed sensitive private
information such as social security numbers, bank account details, and
health records, resulting in identity theft and fraud. The US Department
of Health and Human Services maintains a list of data breaches of
hospitals and other healthcare-related institutions at its portal. (cid:0)
FIGURE 10.2: Left: FTC staff report finds data privacy and data security
issues on streaming and social media platforms, issues that could put
children and teenagers at risk. Right: AI-generated fake image of Pope
Francis in a puffer jacket. Image credit: Midjourney, Public domain, via
Wikimedia Commons. ⏎
In the following sections, we will discuss different types of bias and
ethical issues around data, its use in AI applications, and potential remedies.
AI is in a stage of rapid development where governments and regulators are
trying to keep up and provide guidance and guard-rails. The technology has
tremendous promise and power, and it is important to ensure that it is used
in good faith.
10.1 Types of bias
Bias in AI can be deliberate, or unconscious. It can arise from lopsided data
as well as the working of the algorithms. Bias can creep in at any stage in
the AI process: data collection, storage and retrieval, model building,
validation and deployment, and feedback mechanisms. Regardless of intent,
bias can have serious consequences. The PBS video Crash course in AI:
Algorithmic bias and fairness (cid:0) provides an excellent overview of
algorithmic bias in AI. We will explore some ways in which data can
introduce bias into AI models and applications.
10.1.1 Bias in training data
What if the data on which AI is trained is not representative? The reasons
might not be necessarily nefarious, sometimes it could just be a question of
what data was available or could be collected within the time and resource
constraints. For example, AI algorithms in the field of art were historically
trained on portraits of aristocrats and nobility since only they could afford
to have artists paint their personal portraits. An algorithm trained on such
data might not be able to detect features and attire associated with other
strata of society, and it either fails to classify them, or classifies them as
non-human. Or, the data might have been collected in a different context
but reused since it is conveniently and cheaply available. Or, an AI
algorithm trained on historical data might reflect outdated practices, e.g.,
when women were underrepresented in computer science, leading to
resumè databases dominated by men's resumès. Since then, women have
increasingly entered and thrived in this discipline, but algorithms trained on
past data might prioritize male resumès since they have a higher
representation and serve as proxies for successful hires, while ignoring
resumès that had female-sounding names or activities and hobbies. Here are
specific examples of bias in training data:
Bias in historical data used for recidivism screening: Pro Publica, an
investigative journal, presented a study that showed that machine
learning algorithms used for screening for repeat-offenders had racial
bias. The scores it provided for the risk of recidivism did not match up
with the real-world data collected by government agencies and others. It
is possible that the judgments and criminal records used to train the
algorithm were outdated, and no longer reflected the criminality of
neighborhoods. Such bias has serious consequences in terms of parole
applications which take into account the likelihood of repeating criminal
acts once released from prison. See Section 10.8 for a series of articles
published by Pro Publica in 2016 that made the criminal justice system
examine the risk scoring methods used by third-party contractors.
Poor representation in facial recognition training data: The National
Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), a US government agency,
found that many facial recognition software tools had difficulty
recognizing African American and Asian faces. This was due to a lower
representation of such images in the training data which was
predominantly Caucasian. Such failure could result in disproportionate
scrutiny at airports and other types of screening. Section 10.8 has a link
to the NIST story.
Unintentional bias in crowd-sourced data: A popular augmented-reality
gaming app, Pokemon Go, required players to capture game elements in
physical geographical locations. However, the locations showed a bias;
although, the origin of the bias was innocuous. The creators of Pokemon
Go used crowd-sourced geographical data, specifically from players of
the game Ingress, who were predominantly young, male and lived in
affluent neighborhoods. The choice of data collection was mostly
convenience. However, there was an unnoticed correlation between
affluence and race in the neighborhoods where Ingress players lived.
A research study by Colley et al. of Pokemon Go placement of game
elements showed a strong association between prevalence of game
elements and non-hispanic white neighborhoods. This geographical
database of locations was copied by other game creators, unintentionally
perpetuating the bias and depriving less wealthy and more diverse
neighborhoods of access to game elements, and the resulting foot traffic
to boost to local businesses. A detailed study on Pokemon Go is available
in Section 10.8.
Data Debate 10.1.1 What other examples of bias could creep into AI
applications? Can bias in data collection be beneficial? When and how?
10.1.2 Bias in algorithms due to feedback
Algorithms learn from data in the form of responses called feedback,
discussed in Chapter 2. If a particular recommendation yields results, the
attribute pattern corresponding to that recommendation gets boosted. No
wonder you end up with a deluge of baby videos if you happen to “like”
just one video of your cute baby cousin on a social media site. Such
affirmative feedback can reinforce such patterns in the algorithm and cause
it to become increasingly biased in favor of those patterns.
Algorithms that interact with users for feedback can become corrupted
quite quickly. For example, Grok, an AI chatbot, was soon manipulated by
its users to such an extent that it started posting racist and sexist hate speech
in a rant filled with lies and misinformation. Details are available in Section
10.8. Since then, it has been cleaned up and is now embedded in the federal
government's toolkit. There are several such instances. We have already
discussed Tay, Microsoft's Twitter bot that was decommissioned after it was
derailed in a similar manner by users. In another instance, a New York Times
reporter, Kevin Roose, was unsettled by OpenAI's chatbot which, during a
two-hour conversation with Roose, went on to declare that it had fallen in
love with Roose and suggested that Roose leave his wife. Read the entire
article on Roose's conversation with Bing's chatbot in Section 10.8. It is
possible that the training data included celebrity gossip and romance novels
where such incidents and storylines are common.
10.1.3 Bias due to measurement proxies
Bias can enter AI applications at any stage in the AI process. One of the
earliest stages is when the decision to collect data is made. Sometimes, it is
not possible to measure or collect specific types of data since they are too
abstract or difficult to quantify, as discussed in the video Crash course in
AI: Algorithmic bias and fairness. (cid:0)
As an example, suppose you want to measure how delicious a dish is.
You can measure nutritional details, e.g., the amount of carbohydrates, salt
and calories. Its popularity can be measured by how frequently it is bought.
But “deliciousness” is intangible and highly subjective. This disparity
between what we want to measure and what we can measure could lead to
bias, e.g., convenient fast foods that are popular but not necessarily
delicious.
Use of proxy measurements to train AI algorithms can have negative
consequences. Here are some specific examples:
Essays in standardized tests in some states are graded by AI software.
Since creativity or originality cannot be quantified as a metric, they use
proxies like “sentence length, vocabulary, spelling, and subject-verb
agreement—the parts of writing that English language learners and other
groups are more likely to do differently,” as discussed in the article
Flawed Algorithms Are Grading Millions of Students' Essays in Section
10.8. This results in nonsensical word-salad essays that get high scores,
whereas essays with original ideas expressed in a different way, e.g.,
shorter sentences, such as those favored by famous writer Raymond
Carver, are penalized.
Many schools are evaluated by the test scores of the students. Under
such a high-pressure situation where schools' and teachers' performance
is tied to test scores used as proxies for good teaching and learning, the
incentive for optimizing the proxy metrics can be high. The focus shifts
from students, and teaching becomes aimed at gaming the test scores and
even cheating, see Under Pressure, Teachers Tamper With Tests in
Section 10.8. This is exactly what happened in a Texas school district in
2010, where the careers of principals and a monetary bonus for fifth-
grade teachers were tied to the performance of students on a
standardized test.
An article, Reliance on metrics is a fundamental challenge for AI, in
Section 10.8 by National Institutes of Health (NIH), a US government
agency, mentions that metrics not only are inadequate for measuring the
real-world aspect, but can be gamed and incentivized to be measured in
highly addictive environments. For example, using the number of clicks
or time spent viewing a website as a measure of user satisfaction can
lead to unsavory practices that get the users addicted e.g., automatically
popping up the next video clip as soon as a viewer finishes watching
one, keeping the user glued to the screen. However, in order to do this,
the content gets increasingly intense as the level of stimulation needed
for the user to stay engaged increases, just like any other addictive
substance. This aspect of human psychology is skillfully employed by
social media platforms.
Algorithms are increasingly being used to make decisions that
profoundly impact our lives. It is our responsibility to stay informed and
constantly question how such technology will affect us as individuals, our
communities, and the society we live in.
GeminiBox 10.1.1: Bias in AI
“Provide a brief summary of bias and fairness in AI.”
10.2 Misinformation and Fakery
One of the negative impacts of AI has been the explosion of misinformation
and fake images and videos. The latter are so realistic that even real videos
have been subject to doubt. Figure 10.3, left, demonstrates how Stephen
Wolfram, creator of Mathematica, can be made to look like Isaac Newton.
Such deepfakes can be generated using neural network techniques such as
face swapping, as mentioned in A Few Thoughts about Deep Fakes (cid:0). AI is
being used to defame and blackmail people. Countries are using them to
fight proxy wars, to meddle in elections and to propagate misinformation
that can divide and destroy countries. Here are some specific examples:
On March 18, 2022, Russia used fake videos of the Ukrainian President
Zelensky urging his troops to surrender, but was foiled when Zelensky
countered with a live feed cautioning his troops and the public to be
aware of fake news and videos. The article Weaponized AI: The New
Frontline in Global Disinformation Warfare in Section 10.8 has more
details.
Ahead of its 2024 elections, India, the largest democracy in the world,
set up a Deepfake Analysis Unit as part of its Misinformation Combat
Alliance. The public could submit videos through a WhatsApp hotline to
verify the authenticity of a video. Their analysis found that: “Packaging
misinformation using well-known faces may increase the likelihood of
people falling for it, such as featuring people in business in videos
promoting financial scams.” The government focused on increasing
public awareness by giving helpful tips as to how to detect deepfakes.
You can read more about this very interesting effort to fight deepfakes in
Year of elections: Lessons from India's fight against AI-generated
misinformation, in Section 10.8.
Governments and institutions have stepped up efforts to contain the
damage, particularly to individuals and celebrities such as Taylor Swift
who was victim of an obnoxious and unconscionable deepfake video
X
posted on the social media platform . By the time the platform
removed it, the damage had been done. After a huge public outcry, the
Take It Down Law was passed by the US Congress in 2025 and signed
into law by President Donald J. Trump. The congressional webpage Take
it Down Act signed into law by President Donald J Trump on May 19,
2025 in Section 10.8 has details.
FIGURE 10.3: Left: Creation of a deepfake with neural nets (Stephen
Wolfram). Right: Congress passed the Take It Down Act in 2025 to fight
harmful deepfakes. (Photo: Ted Eytan.) ⏎
The articles 5 Telltale Signs That a Photo Is AI-generated from The Kellogg
School of Management, and How to detect Deep Fakes, from MIT Labs, in
Section 10.8 describe how AI fakes can be screened for inconsistencies
such as anatomical anomalies (many fingers), violation of laws of physics,
or a weird sheen to the skin. For example, in Figure 10.2, right, the chain
holding the holy cross worn by Pope Francis seems to be missing a half.
Make sure to explore the prompt in GeminiBox 10.2.1 to figure out how
you can help fight misinformation and deepfakes.
GeminiBox 10.2.1: Fighting deepfakes and misinformation ⏎
“What is being done to fight deepfakes and misinformation?” (cid:0)
10.3 Data Privacy and AI
Every time you make a purchase on Amazon, or browse through your social
X
media accounts (Instagram, , WhatsApp), your personal data is being
siphoned off by companies and stored in data centers for AI applications.
These digital traces are rich in personal information: entertainment
preferences, political affiliations, friends and family, even one's alma mater.
Many companies do not just collect data, they also sell it to third parties. It
is not illegal, but certainly seems unethical, to collect and sell personal
information without an individual's consent or even knowledge. Figure
10.2, left, shows the many ways such information is collected.
Such personal information is never quite safe from hackers; it is bought
and sold on the dark web for identity theft, for committing fraud and even
blackmail. Such data breaches have become common including at banks,
hospitals, universities, and healthcare companies. The American
government maintains a list of data breaches of “unsecured protected health
information affecting 500 or more individuals”, which are under
investigation by the US Department of Health and Human Services, Office
of Civil Rights.
Private data that could potentially harm an individual if made public is
often protected by governments. For example, social security numbers,
bank account information, academic grades and health data are all
considered sensitive and private information. Many countries and some
states in America have strict laws about types of data that need to be
protected by law, and secured by the companies that collect and store them.
Section 10.5 has details about regulatory measures to help keep private
information secure.
In addition, companies are not always transparent about when and what
type of data they collect, and how they use it. Even if they are, they usually
bury it in long, wordy legal documents that are hard to read and
comprehend, crammed in pop-up boxes that require consumers to hit the “I
agree” button in order to proceed. Most consumers simply skip reading
such declarations, as shown in Figure 10.4, left.
Extended Description
FIGURE 10.4: How Americans feel about companies using their personal
information for AI systems. Retrieved from Pew Research Center. ⏎
The Pew Research Center has conducted an extensive survey on how
Americans feel about data privacy. Many have experienced identity theft
and fraud. Explore some of the results of the survey illustrated in Figure
10.4 and discuss in class.
Recent AI applications of genAI have been accused of using creative
works such as newspaper articles, essays, short stories and novels without
permission. This is a clear violation of copyright laws. The New York Times
has sued OpenAI and Microsoft for training genAI applications on their
articles without permission. Read the article The Times Sues OpenAI and
Microsoft Over A.I. Use of Copyrighted Work in Section 10.8 to learn more.
Try the prompt in GeminiBox 10.3.1 to understand what governments
around the world are doing to protect citizens' private data.
GeminiBox 10.3.1: Privacy and AI ⏎
“What are governments around the world doing to ensure privacy of
data used by AI?” (cid:0)
10.4 Ethics in AI
Ethical practices have always been the focus of debate, long before data and
AI, particularly in the field of medicine where it can mean exercising
painful choices between life and death. For example, a new and important
medicine, e.g., for curing a deadly disease, is to be tested by a
pharmaceutical company. The company decides to hold clinical trials in
poor rural areas of developing countries where the medicine provided by
the trial is the only option available to relieve the symptoms of very sick
patients. Is this an ethical practice? Probably not, since the patients are
unlikely to reveal negative side effects, fearful that they might lose access
to the medication. This results in an under-reporting of harmful side effects
that later come to light when the drug becomes available to the public and
people start using it on a large scale. Several medicines, for example
arthritis pain relieving ones, have been recalled after a significant incidence
of gastrointestinal side effects that did not come to light during clinical
trials.
The development of AI has been so rapid, and its consequences so
significant that governments are finally contemplating regulation. Here are
some examples of unethical practices that might motivate governments to
take stronger action.
Cambridge Analytica, a data analytics and surveillance company, and
Facebook (now Meta) illegally harvested and misused the data of more
than 87 million Facebook users for political purposes. Location data, and
political preferences based on the news feeds of individuals allowed
Cambridge Analytica to psychologically manipulate voters by glorifying
one candidate and publishing damaging, often fake, content about the
opponent. Subsequently, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg was called before
Congress, and in 2025 the company was fined $5 billion for privacy
violations and Cambridge Analytica went bankrupt. A lack of
accountability and transparency resulted in the abuse of private data and
psychological targeting of vulnerable individuals. The story is
documented in the article Facebook-Cambridge Analytica data scandal
in Section 10.8.
Houston Astros and other major league athletic teams have leaned
heavily into AI and data analytics. However, stealing pitch signs and
passing them to hitters to anticipate the type of pitch, as the Astros have
been accused of, is unethical and goes against the very spirit of
competition. Similarly, New England Patriots were accused of filming (a
form of data gathering) opposing teams. Yet another team was accused
of transmitting information via smartwatches to players to give them an
edge. Read Tom Davenport's article on AI and ethics in the sports
industry in Section 10.8.
Crypto scams use AI and data to dupe people into giving money. The
scams impersonate people online, and use AI to mimic voices of
relatives over the phone, pleading for money in an emergency, to be
wired immediately. AI is used to create authentic-looking websites and
platforms that seem to be investment portals but actually funnel money
to hackers. A combination of people and technology has proved to be a
deadly and unethical combination that has scammed millions of people
to part with their hard-earned life savings. The United States government
has just announced a strike force to combat such crypto scammers, New
Scam Center Strike Force Battles Southeast Asian Crypto Investment
Fraud Targeting Americans, featured in Section 10.8.
There are numerous examples of unethical uses of AI. Using AI to cheat on
exams and projects in school, to create artificial identities to scam money as
in “catfishing” or “pig butchering”, to use the creative works of others and
copyrighted material to train AI, and to discriminate against specific groups
through biased AI algorithms, e.g., in hiring, policing or lending practices.
The next section provides a brief overview of efforts to combat abuse of AI.
10.5 What Is Being Done
AI is in a rapid development stage where its consequences are yet to play
out. Regulating such a powerful and complex tool is challenging because
not many people, particularly politicians and policymakers, understand the
inner workings of AI. In addition, there is a concern that regulating such an
explosive technology might throttle its development, putting the United
States at a competitive disadvantage. However, rudimentary attempts at
regulation are just emerging. Here are some specific efforts that are
underway to understand and harness AI and data for the benefit of the
public.
Government regulation: The European Union has passed a stringent data
protection law, General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), that
protects an individual's right to privacy and control over how their
personal data is used.
The United States government and some states have started to create
laws to protect the public from the misuse of AI. The Take It Down Law
mentioned in Section 10.2 is a good example, where harmful personal
information posted without consent is required to be taken down. The
State of California has set up a crypto scam tracker (cid:0) to provide guidance
and help those scammed by fraudsters. California has a data privacy law
California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA) that protects consumer data
from being sold without consent. Other data privacy laws include those
that protect medical Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act
of 1996 (HIPAA), children's (Children's Online Privacy Protection Act
(COPPA), and financial Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (GLBA) information.
Data Debate 10.5.1 Use Gemini to explore each of the laws mentioned
above, learn what the acronyms stand for, and what the laws state.
Policies and compliance: International organizations like the
Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD)
have provided guidance in terms of principles that AI should adhere to,
such as sustainability, equity, transparency, safety and accountability.
The OECD also provides policy recommendations and identifies
countries that adhere to these principles. Corporations too have
implemented AI governance policies and regularly monitor compliance.
Education and outreach: Governments, private institutions and
international organizations have created policies and programs to
educate children, teachers and the workforce about AI, its applications
and ethical use. The general public, especially older adults, is vulnerable
to scams and misinformation.
Community organizations such as local libraries and community centers
provide much needed information sessions to educate this segment of
our society. Read the blog in Section 10.8 about the program offered by
NYC public library.
The now defunct US Department of Education provided guidance for
improving educational outcomes (cid:0) by incorporating AI into K-12
education, training teachers, and offering personalized instruction for
differential needs.
Private technology corporations like Google and Nvidia have provided
funding and access to software tools to promote AI education. UNESCO
(United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization) has
provided comprehensive guidelines (cid:0) for AI education of students and
teachers centered on an ethical and human-centric approach.
These are just a few efforts that are underway. As the use of AI expands,
there will certainly be more initiatives on all three fronts discussed above.
10.6 Our Responsibility as AI Consumers
AI's power can be used responsibly and ethically by exercising caution and
building guard-rails to prevent its abuse. Specifically, as individuals, we can
take the following steps.
1. Transparency: AI applications, e.g., deep learning algorithms, can be
quite opaque. The ProPublica article in Section 10.1.1 mentioned that
the recidivism risk scores assigned to convicts by the algorithm were
never explained, nor was the data on which the algorithm was built
shared. The parole applicants have a right to know why they were turned
down, at the very least. Transparency in the decision making process
would not only address issues of fairness and bias, but make it less
prone to abuse.
Open data initiatives that make data available to the public are an
important part of transparency. Federal government organizations such
as NOAA do an outstanding job of making data accessible.
Another method of increasing transparency and providing visibility into
AI systems is to use logs that are generated by AI systems. Such logs,
also called AI audit logs, are often used to audit them, to identify what
actions were taken, when and by whom.
GeminiBox 10.6.1: AI system logs
“What are AI system logs?” (cid:0)
2. Accountability: Corporations and institutions should be held accountable
for the consequences of their AI applications to incentivize them to
monitor and manage their applications better. The Cambridge Analytica
example mentioned in Section 10.4 is an example of the worst possible
abuse of AI and data that required a congressional hearing to bring it out
into the open. Holding individuals and corporations accountable for the
abuse of AI should not need such escalation.
3. Digital Trust: Trust is an abstract and intangible concept. However, we
are accustomed to measuring trustworthiness of companies through
organizations like the Better Business Bureau, and of products through
publications such as Consumer Reports. Professional organizations, e.g.,
the American Statistical Association, provide guidelines for the ethical
practice of statistics, see Section 10.8. Endorsements from such
organizations foster trust in the public.
With the advent of AI, there is a similar push, all across the world, to
provide ways to measure digital trust in organizations. The World
Economic Forum has provided guidelines captured in Figure 10.5, with
the goals of security, accountability and inclusiveness, and dimensions
to measure the progress towards the goals, shown in Figure 10.5, left.
4. Auditable Data and AI Protocol: Many professions that deal with
complex scenarios, such as medicine and space exploration, have a
protocol checklist that ensures that a process is completed safely and
fully. Such checklists are extremely useful for practitioners. The Royal
Statistical Society of Britain has created a checklist for the safe practice
of data science, described in the article A Guide for Ethical Data Science
(cid:0). The European Union commission has created a checklist ALTAI, or the
Assessment List for Trustworthy Artificial Intelligence, for AI
applications to engender digital trust, shown in Figure 10.5, right. The
checklist consists of principles AI systems should require such as:
Human agency and oversight
Technical robustness and safety
Privacy and data governance
Transparency
Diversity, non-discrimination and fairness
Societal and environmental well-being
Accountability
We have already discussed many of these concepts. The article The
assessment list for trustworthy artificial intelligence: A review and
recommendations in Section 10.8 discusses the strengths and
weaknesses of this list.
5. Be aware of limitations of AI: AI has no concept of “truth”, “ethics” or
“shame.” It has no emotions or feelings, its goal is to perform whatever
task it is prompted to do. If we request a list of ten books, it will provide
a list of ten books, some of which might not exist, as we learned in the
section on AI hallucinations. As consumers of AI, we need to examine
and question the output of AI. In order to perform these sanity checks,
we need to educate ourselves about how AI works.
6. Human in the loop: We have seen that AI systems, as of now, are prone
to errors and can be quite brittle. For example, AI's recommendations
and solutions are not consistent. An MIT and Penn State University
study (cid:0) analyzed Ring doorbell videos. AI models were asked: “Is there a
crime?” and “Should the police be called?”. The results were
inconsistent and were prone to false negatives, indicating that the
models were calibrated to being conservative about declaring a crime.
Similarly, there was inconsistency in recommending police intervention,
which seemed to stem from the demographics of the neighborhood. The
models recommended calling the police more often in minority
neighborhoods for the same type of crime which went unflagged in
other neighborhoods. The models were also biased in the choice of
words to describe people in the videos based on the neighborhood. This
is clearly problematic, since such biases get reinforced with the
increasing use of such data, algorithms and models.
One way to make AI models more robust is through human oversight
called human in the loop (HIL). Humans learn from the time they are
born and have vast reserves of knowledge about the real-world that can
be used to fine-tune AI and help it learn. HIL can be in the form of
simple validation of AI results or more complex interaction through
input at different stages. Medical diagnostics is a classic example where
AI can screen imaging for abnormalities and a human expert can
diagnose it further.
Extended Description
FIGURE 10.5: Left: Goals and dimensions of digital trust as proposed by
the World Economic Forum. Right: A checklist proposed by the European
Union for measuring trustworthiness of AI applications. ⏎
10.7 Conclusion
Like any revolutionary technology, AI engenders fear as well as unrealistic
expectations. Will it take over the world; will it annihilate the human race?
Or, will it create an army of faithful robots that will improve the quality of
human lives through tasks such as elder care, bomb disposal, scouting for
poisonous gases and so on. Will it take away jobs, or will it create a utopia
where humans can pursue their dreams assisted by robots? AI technology is
powerful but not perfect, and at this point in time, flawed in many ways.
AI is prone to hallucinations. In order to provide an answer, AI tends to
invent facts in an authoritative and plausible manner. AI amplifies and
reflects the flaws in its training data. If there is bias in the data it is trained
on, it reinforces such biases. If the data has quality issues, they will affect
the results and conclusions of AI models, in ways that might not be easily
detectable. In the hands of unscrupulous parties, AI can, and has been, used
to cause harm and divisions in society, to pit one group against another, and
to create increasingly addictive harmful content. The privacy rights of
individuals have been violated by harmful deepfakes. AI has also been used
to perpetrate fraud in the form of identity theft, catfishing, and crypto
scams.
However, AI and data have the potential to uplift society by expediting
the progress of new medical technology like prosthetics and health
monitors, by helping doctors refine diagnoses, by speeding up discovery of
new life-saving medicines. It can improve infrastructure, find new ways of
growing food, and help millions rise out of poverty.
The first step towards embracing this technology is education. This
textbook provides a rigorous introduction to the statistics and data behind
AI, and it serves as a stepping stone to more advanced understanding of AI
and data.
