10.9 Exercises
1. Write an example where data collected about you could help, and where
it could harm.
2. Give three examples of bias while collecting data. Are they deliberate or
unintentional? What are the consequences? How can one determine if a
given data set is biased?
3. Find one example of a country, and a state in the United States that have
consumer data protection laws. What do these laws state?
4. Why is data privacy important? What are different ways in which your
personal data can be stolen? What are possible consequences?
5. What steps can technology companies that are developing AI take to
safeguard against biases in their AI models? Choose a tech company and
research how they are addressing this issue.
6. Read the articles: How to detect Deep Fakes, from MIT labs and 5
telltale signs that an image is an AI fake in Section 10.8. Do you think
these are helpful to detect a fake image or video generated by AI? What
were the three ways to detect fake images that you felt were the best?
10.10 Summative Project
For this research project, find the latest information or news from articles,
videos or reports about the following and write a summary. Verify that these
articles are from trustworthy sources.
1. Find a news article or report on the ethical use of AI. This can be either a
report on misuse of AI or of biases of AI. Who was impacted by this?
Write a summary.
2. Find a news article or report that talks about new regulations or
safeguards that are being put in place to help prevent scams or fraud
using AI. Do you think this is an effective law or regulation? Write a
summary.
3. Find a chart or graph that displays information about AI in the context of
Responsible use of Data and write a brief summary.

Extended Description for Figure 1.3
FIGURE 1.3: Statistics, data and AI are interconnected. AI builds on
fundamental concepts from statistics and uses data for training, while
statistical models gather data in the form of feedback loops from AI to
improve performance and advance foundational concepts.
The sequence begins with understanding the problem and deciding what
data to collect, then collecting, storing, accessing, and retrieving data. It
continues with exploring, describing, summarising, and visualising data,
then statistics, machine learning, and other A I. Accuracy links to data
quality, while responsible use links to bias, fairness, and ethics. A sanity
check examines results for hallucinations and errors. An adjacent A I list
includes symbolic A I, machine learning, neural nets, deep learning,
robotics, web search, Gen A I, agentic A I, and A G I.

Extended Description for Figure 6.2
FIGURE 6.2: Are puppies favored for adoption over other animals at
animal rescue centers? The process of statistical hypothesis testing is
designed to answer such questions in an objective, data-driven manner.
A question about puppy adoption leads to null and alternate hypotheses,
then to sample data collection, test statistic computation, test and error
tolerance selection, and a Z-score test for proportions with alpha at zero
point zero five. The decision step checks whether the absolute Z-score is
less than one point nine six. Yes leads to no significant difference in
adoption rates. No leads to a difference in adoption rates.
⏎

boundaries, and critical regions at both extremes. The second plot gives a
one-sided greater-than test, with one minus alpha in the typical region,
alpha in the right tail, one critical value, and the critical region beyond it.

Extended Description for Figure 6.10
FIGURE 6.10: Type 1 error or false positive: Reject the null hypothesis due
to an abnormal sample value from the null distribution. Type 2 error or false
negative: Mistakenly accept the null hypothesis (i.e., reject the alternate
hypothesis) due an abnormal sample from the alternate distribution.
The vertical axis is density, and the horizontal axis ranges from negative
four to six. One curve peaks near one, and the other peaks near three. The
decision boundary is marked at about two. Type two is marked on the lower
central area before the boundary, and type one is marked on the lower area
after the boundary. Paw and puppy icons appear below the plot.

P (X) P (X)
between well-separated null ( N ) and alternate hypotheses ( A )
(e.g., distribution of weights of horses and elephants) but low power when
the two distributions have a high overlap (e.g., distribution of weights of
horses and zebras).
The first plot compares well-separated P of X and P A of X weight
distributions for horses and elephants, with a critical value C alpha between
them. Type two equals beta is small, and type one equals alpha is marked
near the boundary. The second plot compares highly overlapping P of X and
P A of X weight distributions for horses and zebras, with a larger type two
equals beta region and a type one equals alpha region beyond C alpha.
